for task in tasks walks a copy (3.10a). The copy is freed with zeno_list_drop, never with the function that frees items.

Why. The copy holds the same items as the real list, which still owns them. Freeing them through the copy would free them twice. Turned down: walk the real list when the items have one owner. An append in the body may move the list's storage, which is what 3.10a is for. Skipping the copy when the body cannot append is an exercise in docs/one-owner.md.

5.15h The except owns the error it caught, and the checker says so

5.9d's rule moved into the checker for this build: e in except Bad as e is a look at the error. It can be read and lent to a borrow parameter, which is new. Keeping it is refused in words about errors, not about borrows.

Why. It is the same rule as a loop's item: something else owns the value and frees it when the block ends. One mechanism now covers both.

5.15i The C backend assigns through an index, value first

points[1].x = 30 and grid[1][0] = 5 were refused by zeno build and accepted by zeno run and the LLVM backend. They compile now. The value is worked out first, then the list or object, then the index.

Why now. Lists of objects make these everyday lines. Why that order. It is the interpreter's, and four engines must agree. The test uses functions that print when they are called, so a change of order fails it.

5.15j What a no-collector build still refuses (was provisional: 11.2)

This replaces the list in 5.9g. (Step 4 took the first row off it. The list as it stands is in 5.16i.)

  • A list or object inside an or none, an enum case with values, a list of int or none. (Step 4.)
  • A function written where a value goes, a function type that mentions a list or an object, an error that is kept or raised again. (Step 5.)
  • A list or object as what a type parameter stands for. The copy made for T would have to be checked as a value that moves.
  • A function that uses a list or object declared at the top of the file (5.8d rule 4). A table of constants that several functions read is the commonest shape of firmware data, so this one matters. A top-level let that the program never gives away could be lent to every function without being passed. (For the audit.)
  • A method that takes out what its object owns (5.15e).

A note for 5.9q's known gap. It is wider than was written there: one print that shows two lists is enough for the C and LLVM builds to report text-room peaks a byte or more apart. What is printed is the same. The fix described there still stands, and the test programs still avoid the mix.

5.16 With no collector, an enum value that carries something has one owner

Wait(250) and Blink([2, 3, 5]) are kept in a block from the pool, as an object is, and whoever receives one owns it and frees it. The walkthrough is docs/enums-and-none.md.

Why. It is 5.15 again, for the last kind of value that was made and never freed. One rule for lists, objects and enum values means there is nothing new to learn: a programmer who has read docs/one-owner.md already knows what let b = a does to a command. (Principle: unambiguous and intuitive. One rule, not one per kind of value.)

Turned down: keep the value in place, with no block, like a C tagged union passed by value. It would need no pool at all for a Wait. But a case may carry a list, which has one owner, so a copy of the value would give that list two. Copying would then have to be refused for some enums and not others, depending on what the cases carry. List slots are 8 bytes, so an enum bigger than that could not sit in a list without a second way of storing it. And the two backends would each need a second representation of every enum. A block of the case's own size (5.16d) costs a Wait 16 bytes of pool and keeps one representation everywhere.

5.16a A case that carries nothing is a fixed value, and a plain enum is copied

There is one Stop in a program, placed before it starts. Making it takes no memory and freeing it does nothing. An enum in which no case carries anything (a Mode that is Idle, Busy or Halted) is not owned at all: its values are copied like numbers in every build.

Why. A state or a mode is the commonest enum in firmware, and making every Idle cost a block, or making let b = a take a away for a value with nothing in it, would be a surprise with no safety bought. Why the type decides and not the case. A variable of type Command has one owner even at a moment when it holds Stop. Which case a value is at some line is not known when the program is checked, and a rule that depended on it could not be read off the page.

5.16b What may be none can have one owner, in every build

Command or none, Packet or none and list[int] or none are owned when the thing inside is. none owns nothing. This holds in a build with a collector too, when the words are written (owned list[int] or none, a Packet or none of an owned class).

Why now. The old refusal (docs/ownership.md said "taking one back out would have to leave a hole") mixed up two things. A variable that may be none is an ordinary owner: it is given away whole, and the checker already knows a given-away variable holds nothing. Only a field has the hole problem, and a field is already protected by the rule that nothing moves out of an object. So nothing new was needed, only the refusal taken away.

How it is written down in the checker. or none is always the outermost part of the type: an owner is "maybe (owned X)" and a look is "maybe (look at X)". So borrow Packet or none reads as it is written, a look at a packet or at nothing, and is not none narrows both the same way. Turned down: an owned "maybe X". Narrowing would then have to rebuild the type from the inside, and a look at it would be a third shape.

Still refused inside a function type and the [ ] of a generic, with every other value that has one owner (5.15j).

5.16c match looks, and a name in a pattern is a copy or a look

match never takes anything out of the value it is given. In case Blink(pins) the name is a look at the list. In case Wait(ms) and case Say(text) the number and the text are copied. case other is a look at the whole value.

Why. Reading a command must not use it up: cost(command) and then run(command) is the ordinary shape. Rust lets a match move parts out, and then has "partially moved" values that can be used in some ways and not others. That is hard to hold in the head, and the unambiguity rule is against it. If taking a part out is ever wanted it will be a word of its own, not a second meaning of match.

Why text is copied. Text is copied whenever it is kept (5.9l), so this is the existing rule and not a new one. The copy is freed when the case ends.

5.16d While a case holds a look, the owner cannot change

If a pattern gave a name to a part that is looked at, then for the length of that case the owner of the matched value cannot be given away, be given a new value, or have items taken out. A case with no such name is free to do all three.

Why. The look points into the owner's block. state = Stop would free that block under it. This is the loop rule of 5.15e, for the same reason, and the checker keeps both in one list (owned.walked, with Looking::Loop or Looking::Case), so the messages differ and the mechanism does not.

Why only when there is a look. A state machine is match state { case Waiting(ms) { state = Running } ... }. ms is a number, copied out, and nothing points into the old state. Refusing that would make the most natural use of an enum impossible. The message for the refused case says what to do: write _ for a name that is not needed.

5.16e borrow can be written on an enum that carries values, in every build

function cost(command: borrow Command). With a collector it changes nothing about memory.

Why. The same file has to build with and without a collector (5.15a). If borrow on an enum were an error with a collector, a program written for --no-collector would not run under zeno run. On an enum that carries nothing it is still an error, with a message that says to pass the value as it is: there is nothing to lend.

5.16f In C, each case takes its own size from the pool

zeno_new_Command_Wait asks for offsetof(z_Command, as) + sizeof(as.c1), not sizeof(z_Command). This is in every C build.

Why. The LLVM backend has always made a struct for each case (5.11h), and the two backends must give the same memory report (5.11t). And on a small pool it matters: one large case would otherwise make every small one pay for it. The cost. A value can never change its case in place, since its block may be too small for another. Zeno has no way to write that, and "a new value takes the place of the old one" is the rule everywhere else, so nothing is lost.

5.16g Found on the way: a case pattern on a value that may be none

case Stop and case Wait(ms) on a Command or none were compiled wrongly by both backends, with or without a collector: the first as a new name that matches anything, the second refused. The interpreter and the VM were right. Both backends now test "is it there" and then the tag.

Why it is written down. Four engines must agree, and this one slipped through because no test matched an enum case against an optional subject. a_case_of_an_enum_matches_a_value_that_may_be_none does now.

5.16h list[int or none] stays refused with no collector (was provisional: 11.2)

A number that may be none is kept in a small block of its own when it sits in a list (5.11i). Giving those blocks an owner would mean a deep copy in every place a list is copied, as text needed (5.9x).

Why not now. There is a way to write it today that costs less and says more: an enum Reading with a Missing case and a Value(n: int) case, and a list[Reading]. Missing takes no memory and the list owns each Value. The compiler's message says so. The right fix is to keep the pair in the slot without a block, which touches the slot's size, and is for later. (Keep whatever works.)

5.16i What a no-collector build still refuses (was provisional: 11.2)

This replaces the list in 5.15j. (Replaced in turn by 5.17g: the step 5 items below build now.)

  • A function written where a value goes, a function type that mentions a list or an object, an error that is kept or raised again. (Step 5.)
  • A value that has one owner inside a function type or as what a type parameter stands for.
  • list[int or none] (5.16h).

For the audit that follows these steps, all of them about what is intuitive rather than what is safe:

  • A function cannot use a list or object declared at the top of the file (5.15j). A top-level let that is never given away could be lent to every function without being passed.
  • A method cannot take out what its object owns, because self is a borrow (5.15e). machine.waiting.pop() has to be written outside the class today.
  • A plain parameter takes and borrow looks (5.15b). Most functions only look, so most parameters carry the word. examples/tour.zeno and examples/shapes.zeno do not build with no collector for want of it. Whether looking should be the plain form is a question about the direction of the language, and is the owner's to answer. (Answered: 5.17h.)
  • After case none, a later case name still has the type T or none. The checker could narrow it.

5.17 With no collector, an error has one owner: raise takes it and except owns it

An error used to be the one value that lived by a special rule: its except freed it, so it could not be kept, handed on or raised again, and it could only be made by raise. Now it is a value like any other. raise x takes x. The except that catches it receives it, and the name after as owns it. At the end of the block the name frees it, unless it was kept, returned, given away or raised again.

Why. Every other rule in this build is "whoever receives a value owns it". One exception to that is one more thing to remember, and the programs it refused are ordinary firmware: log the last fault, keep a list of them, pass a fault up after noting it. With lists and objects owned (5.15) nothing stood in the way any more.

Turned down: keep the old rule and add e.copy(). A copy of an object that holds a list is a deep copy, which costs pool and time the reader cannot see from the word.

Unambiguous and intuitive. There is one rule for every value now. Nothing new is written: raise fault, faults.append(fault) and last = fault mean what they mean for any owner, and the messages for "given away" and "only borrowed" are the ones already known.

5.17a An error of a kind Zeno knows has one owner too, and can be made anywhere

ValueError("x") makes an error whether or not raise stands in front of it. let ready = ValueError("made early") is allowed, and so is a parameter of type ValueError, a list[ValueError], and borrow ValueError.

Why. "Only raise can make one" existed because nothing else could free it. With an owner, that reason is gone, and an error made ahead of time is useful (one error for every caller of a table of checks).

In the checker such an error is an owned Error(kind). An error that is an object of a class needed nothing: with no collector an object already has one owner.

5.17b With no collector, a function value has one owner, and a function of the program is a fixed value

A function written where a value goes (function(n: int) -> int { ... }) is kept in a small block with what it uses. That block has one owner, like a list. A function of the program, used by name (double), is not in a block: it is one fixed value that is never freed, like an enum case that carries nothing (5.16a). The type says which rules apply:

  • var f: function(int) -> int can hold either kind. It is an owner: giving it away empties it, and a new value frees the old one. Freeing a function of the program does nothing.
  • let f = double, with no type written, holds that one function. It is not an owner, and can be used any number of times.

Why the type decides and not the value. The checker must know at every line whether a name still holds something, without running the program. A variable that can hold either kind has to follow the stricter rule.

Turned down: counting who holds each function. That is a small collector, and this build promises none. Turned down: copying the block when it is kept twice. A copy is easy for numbers, but a function that keeps text would copy the text too, and a hidden copy is exactly what this build is for avoiding.

For the audit. var f = double followed by f = other is refused, because without a written type the variable holds that one function. The message says to write the type. Inferring function(int) -> int for a var would be less surprising.

5.17c What a function written where a value goes may use from outside it

It uses What happens Why
a let number, bool or plain enum the value is copied into the block it never changes, so a copy is the same thing
a let text a copy of the text is kept in the block and freed with it text is kept by copy everywhere in this build (5.9l)
a var of the function around it refused the two would share one variable, and with no collector nothing is shared
a var or let at the top of the file used where it is it is the program's, and lives as long as the program
a list, an object, or anything else with one owner it is looked at (5.17d) taking it would silently empty the variable

Why refuse the var. In zeno run the inner function and the outer one see the same variable, so a change by one is seen by the other. To do that, the variable has to live in a block that both hold, which is two owners. Copying it instead would compile, and print something else than zeno run prints. A refusal is the only honest answer. The message says what to write: let if it does not change, an object if it does.

Turned down: a word such as move on the function, as Rust has. It would be a second way to write a function, for a case that has an answer already: give the function a parameter.

5.17d A function that looks at a value belongs to the line that writes it

A function that uses a list or an object from outside looks at it, and a look must be gone before the owner is. So the function is itself a look: the statement that writes it owns it and frees it. It can be passed to map, filter, reduce or a borrow parameter. It cannot be put in a variable, returned or given away, and it cannot take an item out of what it looks at. While that statement runs, the value it uses counts as being looked at, so it cannot be given away on the same line.

Why. This is the common case: readings.map(function(r) { ... }) with something from the surroundings in the body. It is safe with no new rule, because a borrow that lasts one statement is what a borrow parameter already is.

Turned down: let it be kept, and stop the owner from changing until the function is gone. That is a borrow with a lifetime, which Rust needs names and annotations for. One statement is a lifetime nobody has to write.

Was provisional (11.2). A function that uses another function value which has an owner (function(n) { return f(f(n)) } where f is a parameter) looks at it, so it cannot be returned. Writing "compose" needs a way to take a value into a function. To be looked at with real programs.

5.17e A function type says what each parameter does, in every build

function(borrow list[int]) -> int is a function that looks at a list. function(Packet) -> int takes the packet. A function can return a list or an object, and the caller owns it. Each parameter inside a function type is read by the same rule as a parameter of a function, so a call through a function value lends or gives exactly as a call by name does. A function fits such a type only if its parameters do the same thing.

This holds with a collector as well, for owned class and owned list. It replaces the refusal of a value with one owner inside a function type (5.16i).

Why. Before, a function type could not say borrow, so no function value could be handed a list, and map over a list of objects was the only way in. Still refused: a value with one owner as what a type parameter stands for (same[T](x: T) with a list), because the copy for T would have to know that it moves.

5.17f In C and IR: a drop entry in the block, only with no collector

A function value is { code, name } followed by what it uses. With no collector there is a third entry, drop: the function that frees this block. It is zeno_closure_release when the block keeps no text, a function written for that one literal when it does (it frees the text first), and NULL for a function of the program. The owner calls zeno_closure_drop, which does nothing for NULL.

Every literal gets a block of its own, even one that uses nothing. Why. Two values made by the same literal are different functions in zeno run (== says so), and one rule for every literal is simpler than two. It costs one small block of three words.

A build with a collector has no drop entry and is unchanged.

5.17g What a no-collector build still refuses (was provisional: 11.2)

This replaces the list in 5.16i.

  • A function that uses a var of the function around it (5.17c), and keeping a function that looks (5.17d). Both by design.
  • A value that has one owner as what a type parameter stands for.
  • list[int or none] (5.16h).
  • A function that can raise, used as a value (the --firmware rule, since errors travel as values in this build).
  • A method used as a value (let m = counter.bump).

5.17h Decided: a plain parameter will look, and a word will take

Asked of the owner of the language on 2026-11-15, and answered: a plain parameter should look at a list or an object, and a word should mark the parameter that takes it. Today it is the other way round (5.15b): plain takes and borrow looks.

Why. Most functions only look, so today most parameters carry a word and the rare one that takes carries none. The common thing should be the plain thing, and the thing that empties the caller's variable should be the one that is visible.

When. In the audit stage, after manual memory. It touches the checker, both backends' tests, every example and every page that shows borrow, so it is done in one piece and not mixed into another stage. The word is to be chosen then, with the reasons written here.

(Done: 5.19 to 5.19o.)

The audit's list, carried on from 5.16i: functions reaching top-level lists and objects, methods taking out what the object owns, narrowing after case none, the order C evaluates arguments in (5.9q), list[int] + list[str] under --strict, list[int or none], and var f = double (5.17b).

5.18 Memory by hand lives beside the other two, and only inside unsafe { }

Asked by the owner of the language: can C-like manual memory still be used, with the collector and with one owner already in place? Yes. A program asks for memory with allocate, uses it through a pointer, and gives it back with free. The lines that Zeno cannot check go inside unsafe { } (docs/unsafe.md).

Why have it. Firmware needs two things that neither the collector nor one owner can give: a register at an address the data sheet names, and now and then a block of bytes laid out exactly as the program says. Without them the language stops one step short of the hardware.

Why the block. A wrong pointer damages something far from the line that is wrong. If every such line is inside a marked block, the search for the mistake has an end: grep -n unsafe. Everything outside the blocks keeps every promise Zeno made before this stage.

Turned down: no block, as in C. Then any line may be the one, and the promises hold nowhere. Turned down: a flag for the whole file or build. It says "somewhere in here", which is what the block is for avoiding.

Checked against "unambiguous and intuitive". One word, one meaning (5.18a). One way to write each thing (5.18c, 5.18d). The messages say what to write. The one thing that is not intuitive, that a compiled program does not check, is said in every message of zeno run (5.18e).

5.18a unsafe is a reserved word, and the block is a statement that opens a scope

unsafe { ... } is a statement, like the body of an if. What is declared inside ends with it. The word cannot be used as a name.

Why reserved. The first version made unsafe an ordinary name except in front of {. It worked, and it meant that if unsafe { was a condition while unsafe { was the block: one word with two meanings, told apart by where it stands. Nothing is lost by reserving it.

Why a statement. if and match are statements in Zeno. A block that gives a value would be the only one. let x = unsafe { p[0] } is refused, and the message says to put the whole line inside.

Why it opens a scope. Every { } in Zeno does. One that did not would be a surprise of its own. The cost is real: a value read inside the block and used after it needs a var declared before the block, or the return goes inside, as Queue.get does in driver.zeno.

Turned down: unsafe function, as Rust has. It would be a second way to mark the same thing. A function that takes a pointer is safe to call. Only the lines that use the pointer are not.

For the audit. owned, borrow and strict are still ordinary names except in one place each. They should be looked at with the same eyes.

5.18b Five things need the block, and nothing else does

Reading through a pointer, writing through one, free, reading a register and writing a register. Making a pointer or a register, passing it, keeping it in a field or a list, p + n, p - n, == and print are safe anywhere.

Why. Those five are exactly the things that can damage memory or hardware. An address that points nowhere harms nothing until it is used, so working one out is not marked. If harmless lines needed the block too, the blocks would grow, and a big block says less.

Why allocate is safe. It can fail in one way, by running out, and that stops the program with a message. It damages nothing.

A function written inside the block starts safe again. It is a new function and may be called from anywhere, so its body needs its own block. The checker counts blocks per function (Frame.unsafe_blocks).

5.18c pointer[T] points at numbers of one size

T is int, float, or one of u8 u16 u32 i8 i16 i32. p[i] is item i. p + n and p - n move by items. Two pointers compare with ==.

Why pointer[u8]. It is written like list[int], which every Zeno programmer already reads. Turned down: *u8, ptr[u8]. The syntax direction is whole words and little punctuation.

Why numbers only. A bool has no size the language promises. An object or a list has an owner or the collector, and a raw pointer to one would be a second way to reach it that neither knows about.

Why items and not bytes. (p + 1)[0] and p[1] must be the same thing. It is also what C does, so nothing has to be unlearned.

Why 1 + p is refused. One way to write it: the pointer, then how far. The message says so.

Turned down for now: < between pointers, p - q, reading a pointer[u8] as a pointer[u32], the address of a variable. Nothing written so far needs them, and each is a new way to be wrong. They can be added when a program asks.

What is written through a pointer follows the sized-number rules (2.8 to 2.8c and docs/sized-ints.md), so p[0] = 300 on a pointer[u8] is an error as you compile, and arithmetic needs u8(...).

5.18d allocate(u8, 16) and free(p)

The type of one item first, then how many. The memory is all zero. Fewer than one item stops the program. free wants the pointer that allocate gave.

Why the type first. The type decides what comes back, so it is read first. register(u32, address) has the same order.

Why whole words. Turned down: malloc, a name that says nothing to someone who has not met C. Turned down: alloc, an abbreviation. free is already the whole word.

Why zero. C's malloc gives whatever was there before. A program that read it would print one thing under zeno run and another when compiled. Zeroing costs one calloc.

Why running out stops the program. Giving back none would put a check on every allocate, for a case most programs cannot recover from. Was provisional (11.2): firmware that must survive it will want pointer[u8] or none.

There is no null pointer. A pointer that may be missing is pointer[u8] or none, checked like any other value that may be none.

5.18e zeno run checks every use of a pointer, and a compiled program checks nothing

zeno run and the VM keep the block behind each pointer: its length and whether it was freed. Out of bounds, use after free, a second free, and free of a pointer into the middle each stop the program at that line. A compiled program has the address only, as in C.

Why not check when compiled. A check needs the length and a freed mark to travel with every pointer. Then a pointer is no longer an address: it cannot be handed to C or taken from it, and every access costs a comparison. Zeno has a checked sequence already. It is called a list. A pointer is for when the check is what you want to leave out.

Why check in zeno run. It is the cheapest place to find these mistakes, and it makes zeno run the tool that C programmers reach for a sanitizer for. Every message says that a compiled program would not have stopped, so nobody learns the wrong thing from it.

This is the one place where zeno run and a compiled program may differ, and only for a program that is wrong.

5.18f Memory by hand comes from the heap, outside the collector, or from the pool

In an ordinary build and with --firmware, allocate is calloc. The collector does not scan the block and never frees it. With --no-collector there is no heap (5.9k), so it comes from the pool.

Why outside the collector. The program said it would free it. A collector that freed it too would free it twice. The block holds only numbers (5.18c), so there is nothing in it for the collector to find.

--memory adds one line where there is a collector: how many blocks allocate gave and how many were never freed. With no collector the ordinary report already says it: blocks left at the end.

An object that holds a pointer does not free what it points at when its owner frees the object. Memory asked for by hand is given back by hand.

5.18g A register is its own type: Register[u32], from board

register(u32, 0x4002_0014) gives a Register[u32]. r.read() and r.write(v) go inside unsafe { }. The size is u8, u16 or u32.

Turned down: a register as a pointer at a fixed address, as C writes it. That needs turning a number into a pointer, which is the most dangerous thing a pointer can do, and it would be needed in every driver. And memory and a register are not the same: the C compiler may skip or repeat a memory access, and must not do that to a register. Two types let each be compiled as what it is: [ ] for one, one volatile access for the other.

Why read() and write(v), and not a field. Reading a register can change the hardware. It should look like something that happens, which a call does and a field does not.

Why the capital letter. What the language has built in is written small (int, list, pointer). What comes from a module is written like a class (Pin, Register).

Why unsigned only. A register is a pattern of bits.

No helpers such as set_bit. |, & and ~ already do it, and keep whatever works.

5.18h The simulated board prints every write to a register

A pin that is driven high twice prints one line, because it is still high. A register prints a line for every write, also one that changes nothing.

Why. On real hardware a write is an event. The same byte written twice to a serial port is two bytes sent. The first version printed only on a change, and the driver example then lost a line whenever two equal bytes followed each other.

5.18i Found on the way

  • bool used as a value (allocate(bool, 4)) said "bool is not defined". It is defined, as a type. The message now says that.
  • The C backend wrote an unreachable second return after unsafe { return p[0] }. A block that ends in return now ends the function.

For the audit, added to the list in 5.17h: the three words that are names except in one place (5.18a); a --firmware build cannot catch an OverflowError that u8(...) raises, which zeno run can; and messages that say "1 blocks" or "1 item(s)".

5.19 The audit: every part of the language is held against "unambiguous and intuitive"

The owner of the language asked for it in one sentence: the entire language must be unambiguous and intuitive. Each feature was read again with five questions, and what failed one was changed or written down with its reason (docs/audit.md).

  1. Is there one obvious way to write it?
  2. Can any line be read two ways, by the parser or by a person?
  3. Does anything happen that the line does not say?
  4. Is every word a plain word, with one meaning?
  5. When it is refused, does the message say what to write instead?

Why these five. They are principles 1, 2 and 4 turned into questions that have a yes or a no. "Intuitive" on its own cannot be tested. "Does the line say what happens" can.

What an audit may change. Anything, including what earlier decisions settled, as long as the earlier entry stays in this file with a line that says what replaced it. The reasons of the time are part of the record.

5.19a A parameter looks, unless it says take

function report(task: Task) -> str { ... }            # looks
function retire(task: take Task) -> int { ... }       # takes, and frees

A parameter written with its type alone looks at the list, object, function or error it is given. The caller still has it afterwards. A parameter that takes the value over says take in front of its type, and then the caller's variable holds nothing. This carries out 5.17h, and replaces 5.8c and 5.15b, where plain took and borrow looked.

Why. Question 3. retire(task) emptied the caller's variable, and nothing on either line said so: the reader had to know that a parameter with no word takes. Now the line that empties a variable is the one that carries a word.

The count that 5.15b asked for. In the example programs 10 parameters only looked and 7 took. In the programs the C backend is tested with, which are longer, 57 looked and 12 took. Under the old rule the larger group carried a word. Under this one the smaller does. The count alone would not have settled it for the examples. The reason above does.

Why it is safe to make looking the default. A look cannot be kept, returned or given away, and the checker already enforced that for borrow. So a forgotten take is always an error at the line that tries to keep the value, and the message names the word. A forgotten borrow used to be silent until the caller used its variable again.

self always looks, as before. A method that made its own object vanish would surprise everyone.

Turned down: a mark at the call as well (retire(take task)). It would show the move where it happens. But the checker reports any use after a move with the line of the move, and one way to write a call is worth more than a second reminder. (Was provisional (11.2), as in 5.8a.)

5.19b The word is take, and it stands in front of the type

frame: take Frame, and in a function type function(take Frame) -> int.

Why take. It is what the function does, said from the function's side, in the word the messages already used ("takes the value over"). It reads as a sentence: "send takes a frame".

Turned down: owned. It already says what a value is ("this list has one owner"). On a parameter it would also have to say what the function does, and in an ordinary build xs: owned list[int] is needed for the first meaning. One word, two meanings: question 4. Turned down: move. It describes the call from the caller's side, and it is a term from C++ and Rust more than a plain word. Turned down: keep. A function that takes a frame and sends it does not keep it. Turned down: own. One letter from owned.

Why in front of the type and not the name. owned goes there, and a function type has no parameter names to put it in front of.

take on a number is refused. A number, a bool and a text are copied, so there is nothing to take, and a word that did nothing would be noise. The message says to write the type alone.

5.19c owned, take and strict are reserved words, and borrow is gone

A word of the language is never also a name. let take = 3, let owned = 1 and queue.take() are refused, and the message says where the word goes. This replaces the part of 5.8a that left the words unreserved, and finishes what 5.18a began with unsafe.

Why. Question 2. strict on the first line set a mode and on the second line was a variable. owned in front of class was a word and in front of = was a name. The parser could always tell, so nothing was ambiguous to the machine. A person had to stop and work it out. One rule with no exceptions is easier to hold than "reserved, except these three".

What it costs. take is a good name for a method of a queue. driver.zeno had one and now calls it get. That is the whole cost found so far.

Type names stay unreserved (int, list, pointer). They are names, of types, and a type and a variable never stand in the same place.

borrow is no longer a word. Writing it in front of a type gives "borrow is no longer a word. A parameter looks at what it is given without one: write the type alone. A parameter that takes the value over says take". Everywhere else it is an ordinary name again.

Why not keep borrow as an optional word. Then there would be two ways to write a parameter that looks: question 1.

5.19d Where values are shared, owned list[T] on a parameter looks at any list

In a build with a collector there are two kinds of list, and a parameter can ask for either:

Written Means Accepts
xs: list[int] a shared list, which the function may keep shared lists
xs: owned list[int] a look at a list any list, owned or shared
xs: take owned list[int] the function takes an owned list over owned lists

An owned list passed to a plain list[int] is refused, and the message says to write owned list[int]. An owned class needs no word: its type already says that it has one owner, so frame: Frame looks and frame: take Frame takes.

Why the middle row accepts a shared list. Anyone may look at a shared value. A function that only reads a list should be usable with every list, and this is how to write that function once.

take on a shared value changes nothing. take Point, where Point is a plain class in a build with a collector, is allowed and does what Point does. So a program written for a build with no collector, where the word matters, is the same program in every build.

5.19e One program that a build with no collector accepts and an ordinary build refuses

5.15a promised that the rules for one owner only ever refuse. There is now one case the other way round:

function total(xs: list[int]) -> int { return len(xs) }
let window: owned list[int] = [0, 0]
print(total(window))

With no collector every list has one owner and xs looks, so this is fine. With a collector xs: list[int] is a shared list that total may keep, and window cannot go there.

Why it is left. Accepting it with a collector would let a shared place hold an owned list (rule 6 of 5.8). Refusing it with no collector would mean that the word owned changes something in that build, which 5.15 says it does not. The message names the fix, and the fixed program (xs: owned list[int]) builds both ways. A program that never writes owned, or writes it on every list it means, never meets this.

5.19f What take cannot do yet

  • A type parameter cannot be taken: function keep[T](x: take T) is refused, because T may be a number. A value with one owner cannot fill a type parameter at all yet (noted in docs/generics.md).
  • A parameter with no type is only allowed where values are shared (--no-collector needs every type written), so the question of what it does with an owner does not come up.

5.19g + on two lists follows the rule append has

xs.append("three") on a list[int] was always refused. xs += ["three"] and [1, 2] + ["three"] were not, even under --strict. Now + and += refuse two lists whose item types are both known and have nothing in common: "+ joins two lists that hold the same kind of thing, and these are list[int] and list[str]".

Why. Two ways to add to a list with two different rules fails question 1 of the audit (one way to write each thing, and the same answer from each way). The compilers also had to give such a list the kind "anything", which a program that wrote types everywhere never asked for.

What still joins. A list that may hold none with one that does not (the result may hold none), and a list whose items are not known yet, such as []. The test is exactly: neither side holds "anything", and the joined type would.

Turned down. Refusing only under --strict. append refuses without it, so + would still disagree with append in an ordinary check.

5.19h After case none, the later cases know the value is there

In a match on a value that may be none, every case after case none sees the value without the none. It works for a new name (case point { point.name }) and for the variable itself (case _ { found.name }). If every case none leaves (return, raise, break, continue), the lines after the match know too. An enum that may be none is complete with case none and a case for each of its own, and needs no case _. A missing none is named: "this match does not handle none".

Why. if found is none { return } already taught the lines below it. A match that said the same thing and was not believed made the writer ask twice, and there were two spellings of one test with two different results (questions 1 and 3).

How. The checker narrows the name in its scope, the same record that if x is not none makes, so both compilers unwrap the value with the code they already had. A new name is declared with the whole type and then narrowed, because the compilers store an int or none differently from an int and need to know that the box is still there.

Turned down. Narrowing only the new name and not the variable. The else of an if narrows the variable, so match would have been the odd one.

5.19i Every function can look at a let at the top of the file

With one owner (an owned value, or any list or object with no collector), a let at the top of the file can be read by any function, any method and any function that is kept in a variable. They may change what is inside it (table[0] = 5, tasks.append(t)), but not take an item out or put a new value in an owned field. In return the top of the file may never give that let away, before or after the function is written: "table is used by a function, on line 3, so it cannot be given away".

Why. A table or a configuration object at the top of the file, read by the functions below it, is the first thing a firmware program writes. The old refusal was sound and nobody would guess it (question 3: nothing surprising). A let cannot be given a new value, so if it is never given away it holds the same value until the program ends, and a look at it can never outlive it. That is a proof, and it needs no new word.

Why not a var. A var can be given a new value, which frees the old one while a function may be looking at it. The message says so and names let as the fix when the value never changes.

Why a function may not take out of it. The top of the file owns it. A function that popped from it would be doing the owner's work from a place that cannot see what the owner's loops are looking at. A method of the object can (5.19k), because the call is then checked where the owner is.

Found on the way. A name used above the line that declares it said "table is not defined". It now says "table is declared on line N, which is further down", and that the declaration has to come first. The rule itself (a name is usable below its declaration, in functions and methods too) stays: one rule for every name.

Turned down. A word such as global or static. let at the top of the file already says everything the word would.

5.19k A method can take out what its own object holds

function next(self) -> Task { return self.tasks.pop() } and function remember(self, task: take Task) { self.last = task } are allowed. They were refused because self only looks, and taking out or replacing is for the owner.

The rule moves to the caller. The checker reads the method's body. If it takes out or replaces something self owns, the method is marked, and each call of it is checked as the same act done by hand: only the owner of the object may call it, not inside a loop or a match that is looking at something the object owns, not on a line that has already looked at it, and not with an argument that looks at the same object. Inside the method the loops and cases over self count in the same way.

Why. A class that cannot pop from its own list cannot look after its own parts, and the only way round was a free function that took the whole object, which is not what a class is for. The danger (a loop is looking at the item that goes) is at the call, so that is where the check belongs.

Why it is not written down. A word on the method (function next(take self) or changes) would be a second thing to keep in step with the body, and a method that said it and did not do it would be a new kind of mistake. The checker already works out a return type that is not written in the same way. The message at the call names the method and says what it does: "call next, which takes out or replaces something the object owns".

Turned down. Letting any parameter that looks do this. self is special because the call names the object (queue.next()), so the caller's own checks can be run on it. For a plain parameter the word take already says "this function is the owner now".

(There is no 5.19j: the letter is left out so it is not read as an i.)

5.19l var f = double gets the type of the function

let f = double holds the one function double, as before. var f = double with no type written now has the type function(int) -> int, so f = triple is allowed when triple fits.

Why. A var is something that will be given another value. A type that only one value fits made the var useless and the message absurd: "the new value should be a function, but this is a function". For a let the narrow type is the useful one, since a call through it is a direct call.

Turned down. Asking for the type to be written (var f: function(int) -> int = double). It still can be, but var n = 1 does not need : int, and this is the same case.

5.19m In a firmware build, raises of a fault that nothing raises is refused

A firmware build stops at a division by zero, an index out of range or a number that does not fit (5.5l). A try around such a line was already refused there. A function could still say raises ZeroDivisionError, divide, and be called inside a try: zeno run caught the error and the firmware program stopped. Now a function that declares ZeroDivisionError, IndexError or OverflowError, with no raise of that kind in its body and no call that raises it as a value, is refused when the build is firmware (and so with no collector), with what to do: test before it happens and take the raises off, or build without --firmware.

Why. The two ways of running a program may differ only where the compiler has said so (question 4 of the audit). This was a place where they differed in silence.

Why not make the fault travel as a value. Then every division and every index in a firmware program would carry a status and a branch, and a function that divides could fail in a way its type does not say. 5.5l chose a stop for exactly that reason, and MISRA wants the test written before the division anyway.

What still works. raise ZeroDivisionError("...") written by hand travels as a value in every build, so a function that tests b == 0 and raises may say raises ZeroDivisionError.

5.19n Messages count in words

"takes 2 argument(s), but 1 were given" is now "takes 2 arguments, but 1 was given", and a compiled program's memory report says "1 block" and "2 blocks". Two small functions in error.rs (counted, were) do it for the interpreter, the VM and the checker, so the three cannot drift apart again.

Why. A message is part of the language. One that is not a sentence makes the reader stop at the grammar and not at the mistake.

5.19o Looked at in the audit and left as it is

Each was held against the five questions of 5.19 and kept.

  • list[int or none] with no collector stays refused (5.16h). Every number would need a block of its own. The message names what to write, so it is a limit and not a surprise.
  • A function that is kept cannot take over a list or an object from outside it (5.17c). It may look for one line, or look at a let at the top of the file (5.19i). The kept function has no place to say take, and a parameter does.
  • A value made inside unsafe { } and used after it needs a var before the block (5.18a). Every pair of braces opens a scope. A block that did not would be the one exception.
  • The order C works out the arguments of a call (5.9q). What is printed never depends on it, and calls that change something are already put in order. Only the highest mark of the text room in a --memory report can move by a few bytes, when one statement shows a list and builds other text. Fixing it means a temporary for every piece of text, which makes all the C harder to read. The page on the audit says to leave the room some headroom.
  • A function or a method sees only what is declared above it. One rule for every name, and the message now names the line (5.19i).

What is left for later, none of it part of the audit: the limits in 5.19f, and the lists in 5.16i and 5.17g.

6. The standard library

6.1 What goes into the library, and in what order it is built

The standard library is everything a program can use without writing it: the functions that have no module (print, len), the methods of text and lists, and the modules (math, time, board). It is built in this order:

  1. Text and lists (this stage).
  2. Reading and writing: a line from the keyboard, files, the arguments a program was started with.
  3. More of math.
  4. A dictionary, which is a new type of the language and a stage of its own.

Why text and lists first. Everything after them is written with them: a file is read as text and cut into lines, and a line is found, sliced and joined.

What gets in. A method is added only if (a) most programs in the big languages use it, and (b) it cannot be said in one short expression with what Zeno already has. That keeps out more than it lets in:

Left out Because Zeno already says it
text.contains(part), xs.contains(x) part in text, x in xs
text.is_digit() "0" <= ch and ch <= "9"
sorted(xs), reversed(xs) xs.slice(0), then sort() or reverse()
xs.remove(x) xs.find(x), then pop(at) (6.5)
xs.extend(ys) xs += ys

Python's str has 47 methods and its list 11. Zeno's text now has 10 and its list 12. Every one of them was held against the five questions of the audit (5.19): one way to write it, one way to read it, nothing surprising, the same in every build, and a message that says what to write.

6.2 A method is asked of a value, and a function makes a value of another kind

text.find(" "), xs.sort(), words.join(", ") are methods. int("12"), str(3), float("1.5"), u8(n) and now char(65) are functions, each named for what it gives.

Why. A method reads in the order things happen (3.6b), and the value in front says what is being worked on. A conversion has no value of the new kind to be asked of yet, so it is a function, and its name is the kind it makes.

So the number of a character is "A".code() (asked of the character), and the character of a number is char(65) (made from a number, as str(65) makes "65").

Turned down. ord and chr, Python's names: abbreviations that say nothing to someone who has not met them (principle 2). A function code("A"): it would take a name, code, that half of all programs want for a variable.

Kept as they were. len, min, max and sum are functions and stay functions. They were decided with the first interpreter, every example uses them, and making them methods would add a second way to write each for nothing.

6.3 find answers a position or none, never -1

let space = line.find(" ")
if space is none { return line }
return line.slice(0, space)

text.find(part) and xs.find(item) give an int or none.

Why. -1 is a number. In Python text[text.find("x")] quietly reads the last character when there is no x. In Zeno a negative position is an error (2.6), so the mistake would at least stop the program, but far from where it was made. none cannot be used as a position at all: the checker asks for the test first (3.12), and if or match then gives the number (5.19h). The mistake is caught before the program runs (principle 1).

Turned down. Raising an error when the thing is not there (Python's index): "not there" is an ordinary answer, not a failure. Having both, as Python does: two ways to ask one question.

With one owner. tasks.find(Task("d", 4)) only looks at what it is given. An item made in the brackets belongs to the statement, which frees it when it is over.

6.4 A part is taken with slice(from, to), and xs[1:3] is not Zeno

text.slice(1, 3) and xs.slice(1, 3) take positions 1 and 2. to is not taken, as in range. slice(from) goes on to the end.

Why a method. [1:3] would be the one place where : does not mean "a type follows". Python's slice has three optional parts and negative positions, and each needs a rule that has to be remembered. A method needs a name and nothing else.

Outside the text or the list is an IndexError. Python cuts the slice to fit without a word, so "abc"[2:9] is "c". A position that is out of range is nearly always a sum that went wrong, and Zeno says so, as it does for xs[9].

Writing xs[1:3] gets told what to write: "a part of a list or a str is taken with .slice(from, to), as in xs.slice(1, 3)".

Positions in text count characters, as len and text[i] do, and not bytes. In UTF-8 a character is one to four bytes, so finding position 7 means walking past seven characters. That is the price of "wörld".slice(1, 2) being "ö" and never half of it.

With one owner. A slice of numbers or of text is a new list with copies. A slice of objects is refused, as filter is: the items would be in two lists, and each has one owner.

6.5 pop(at), insert(at, item), clear() and reverse()

xs.pop() takes the last item out, as before, and xs.pop(2) takes out the one at position 2. xs.insert(0, x) puts x in front; the position may be the length of the list, which is what append does. clear() empties the list. reverse() turns it round where it is.

Why pop(at) and no remove. remove means "by value" in Python and "by position" in Rust, and in Java list.remove(1) on a list of numbers is a well-known trap, because it could be either. pop(at) can only be a position. Taking out by value is find and then pop, two lines that have to say what happens when the value is not there.

Why clear(). Most lists are held by a let, which cannot be given a new list.

Why reverse() and sort() change the list and give nothing back. A new list is a new block of memory, which a program on a small board may not want to spend. And a method that gives the list back invites let ys = xs.sort(), where it is not clear whether xs changed. A copy is asked for by name: xs.slice(0).

With one owner pop(at) and clear() take items out, so they are held to the rules of pop() (5.15e): only the owner, not in a loop over the list, and on a line of its own. insert keeps what it is given, as append does. A method of a class may do all of them to a list its object holds (5.19k).

6.6 sort() is for numbers and text, smallest first, and is heapsort in every engine

Only a list of ints, floats, sized numbers or text can be sorted. Anything else is refused: "sort puts numbers or text in order, smallest first, and this is list[bool]. There is no one order for those items, so write the loop that puts them in yours". (Since chapter 21 it says to give sort a function, which is that chapter.)

Why only those. They are what < compares, and each has one order that nobody has to be told. Tasks could be ordered by name or by priority, and a sort() that picked one would be a guess. Sorting by a function that the program gives is left for when a real program needs it (was provisional: 11.3).

Why heapsort. It sorts inside the list, so it asks for no memory. It is a loop and not a function that calls itself, so it needs no deep stack, and MISRA has nothing to say against it. It takes about n log n steps whatever the order was to begin with, so there is no bad case to find on the bench.

Why the interpreter does not use Rust's sort. Decision 5.5d says a compiled program prints what zeno run prints. Heapsort may swap two items that compare equal, and that can be seen: 0.0 and -0.0 are equal and print differently. So zeno run sorts with the same steps as the compiled program, written once in Rust (builtins.rs) and once in C (runtime.c), and a test sorts such a list in every build.

Turned down. C's qsort: it is missing from some firmware C libraries, and the order it leaves equal items in is not defined.

Text is ordered by the code of each character, as < orders it, so "B" comes before "a".

6.7 join is a method of the list

words.join(", ") reads in order: the words, joined with a comma. Python's ", ".join(words) starts with the least important part. JavaScript and Rust write it Zeno's way.

Only a list of text. A list of numbers is refused, with what to do: "Make each item text first, with str( )". Nothing in Zeno turns into text by itself (2.3).

6.8 code() needs exactly one character, and char refuses 0

"A".code() is 65 and char(65) is "A". The numbers are Unicode's, so "€".code() is 8364.

code() on text that is empty, or longer than one character, is a ValueError. Taking the first character without a word would hide a mistake.

char(n) is a ValueError for a number that is no character: below 1, above 1114111, or in the range Unicode keeps free (55296 to 57343).

Why not char(0) (was provisional: 11.3). In a compiled program text ends at the first zero byte, as in C, so text with a zero in it would be cut short there and not in zeno run. Bytes that may be zero belong in a list[u8].

6.9 In a firmware build, a bad position or a bad code stops the program

A slice out of range, insert or pop at a position that is not there, code() of two characters and char(0) are faults, as a bad index always was (5.5l): zeno run and an ordinary build raise an error that can be caught, and a firmware build stops.

The rule of 5.19m now covers ValueError as well: a function that says raises ValueError with nothing in it that raises one as a value is refused in a firmware build. int(text) does raise one as a value there, so raises ValueError beside int(text) is still right.

6.10 These methods are written in Rust and C, not in Zeno (was provisional: 11.3)

Each method is written once for zeno run and the VM (builtins.rs), described to the checker (checker.rs), and compiled by both backends (emit_c.rs, emit_llvm.rs) into a call of one C function in runtime.c.

Why not in Zeno itself. Two things are missing. A library written in Zeno has to be brought into the program that uses it, which is the next stage (modules across files). And pop(at), insert and clear take items out of a list or put them in, which a function that was only lent the list may not do when each item has one owner (5.15e). They have to be built in.

Once modules exist, what can be written in Zeno will be.

6.11 The world outside is files, the keyboard and the command line

Part 2 of the library lets a program reach what is outside it. There are three such things on a computer, and each has one way in:

Outside In Zeno
The screen print(...), as before
The keyboard input()
Files import files, then files.read(path) and four more
The command line import program, then program.arguments()

Why print and input are plain words and the rest are modules. Nearly every first program prints and asks. Files and arguments are for some programs, and on a board there may be neither, so they are asked for by name, as board and time are (3.15).

Unambiguous and intuitive. Each function does one thing and its name is the plain word for it. There is no second way to do any of them.

6.12 A file is read whole and written whole, with no handle

let text = files.read("notes.txt")
files.write("notes.txt", "a new start\n")
files.append("notes.txt", "one more line\n")

There is no open, and so no close.

Why. A file that is opened is a thing that has to be closed, on every way out of the block, by somebody. That is the question of who owns a list (5.8) asked again about something else, and the small files that Zeno programs read so far do not need it asked. A whole file as one str is also the form the text methods of part 1 already work on.

Turned down. C's and Python's open(path, "r"): the second argument is text that picks what the function does, so a wrong letter is found when the program runs and not when it is checked. Python's with open(...) as f: a new kind of block for one use.

(was provisional: 11.3) A file too large to hold at once needs reading in pieces. That will be added, with an owner for the open file, when a real program needs it.

6.13 write replaces, append adds, and neither adds a line end

write makes the file, or empties one that is there. append makes the file, or adds to the end of one that is there. What is written is the text and nothing more: a program that wants lines ends them with "\n" itself.

Why two functions and not a flag. files.write(path, text, true) does not say what true means. Two names do.

Why no line end is added. print adds one because it writes lines to a screen. A file is whatever the program says it is, and files.write(path, files.read(other)) must make an exact copy.

6.14 A file that cannot be used is a FileError, and it is a value in every build

Reading a file that is not there, writing into a folder that is not there, removing a file twice: each raises FileError, with a message that names the file (there is no file `notes.txt`, cannot read `notes.txt`, cannot write `notes.txt`, cannot remove `notes.txt`).

It can be caught in a firmware build too, unlike a bad index (5.5l, 6.9). Why. A bad index is a mistake in the program: the program could have looked at the length first. A missing file is not: no test beforehand rules it out, because the file can go between the test and the read. It is an error in the sense of 4.1, something that goes wrong in a correct program, like int(text) on text from outside. So a function that reads a file says raises FileError or catches it, and the checker holds it to that in every build.

Turned down. files.read answering str or none: none cannot say whether the file was missing or was not text. A number that says how it went, as C's errno does: nothing makes the caller look at it.

One kind of error, not several. FileNotFoundError, PermissionError and the rest of Python's family are told apart by programs that go on to do something different for each. The message tells a person which it was. (was provisional: 11.3)

6.15 exists asks whether there is a file that can be read

files.exists(path) is true for a file that files.read could open. It is false for a folder, and for a path with nothing at it. It cannot fail, so it does not raise.

Why a folder is not there. The module is about files. A program asks exists to decide whether to read, and a true that is followed by "cannot read" would be a wrong answer to that question.

6.16 A file holds text, and what is not text is a FileError

files.read gives a str, and a str is characters (6.4). So the bytes of the file must be UTF-8, and none of them may be zero, because text in a compiled program ends at a zero byte (6.8). A file that is something else, such as a picture, raises FileError: `logo.png` does not hold text.

Why not read it anyway. zeno run and a built program would disagree about its length, and the checks that make slice and code count characters would be working on bytes that are no characters.

Nothing is changed on the way in or out. Files are opened as bytes, so "\r\n" in a file from Windows arrives as "\r\n", and write puts down exactly what it was given. lines() is where line ends are dealt with (6.17).

(was provisional: 11.3) Bytes that are not text belong in a list[u8], and a files.read_bytes that gives one is for the stage that needs it.

6.17 lines() is a method of text, and knows both line ends

text.lines() gives a list[str], one item to a line, without the line ends.

  • A line ends at "\n". A "\r" just before that "\n" goes with it.
  • The last line need not end: "a\nb" and "a\nb\n" are both two lines.
  • Empty text has no lines. "\n" is one empty line.

Why not split("\n"). It gives "a\nb\n" a third, empty, piece, and leaves a "\r" on the end of every line of a file from Windows. Both are the kind of surprise that is found late.

Why on text and not files.lines(path). Lines also come from the keyboard, from a serial port, from a string in a test. One method on str serves all of them, and files.read(path).lines() reads as what it does.

6.18 input() gives a line, or none when there are no more

while true {
    let line = input()
    if line is none {
        break
    }
    print(line.upper())
}

input() gives the next line without its line end. input("Name: ") prints the question first and stays on that line. At the end of what there is to read (Ctrl-D at a keyboard, the end of a file piped in) it gives none.

Why str or none. The end comes to every program that reads until there is no more, and that is a correct program, so it is not an error to catch. And the type makes the checker ask for the test: input() used as text is refused with "Check it with is not none".

Turned down. Python's EOFError: the ordinary end of the loop written as try/except. An empty str at the end, as in Lua and shell: an empty line in the middle looks the same.

Why the name. print puts a line out and input takes one in. It is the word Python programmers already know. read_line was the other candidate, and files.read would then have had a namesake that reads something else.

It gives text and nothing else. A number is int(line), which can fail, and says so (2.3).

6.19 program.arguments() gives the words after the program's name

zeno run copy.zeno a.txt b.txt        ["a.txt", "b.txt"]
./copy a.txt b.txt                    ["a.txt", "b.txt"]

The program's own name is not in the list. In C argv[0] and in Python sys.argv[0] it is, and every program that uses them starts by stepping over it. words[0] in Zeno is the first thing the user wrote.

A function, giving a new list each time. board.led is a value because there is one LED. A list is something a program appends to and gives away, so each call makes one that is the caller's own, which is also what one owner needs (5.15).

With zeno run, the words after the file are the program's, even ones that look like Zeno's options, and Zeno's own options go before the file. zeno run copy.zeno --strict gives the program --strict. Why. Otherwise a program could never be given a word Zeno also knows, and which words those are would change with each version.

main takes the command line only in a program that asks for it, so the C and the IR of every other program are what they were.

Left out (was provisional: 11.3): program.exit(code). A program ends by reaching its end, or with an error nobody caught, which ends it with status 1. Environment variables: no program has needed one yet.

6.20 With no collector and on a board

With no collector the text of a file, and a line from input(), are built text like any other (5.9c): they are in the text room until the statement ends, and kept text is copied to the pool. So a file larger than ZENO_TEXT_ROOM (1024 bytes unless given) stops the program with the message that names the macro, and so does a typed line that is longer than the room. A line is read straight into the room, a byte at a time, so it takes its own length and no more. --memory prints how much of the room was used.

(was provisional: 11.3) Reading a large file a line at a time, straight into the pool, belongs with the open file of 6.12.

On a board these functions are the C library's: fopen for files, standard input for input(), which firmware C libraries usually tie to a serial line. A board with no file system gets a FileError from every call, which a program can catch. They are not behind ZENO_BOARD (5.12): a board that has a C library has them already.

6.21 What math holds

Part 3 fills in the math module. It had pi, sqrt, floor, ceil, sin and cos.

Added What it is
math.e 2.718281828459045
math.tan(angle) beside sin and cos
math.asin(x), math.acos(x), math.atan(x), math.atan2(y, x) an angle from a ratio
math.exp(x) e to the power x
math.log(x), math.log2(x), math.log10(x) logarithms
math.round(x) the nearest whole number

How it was chosen. Each is something firmware arithmetic uses and that cannot be written in a line with what was there: a heading from two field readings, a temperature from a thermistor, decibels, the charge of a capacitor, a sine table (examples/instruments.zeno).

Left out, because Zeno has one way already.

In other languages In Zeno
pow(x, y) x ** y
trunc(x) int(x)
fabs(x) abs(x)
degrees(x), radians(x) angle.deg, angle.rad (6.22)
round(x, 2) f"{x:.2f}": digits after the point are a matter of showing
log(x, base) log2, log10, or log(x) / log(base)

Left out until something needs it (was provisional: 11.3): hypot, the hyperbolic functions, gcd, factorial, clamp, inf and nan as names, and random numbers, which are not arithmetic and want a module of their own.

6.22 .rad and .deg change an angle; degrees and radians still never meet by themselves

(180 deg).rad is 3.141592653589793 rad, and math.atan2(1, 1).deg is 45 deg.

This changes 1.15, which kept the two apart altogether because the factor between them, pi / 180, is not an exact number.

Why change it. The functions of 6.24 give radians and people read degrees, so every program with an angle in it needs the change, and without it each would write * 180 / math.pi by hand. .ms is already how a quantity is changed (1.15), so this is that way and not a new one.

Why they still do not meet in + or <. When two units meet, the answer is in the finer one, because that loses nothing. Between degrees and radians neither is finer and either way loses something, so 90 deg + 1 rad has no answer that is obviously right. The message says what to write:

`+` does not work between a quantity in rad and a quantity in deg. Change one of them first, with `.rad` or `.deg`

The factor is one multiplication, by pi / 180 or by 180 / pi, which is what Python's radians and degrees do, so (180 deg).rad is exactly math.pi and (math.pi * 1 rad).deg is exactly 180 deg.

6.23 sin, cos and tan take an angle: a plain number is radians

math.sin(30 deg)       # 0.49999999999999994
math.sin(0.5 rad)      # 0.479425538604203
math.sin(0.5)          # the same

Why a unit is accepted. Degrees handed to a function that counts in radians is the oldest mistake in this corner, and it gives a wrong number, not an error. With the unit on the value the function cannot be handed the wrong thing.

Why a plain number is accepted too, when sleep(500) is refused. sleep(500) is refused because 500 of what is anyone's guess. The sine of a plain number is a thing mathematics defines, and it means radians in every language and every textbook. Signal code is full of it: in sin(2 * math.pi * f * t) the argument was worked out and never was an angle somebody measured.

Turned down. Refusing plain numbers: every formula would need * 1 rad on the end. A second family, sind and cosd, as MATLAB has: two names for one thing.

A quantity that is not an angle is refused by the checker:

`sin` needs a number or an angle, such as `30 deg`, but this is a quantity in s

6.24 asin, acos, atan and atan2 give an angle in radians (was provisional: 11.3)

Their answer is a quantity in rad, not a float.

Why. A float that is secretly an angle is what units are there to end (1.15). math.atan2(y, x) > 45 would compare radians with a number the writer meant as degrees, and run. As a quantity it is refused, and math.atan2(y, x).deg > 45 deg says what is meant.

What it costs. Arithmetic with a plain number needs the unit put on or taken off, as with any quantity: angle + 2 * math.pi * 1 rad, angle / 1 rad. Passing it on to sin needs nothing.

It was provisional (11.3) because it was the first time a library function gives a quantity back that is not a time. If it gets in the way of real signal code, a plain float is the other choice.

atan2(y, x) takes y first, as in C, Python, JavaScript and Rust. It is the angle of the point (x, y), and the order is the one in atan(y / x). It takes plain numbers only (was provisional: 11.3): two lengths would be reasonable, and can come later.

6.25 round sends a half away from zero, and gives an int

math.round(2.5) is 3 and math.round(-2.5) is -3.

Why. It is the rule taught at school, and the one C's round and Rust's round follow. Turned down. Python's rule, a half to the nearest even number, so that round(2.5) is 2 and round(3.5) is 4: better for adding up many rounded numbers, and a surprise to nearly everyone who meets it.

Why an int. floor and ceil give one (their answer is a whole number, and an index or a register value is wanted next). A float too large for an int gives the largest int, as they do.

6.26 log is the natural logarithm, with log2 and log10 beside it

Why log and not ln. It is log in C, Python, JavaScript and Java, which is where the people Zeno is for come from. On a calculator log is base ten, so the two others have their base in their name and the walkthrough says which is which.

Why three functions and not log(x, base). math.log(125) / math.log(5) is 3.0000000000000004. log2(8) and log10(1000) are exactly 3, because the C library works them out directly. Bases other than e, 2 and 10 are rare.

6.27 A number that has no answer is a ValueError

math.log(0), math.log(-1), math.asin(1.5) and math.acos(-2) raise ValueError, with the number in the message, as math.sqrt(-1) always did.

Why not nan. C gives a nan here, which then spreads through every sum it touches and is printed twenty lines later. An error at the line that asked is easier to find.

In a firmware build they are faults and stop the program, as a bad index does (5.5l, 6.9): the program can test the number first.

An answer too big for a float is inf, as it is for * and **: math.exp(710) is inf.

6.28 The answers are the C library's, in every engine

A compiled program calls sin, log and the rest in the C library. zeno run and the VM reach the same functions through Rust. So every engine and build gives the same digits on one machine, which the tests hold them to.

Two machines with different C libraries can differ in the last digit of a result that is not exact. Why accept that. The other choice is Zeno's own sin, a few hundred lines that would be slower and no more right than the one already on the machine.

6.29 A dict is written ["Ada": 36], and one with nothing in it is [:]

let ages = ["Ada": 36, "Alan": 41]
let seen: dict[str, int] = [:]

A dict keeps one value under each key. It is written like a list, in square brackets, with key: value where a list has an item. Partha chose this form on 2026-11-15, from the forms that were put to him.

Why square brackets. In Zeno a pair of braces is always a block of code, and a pair of square brackets always holds values. Somebody reading a line never has to work out which of the two a bracket is.

Turned down. {"Ada": 36}, as in Python and JavaScript: braces would then mean a block in one place and a value in another, and {} after if ready could be read as either. JavaScript lives with exactly that puzzle. A word in front, as in dict("Ada": 36): longer, and it looks like a call, which it is not.

Why [:] for the empty one. [] is the empty list. The colon is what makes a dict a dict, so the empty dict keeps it. The parser knows which it is reading as soon as it has seen the first thing after [.

Unambiguous and intuitive. After the first value comes , or ] (a list) or : (a dict), and every entry after that must be the same kind. Mixing them is refused with a message that says how each is written. A dict prints exactly as it is written.

6.30 Its type is dict[str, int]: the keys, then the values

The same shape as list[int]. The name is Python's, which more people read than any other (the principles, last paragraph).

Turned down. map[str, int], as in Go: map is already the list method that changes every item. [str: int], as in Swift: a type that looks like a value, where every other type in Zeno is a word.

6.31 A key is a str or an int

Names and numbers: ages["Ada"], meanings[404]. Both compare exactly, which a key has to.

Why not a float. 0.1 + 0.2 is not 0.3, so a value stored under one could not be found under the other. That is a mistake the language can simply not offer.

Why not a bool, a list or an object. Two keys are an if. A list or an object can change while it is a key, and then it is filed in the wrong place. An enum case would be a good key and is the likely next one.

All the keys of one dict are the same type, and the checker refuses ["a": 1, 2: 3]. That is what lets it say what a loop variable is.

6.32 A value is anything a list can hold, and never none

This section used to say "an int, a float, a bool or a str", and was marked provisional until a real program asked for more. One did, and 10.11 has the rule as it is now and the reasons. With no collector the first rule still holds (10.14).

All the values of one dict are the same type, like the items of a list that says what it holds.

6.33 A dict remembers the order its keys were put in

A loop over a dict, keys(), values() and print all follow the order in which the keys were first stored. Storing under a key that is there does not move it.

Why. It is the one order a reader can predict from the program, and it is the same on every run, in every engine and in every build. The order a hash table happens to have differs between Rust and C, and the tests compare the output of eight builds line for line.

What it costs. pop closes the gap, so it takes longer the more keys come after the one taken out. Finding, storing and in do not depend on the size.

Turned down. No order at all (cheaper pop, and a program whose output changes when a key is added). Sorted by key (every store pays for it, whether or not anything is ever printed).

6.34 d[key] of a key that is not there is a KeyError

There are three ways to ask, and each says what the programmer knows:

Written Means A key that is not there
d[key] it is there KeyError
d.get(key) it may not be none
key in d is it? false

Why not none from d[key]. Every read would then be an int or none to test first, even where the key was stored on the line above. Why not 0 or "". That invents a value nobody stored, and a misspelt key becomes a wrong total (principle 1).

In a firmware build it is a fault and stops the program where it happens, as a bad index does (6.9). get and in are how a program that must not stop asks first.

6.35 get(key) gives the value or none, and get(key, instead) gives a value

let fan = seen.get(40)              # int or none
seen[code] = seen.get(code, 0) + 1  # int

With one argument the answer may be none, so the checker asks for the test before it is used, as it does for find (6.3). With two, the second is what to give when the key is not there, and it must be of the dict's value type, so the answer is a plain value.

Why one name for both. Both ask without failing. The second argument does not change what is asked, only what is given instead. It is Python's get.

It never adds the key. get only looks.

6.36 d[key] = value stores: a new key is added, one that is there gets the new value

Why one form. Counting, the commonest use, is seen[word] = seen.get(word, 0) + 1, where the program does not care which of the two happens. A program that does care asks with in.

Turned down. A method (d.set(key, value)) beside the brackets: two ways to write one thing.

The same key written twice in a dict that is written out is refused: ["a": 1, "a": 2]. Stored one after the other the second would replace the first, which is fine in a loop and a slip of the hand in one pair of brackets.

6.37 pop(key) takes a key out and gives its value

The word a list already uses for "take it out and hand it to me" (6.5). A key that is not there is a KeyError, as for d[key].

Turned down. remove, which would give nothing back: then getting the value and removing it are two lookups. A del d[key] statement: a new kind of statement for one type. A second argument for a key that is missing: in asks.

clear() empties the dict, as it empties a list.

6.38 A loop over a dict is given its keys; keys() and values() are new lists

for code in seen {
    print(code, seen[code])
}

Why the keys. With the key the value is one lookup away, and with only the value the key is lost. in asks about keys for the same reason.

Pairs. for code, count in seen is given each key and what is under it (10.12). It was added without changing what the loop with one name means, as this section said it could be.

The loop walks the keys as they were when it began, like a loop over a list. The body may add keys and take them out. keys() and values() give lists that are the caller's own for the same reason: changing the dict afterwards does not change them.

6.39 Two dicts are equal when the same keys have the same values
