["x": 1, "y": 2] == ["y": 2, "x": 1] is true. The order says how a dict was built, and == asks what it holds. + and < are not defined for dicts: there is no one meaning for either.

6.40 A dict is a list or two and an index, and is owned like a list

How it is kept. In a compiled program: a list of keys, a list of values beside it, and a table of positions that a key's hash leads into (open addressing, at most half full, doubled when it fills; text is hashed with FNV-1a). In zeno run and the VM: a Vec of entries and Rust's HashMap of positions. Both give the order of 6.33.

Why two lists. keys(), values() and the loop are then the list code that already exists, and so is freeing them.

Who frees it. In an ordinary build a dict is shared and the collector frees it, like a list. owned dict[str, int] gives it one owner by request. With no collector it always has one owner, and everything written in docs/ownership.md about a list holds for a dict: a second name takes it over, a parameter looks unless it says take, and the end of the owner's block frees it. The dict has copies of its own of any text in it (docs/text.md), and frees them too.

[:] with no type beside it cannot say what it will hold, and a build asks for the type, as it does for [].

A program that uses no dict pays nothing for them in a build for a board: the C compiler drops the functions nobody calls.

7. Programs in several files

A program that has grown past a few hundred lines wants more than one file. This section says how one file uses what another declares. The walk through the code is in modules.md.

Checked against "unambiguous and intuitive". One name is one file (7.2). What is written at the import is what is written at the use (7.3). A file means the same in every program that imports it (7.7). A name that could be read two ways is refused with a message that says what to write (7.8). And there is one mechanism, not two: a file of your own is imported with the words that import math (7.1).

7.1 A module is a file, and import is how it is brought in

import shapes                     # then: shapes.area(s), shapes.Point
from shapes import area, Point    # then: area(s), Point

import shapes reads the file shapes.zeno. These are the two forms that import math and from math import sqrt have had since the first stage, and a file of your own is used exactly as a module that comes with Zeno is.

Why a file is the module. There is nothing to declare: no module line, no header, no list of exports to keep in step with the code. The file's name is the module's name, so the reader of shapes.area knows which file to open.

Turned down. Pasting the other file in, as C's #include does: the names of two files meet, and what a line means depends on what was pasted above it. A header beside each file (C, C++): two places that describe one thing, and they drift apart. mod and use as two steps (Rust): the tree of modules is written out in one file and the files have to match it.

7.2 A module is looked for from the folder of the program's first file

import shapes is shapes.zeno in the folder of the file that was given to zeno run or zeno build. A dot is a folder: import drivers.uart is drivers/uart.zeno. That holds for every file of the program, wherever the file is: a file inside drivers/ that wants its neighbour writes import drivers.pins.

Why from the first file, and not from the file that imports. One name is then one file, everywhere in the program. If each file looked beside itself, import pins would mean one file in drivers/ and another in sensors/, and one file could be reached under two names and be read twice.

Turned down. A list of folders to search, set outside the program (Python's path, C's -I): which file is read then depends on the machine. Where other people's files are is said in the program's zeno.package: see section 8.

The five modules that come with Zeno keep their names: math, time, board, files, program. A file called math.zeno is never looked for by import math. (Was provisional (11.4): once library modules are themselves Zeno files, they will be found by one rule.)

7.3 import a.b is used as a.b.name

import drivers.uart
drivers.uart.send("ready")

What is written at the import is what is written at the use. A reader who meets drivers.uart.send halfway down the file can find its import by searching for the same words.

Turned down. Only the last word, uart.send (Go, Rust): two modules called uart in two folders then collide, and the fix is a second name for one of them. A second name by request, import drivers.uart as uart: two spellings for one module, and from drivers.uart import send already gives the short form. (Was provisional (11.4): if long names turn out to hurt in real programs, as is the way to add.)

7.4 Everything declared at the top of a file can be imported, unless it says private

(Was provisional. The word that hides a declaration is private, and it arrived in 10.1. The reasoning below is as it was written, and it held: no program had to change.)

Functions, classes, enums and their cases, and let, var and const variables. What the file itself imports is not passed on: shapes.math is not a name, even though shapes.zeno imports math.

Why everything. The first program anyone splits in two then works with no new word to learn. And it is the choice that can be changed without breaking programs: a word that hides a declaration (private function helper()) can be added later and every program written before it still means the same. Starting hidden and opening up later can be added too, but starting hidden means a mark on almost every line of every module from the first day.

Turned down. A leading underscore that means "leave this alone" (Python): a convention the compiler does not check (principle 1). A capital first letter that means "visible" (Go): spelling that carries meaning (principle 2). pub or export on each declaration: see above.

The cases of an enum are names of their own, as they are in one file. from shapes import Shape brings in the enum's name. Its cases are shapes.Circle, or Shape.Circle, or imported by name: from shapes import Shape, Circle.

7.5 An imported file runs once, before the file that imports it

A file can have lines at its top that do something: fill a table, print a banner. In a program of several files each file runs once, and a file's imports have all run before its own first line does. The file given to zeno run therefore runs last.

Why once. There is then one copy of each of the module's variables, whoever imports it.

So an import of a file goes at the top of the file, above everything that is not an import, and never inside a function or a block. The imported file runs first wherever the line is, and a line further down would say something else about the order than what happens. (import math may still be written anywhere. It has no lines of its own to run.)

Turned down. Running the imported file when the import line is reached (Python). The order in which two modules run then depends on which file happened to import one of them first.

7.6 Files that import each other are refused

error: these files import each other: `a` imports `b`, and `b` imports `a`.

Each would have to run before the other. Python runs one of them half-way and hands the other a module with some of its names missing. C has the programmer declare things ahead. Zeno says which files form the circle and what to do: move what they share into a file of its own, and import that from both.

7.7 A file sees what it declares and what it imports, and nothing else

An imported file cannot see the file that imports it. If shapes.zeno uses a name it does not declare, and the program's first file happens to declare one, that is an error. Otherwise what shapes.zeno means would depend on which program imports it.

Only the file of a module gives its variables a new value.

import counter
print(counter.total)     # reading is fine
counter.total = 0        # refused: give `counter` a function that does it

Where a variable can change is then one file, which is the first thing anyone debugging it wants to know. Python's from counter import total followed by total = 0 changes a copy and leaves the module's own variable alone, silently. In Zeno there is one variable, and the assignment is refused under either spelling. A list or object that the variable holds can still be changed through it, exactly as through a let.

7.8 A name that would have two readings is refused

Written Refused because
from a import x and from b import x which x?
from a import x and let x = 1 at the top of the same file which x?
import shapes and let shapes = 1 at the top of the same file shapes.x is a name of the module, or a field of the variable
import a and import a.b, where the module a declares a b a.b.x is x of a.b, or b.x of a. (With no b in a there is one reading, and both can be imported: see 8.11)
let s = shapes a module is not a value

A name declared inside a function or a block hides an imported one, as it hides any name from the top of the file. That is the rule Zeno already has, and a parameter called area should not stop working because an import was added.

7.9 A value prints by the name its file gave it

print(shapes.Point(1, 2)) prints Point(x=1, y=2), and an enum case prints as Dot. An error that nothing catches is reported as uncaught BadShape.

Why not shapes.Point(...). Then what shapes.zeno prints would change when it is imported instead of run by itself.

The compiler's own messages use the full name, shapes.Point, also for a mistake inside shapes.zeno. There the question is which Point, and two files may each have one.

7.10 strict is asked of one file

A file that begins with the line strict asks for every type in that file to be known before the program runs. Its imports are not made strict by it, and it does not make the file that imports it strict. zeno check --strict and zeno build ask it of every file.

Why. A typed library must be usable from a quick script, and a script's looseness must not leak into the library.

7.11 The files are joined into one program before anything checks it

The stage that reads the files gives every name that an imported file declares at its top the module's name in front, shapes.area, in that file and in every file that uses it, and then puts all the statements in one list, an imported file before its importer (src/modules.rs). Nobody can write shapes.area as one word in a source file, so the name cannot collide with one that somebody did write.

Why. The type checker, the interpreter, the VM and both compilers then take a program in ten files exactly as they take a program in one. Types that are worked out from use are worked out across files with no extra work. A C build is still one C file, and the C compiler still drops what nothing calls.

Turned down, for now. Compiling each file by itself and linking the results. That needs a written-down description of what every module offers, and generics and worked-out types make that description large. It is what makes a big program quick to rebuild, and it will be wanted then. (Was provisional: 11.4.)

In C a module's name is part of the C name: shapes.area is z_shapes_area. Two classes or enums that would get the same C name (shapes.Point and a class called shapes_Point) are refused with a message, as Stack[int] and Stack_int already were.

7.12 One number says the file and the line

Every part of a program remembers where it was written: a line and a column. With several files that is not enough, and adding a third number would have touched every place that passes a position along, down to each call into the runtime of a compiled program.

Instead the lines of the whole program are numbered in one run. The first file's lines are 1 to 999,999, as the editor shows them. The second file's start at 1,000,001, the third's at 2,000,001. Only the places that show a position to a person take the number apart again. Clang and the Rust compiler keep positions the same way, as one number into all the source text.

The cost. A file can have at most 999,999 lines and a program at most 2,000 files, so that the number fits in a C int. Both are checked.

8. Packages

A module is a file of the program itself. A package is a folder of modules that lives somewhere else: beside the program, or in somebody else's git repository. This section says how a program names the packages it needs, how an import finds a module of one, and how zeno fetch gets them. The walk through the code is in packages.md.

Checked against "unambiguous and intuitive". The project file is read aloud as it is written (8.2). A version is one exact thing, never a range that something has to solve (8.4). An import still names one file, and a file still has one name (8.5, 8.8). A package means the same in every program that needs it (8.6). There is one copy of each package in a program (8.7). Running a program never reaches for the network: one command does, and it is the one you typed (8.9). And there is still one mechanism: a module of a package is imported with the words that import a file of your own.

8.1 A package is a folder with a zeno.package in it

filters/
    zeno.package        package filters
    filters.zeno        the module `filters`
    lowpass.zeno        the module `filters.lowpass`

The file zeno.package makes the folder a package and gives it its name. Every .zeno file in the folder is a module of it. Nothing is listed: no table of modules, no exports. That is 7.1 again, one step up: the folder is the package as the file is the module.

A program is a package too. It has a zeno.package from the day it needs another package, and not before: a program in one folder that needs nothing has nothing to write.

Turned down. A list of the package's modules in the project file (a Cargo [lib] path, a package.json files list): a second place that has to be kept in step with the folder.

8.2 zeno.package is written in plain words

# What this program is called, and the packages it needs.
package thermostat

needs filters in "../filters"
needs units from "https://github.com/someone/zeno-units" at "v1.2.0"

One line names the package. One line for each package it needs. A comment starts with # and a string is written as in a program, because the file is split into words by Zeno's own lexer.

Why. Decided by Partha on 2026-11-15, from a card with three options and three more in conversation. The file has two kinds of line, and both can be read aloud. It needs no second syntax to learn and no parser to add: Zeno has no dependencies, and a TOML reader would be the first. Minimal punctuation is the rule of the language (section 1), and it would be odd for the first file a newcomer opens to break it.

Turned down. - TOML, zeno.toml (Cargo, Python): well known, but a second syntax, and a parser for all of it to read two kinds of line. - A Zeno program, package.zeno (Swift, Zig): no new syntax, but then the file can compute, and finding out what a program needs means running code. - JSON: no comments, and more punctuation than words. - The address in the import line, import units from "https://..." (Go before modules, Deno): the version is repeated in every file that imports the package, and two files can disagree. - needs lines at the top of main.zeno: a package that is only a library has no main.zeno.

A mistake in a zeno.package is shown as a mistake in any file is, with the file, the line and what to write:

error: this does not say which version of `units` is needed. Add `at` and a tag,
a branch or a commit of that repository, as in `needs units from "..." at "v1.0.0"`
 --> zeno.package:4:1

8.3 A needed package is in a folder, or in a git repository

Line Means
needs filters in "../filters" the package in that folder, used where it is. The folder is counted from the folder of this zeno.package
needs units from "address" at "v1.2.0" a git repository, and a tag, a branch or a commit of it. zeno fetch gets it

in and from are words Zeno already has, and they say which of the two it is. A line with from and no at is refused: see 8.4.

Why a folder at all. A package is written beside the first program that uses it, long before it has a repository of its own. And firmware projects often keep everything in one tree. The folder form makes a package something you can have in the first five minutes.

Why git, and no registry. A registry is a server, accounts, uploads and a name-squatting problem, for a language whose goal is learning by building. Git is on every machine that builds firmware, it already knows how to reach a private repository with the keys that are there, and a commit hash already says exactly what was fetched. So zeno fetch runs git, and Zeno still links nothing.

Turned down. Downloading an archive over HTTPS: Zeno would need a TLS library, its first dependency. A central registry with short names (needs units "1.2"): see above. (Was provisional (11.4): a registry could be added later as a third kind of line. The two here would stay as they are.)

8.4 A version is one exact thing: what stands after at

at "v1.2.0" is a tag, a branch or a commit, looked for in that order. There are no ranges (^1.2, >=1.0), and a zeno.package has no version line of its own.

Why. A range needs a solver, and what the solver picks depends on what was published this morning. Nobody can read the answer off the file. With one exact name after at, the file says what is built. And a version line in the package would be a second place for what the tag already says, with nothing to check that the two agree.

The cost. Two packages that need the same package must name the same version (8.7). When they do not, the message says which lines to make agree, and a person decides. That is the work a solver would hide.

8.5 An import whose first word is a needed package reads that package

import filters              # filters.zeno in the package's folder
import filters.lowpass      # lowpass.zeno in the package's folder
import control              # control.zeno of the program itself

zeno.package says needs filters ..., so in this program an import that begins with filters is about the package. The words after it are a file inside the package's folder, with a dot for a folder as ever. import filters on its own is the package's module of its own name. Every other import is a file of the program, as before.

What is written at the import is still what is written at the use (7.3): filters.lowpass.start(...).

Why the package's name is the first word. The reader of filters.lowpass.start sees which package it comes from, and zeno.package says where that package is. No import has to be looked up in a search path.

Why import filters is filters.zeno. A small package is one file, and its users should write one word. Python's __init__.py and Rust's lib.rs do this with a special file name. Here no name is special: it is the file with the package's name.

Refused, each with what to write. import filters.filters (it is import filters). A file filters.zeno of the program beside a needed package filters: import filters would have two readings.

8.6 Inside a package, an import means the same in every program

The files of the package filters import each other as import lowpass and import filters: files of their own folder. A package reaches another package only if its own zeno.package needs it, and it cannot reach a module of the program that uses it.

This widens 7.2 by one word. A module is looked for from the folder of the package the importing file is in. For a file of the program that is the folder of the program's first file, as it always was.

Why. A package is written once and used by many programs. If an import inside it were looked for from the program's folder, what the package means would depend on who uses it. This is 7.7 for packages. It also means a package can be run on its own: examples/filters has a main.zeno that tries its modules, and they are found the same way.

The program's folder is the folder of its first file. Its zeno.package is looked for there and nowhere else. Turned down: walking up through the folders above until a project file is found (Cargo, npm). What an import means would then depend on a file in a folder you may not know is there.

8.7 A program has one package of each name

If the program needs units and so does filters, they get the same units. Fetched packages all go into one folder, packages/, beside the program's zeno.package, whichever package needs them. Two needs lines for one name that point at different folders, different repositories or different versions are refused, and the message names the lines.

Why. One name is one file (7.2). With two copies of units in one program there would be two classes called units.Length, and a value of one would not be a value of the other, with both printing the same. npm allows this and it is a well known source of puzzles. In firmware it would also put the code in flash twice.

8.8 A module of a package has the package's name in front

In the joined program (7.11) the module lowpass of the package filters is filters.lowpass, and its start is filters.lowpass.start, whoever wrote the import. That is the name a compiler message uses. The package's own files write lowpass.start. The modules of the program itself keep their plain names.

Because one name must be one file and one file one name, three things are refused when the loader meets them:

Met Refused because
two different files that would both be the module units which one is units.x?
one file reached as two modules, filters and lib.filters it would run twice, and its types would exist twice
an import of the file the program starts in it would run twice

8.9 Only zeno fetch uses the network

zeno fetch              in the folder of the program
zeno fetch --update     look every version up again

zeno run, zeno check and zeno build read files and nothing else. A needed package that is not in packages/ is a mistake with a message: "the package units has not been fetched ... Run zeno fetch in the folder of the program".

Why. A build that may or may not download something is two different commands under one name. On a build machine with no network, or with firmware that must be the same next year, the one that downloads has to be a step somebody chose.

Turned down. Fetching on the first zeno run (Cargo, Go). zeno add units ... that edits the project file: the file is two kinds of line, and writing one is the simplest way to say it. zeno new has come since (10.22), and the file it writes shows both kinds of needs line in a comment, so the line to write is in front of whoever needs it.

8.10 zeno.lock pins what was fetched

zeno fetch writes, for each fetched package, the commit it got:

units from "https://github.com/someone/zeno-units" at "v1.2.0" commit "9f8e7d6..."

The next zeno fetch, on this machine or another, gets that commit even if the tag or the branch has moved since. --update looks each at up again and writes the file afresh. The lock is kept with the program. A package in a folder (needs ... in) is not in it: it is used where it is, and there is nothing to pin.

Why. at "main" is allowed (8.4), and a branch moves. Without the lock two people would build two programs from one source. With it, what at says is what you asked for and what the lock says is what you have.

The lock of a needed package is not read: the program's own lock pins everything in the program. One file answers "what exactly is built?".

8.11 A file can import a module and a module below it (revises 7.8)

import filters and import filters.lowpass in one file was refused in 7.8, because filters.lowpass.x could be x of the module filters.lowpass or the field x of a lowpass that the module filters declares. With packages, that pair of imports is the most natural thing to write.

It is now refused only when there really are two readings: when the module filters declares a lowpass of its own. Otherwise filters.lowpass can only be the module. The message, when it comes, names the name that clashes.

The rules, all of them

  1. A package is a folder with a zeno.package, which names it (8.1, 8.2).
  2. A program says what it needs in its own zeno.package: a folder, or a git repository and one exact version (8.3, 8.4).
  3. An import whose first word is a needed package reads a file of that package. Any other import reads a file of the package the importing file is in (8.5, 8.6).
  4. One package of each name in a program, and one name for each module (8.7, 8.8).
  5. zeno fetch fetches. Nothing else does (8.9). zeno.lock remembers what it got (8.10).

9. Editor support

What an editor needs from a language: a formatter, so that every file looks the same, and a language server, so that mistakes show as you type. The walks through the code are in format.md and lsp.md.

Checked against "unambiguous and intuitive". There is one layout and nothing to configure (9.1). Formatting cannot change what a program means, and the formatter proves that to itself on every run (9.2). Each rule of spacing is the one the examples and the documents have used since the first stage, so zeno format surprises nobody who has read Zeno (9.5). The language server shows what zeno check shows, says about a name what the checker knows of it, and answers nothing where it would have to guess (9.10, 9.11).

9.1 One layout, and no options

zeno format main.zeno        write the file again, laid out
zeno format examples         every .zeno file in the folder and below
zeno format --check .        change nothing; say which files are not laid out

There is no setting for the width of an indent, where a brace goes or how many blank lines are allowed.

Why. "One obvious way to write each thing" is the first principle of the language, and layout is where programmers disagree most and gain least. With no options there is nothing to decide per project, no file of settings to copy around, and a diff shows what changed and never how somebody likes their spaces. Go showed that this works.

Turned down. A settings file (clang-format, rustfmt.toml): every project then has its own Zeno.

9.2 The formatter changes white space, and nothing else

It works on the tokens of the file. Every token stays as it was written, in the order it was written, on the line it was written on. Numbers keep their spelling (0x4000_4400), texts keep their quotes.

Why. A formatter that works from the syntax tree has to print every construct of the language, and the tree has already forgotten the comments, the blank lines and how a number was spelled. Each new piece of syntax would need a new piece of printer, and a slip in one would quietly change a program. Working on tokens, the formatter needs to know only which token it is looking at and which came before. It cannot change a meaning, because it never adds, removes or moves a token.

Checked on every run. After laying a file out, the formatter reads its own output back and compares the tokens and the comments with the ones it started from. If they differ it writes nothing and says that the mistake is Zeno's.

A file with a mistake is not formatted. It has to lex and parse. Indentation is counted from brackets that match, and the parser is what knows that they do. The mistakes are shown as zeno check shows them.

9.3 Line breaks are the author's (was provisional: 11.4)

The formatter never joins two lines and never splits one. There is no line width.

Why. case Off { return 0 } on one line and a long block on several are both right, and which is right depends on the code around it. Choosing for the author needs a width limit and a search for the best way to break each line, which is most of the code of prettier and rustfmt, and still produces layouts people fight. A line break in Zeno also ends a statement, so moving one is no longer only layout.

The cost. Two people can break one long call in two ways. If that turns out to matter, a rule for where a too-long line breaks is the thing to add.

9.4 Four spaces for each bracket open, counted in lines

each(items, function(item) {
    print(item)
})

A line is indented once for each line above it that has left a {, ( or [ open. The first line here opens two brackets and the body is still indented once. A line that starts by closing brackets goes back to where the line that opened them is. A statement that goes on after an operator is indented once more:

let total = price +
    tax

Tabs are replaced. Four spaces is what every example has used, and a file that mixes tabs and spaces looks different in every editor.

9.5 Where a space goes

Rule Example
one space around an operator, = and -> a + b, x = 1, -> int
one space after , and :, none before f(a, b), port: int, ["a": 1]
none inside ( ) and [ ] (a + b), [1, 2]
none before the ( of a call or the [ of an index or a type print(x), items[0], list[int]
one space before any other ( or [ if (a or b) and c, return [1, 2]
none around . shapes.area
one inside { } on one line, and before { if ready { go() }, case _ { }
a - that is a sign holds on to its number -1, a - -1, f(-x)
one space between two words 500 ms, not ready

These are the spaces the examples and the documents already had. The proof is a test: every file in examples/ is taken apart into its tokens, put together again with three spaces between each two, and formatted. What comes out is the file as it is in the repository.

How a - is told apart. If the token ahead of it can end a value (a name, a number, a text, ) or ]), it subtracts. Otherwise it is a sign. The same question, "can a value end here?", tells a call's ( from a grouping one and an index's [ from a list's.

9.6 The = that names an argument has no spaces

let here = Point(x=3.0, y=4.0)
function greet(who: str, excited: bool = false, times=1) { }

Inside ( ), an = straight after a name that begins an argument names that argument, and is written tight. Every other = has a space on each side, including a parameter's value after its type.

Why. A call with named arguments reads as a list of pairs, and the tight = keeps each pair together and apart from the next: Point(x=3.0, y=4.0) against Point(x = 3.0, y = 4.0). It also tells naming an argument from assigning to a variable at a glance. Python settled on the same two forms for the same reason.

9.7 At most one blank line

Never two in a row. None at the top of the file or of a block, and none at the bottom of either. A blank line after a comment stays.

Why. One blank line parts two things. A second one says nothing more, and "one or two?" is a choice nobody needs to make.

9.8 Comments

A comment gets a space after its # when a word follows: #note becomes # note. What stands after that is the author's, so a commented-out example keeps its indentation, and #! and #--- are left alone. A comment on a line of its own is indented as code there would be.

A comment after code keeps the distance the author gave it, with at least two spaces. So comments that were lined up stay lined up:

bytes: pointer[u8]    # `size` bytes, asked for with `allocate`
size: int             # how many fit

(Was provisional: 11.4.) The formatter does not line comments up by itself. gofmt does, and needs rules for when a long line should break the column. Keeping the author's distance is simple and never fights a layout somebody made on purpose.

9.9 zeno format writes the file, and --check only looks

zeno format main.zeno changes the file in place and says "Formatted main.zeno", or that it was laid out already. With --check nothing is written, each file that would change is named, and the command fails if there is one: that is the form for a script that guards a repository.

A folder stands for every .zeno file in it and below it, except the folder packages: what zeno fetch put there is somebody else's.

Turned down. Printing the result and leaving the file (gofmt's default): the common wish is to fix the file, and the common wish should be the short command.

9.10 A language server, and it is the zeno program itself

zeno lsp speaks the Language Server Protocol on its standard input and output. An editor starts it and is then told the mistakes in each open file as it is typed, and can ask for a file to be laid out.

Why the protocol. Writing a plugin for each editor is one piece of work per editor, each in that editor's own language. The protocol turns that into one server, which VS Code, Neovim, Helix, Zed, Emacs and others can all use. What is left per editor is a few lines that say "for .zeno files, start zeno lsp", and the colours.

Why inside zeno. The server shows exactly what zeno check shows, because it runs the same loader and the same checker in the same program. A separate server program would be a second thing to build, install and keep at the same version. There is still no library: the protocol is JSON with a length in front, and src/json.rs reads and writes it in 250 lines.

Turned down. A server written in TypeScript on a ready-made library: quick to start, but a second implementation of "what is a mistake in Zeno", which is the one thing that must not exist twice.

9.11 The server answers from the checker, and nothing it has to guess

The editor says The server does
a file was opened, or its text changed checks every open file, and sends the mistakes of each file whose mistakes changed
lay this file out answers with the file as zeno format writes it
where is this name declared? answers with the file and the line of its declaration
what is this name? answers with the name and its type: count: int
the same two about a field, a method, or the name of a module answers "nothing"

A mistake is marked from where the checker says it is to the end of the word there, with the message zeno check prints.

Where the answers about a name come from. To say rightly where a name was declared, the server must know which declaration the name means, with every rule of scope the checker applies: inside function twice(count: int), count is the parameter, and not the count at the top of the file. Guessing from the spelling is right most of the time, and an editor that is wrong some of the time teaches you not to trust it. So the checker, which works this out anyway, now writes it down: for every place a name is used, what it means there, its type, and where it was declared (checker::Use). The server looks the cursor's place up in that record and adds nothing of its own.

For a function the type is shown the way its first line is written, twice: function(count: int) -> int, with the names of the parameters, because Zeno lets a call name them (twice(count=3)).

Why a field gets no answer yet. (Was provisional: 11.4.) The record holds names: variables, parameters, functions, classes, enums, and the names of a module (shapes.area is one name). A field or a method (p.x, queue.put) is found from the type of what stands before the dot, and the checker does not write that down yet. An empty answer is honest, and an editor shows nothing for it. A guess would be shown as if it were known.

Turned down. - Finding the declaration by searching the file for let name or function name. Wrong as soon as two scopes use one name, which is ordinary code. - A second, small scope walker in the server. Two sets of scope rules that have to agree for ever. The checker's are the rules. - Completion and rename now. Completion has to work on a file that does not parse (the line being typed), and the checker never sees such a file. Rename changes many files at once, and needs the uses in files that are not open. Both wait until a real program misses them.

9.12 The editor's text is the program, and its folder is worked out

The server checks what is in the editor, not what is on disk: a file that is open is read from the editor, also when another file imports it, and only a file that is not open is read from disk. So a mistake shows as it is typed, before anything is saved.

zeno check is given the file a program starts in, and that file's folder is the program's (8.6). An editor opens any file, perhaps drivers/uart.zeno. The server takes as the program's folder the nearest folder, from the file's own upward, that has a zeno.package or a main.zeno, and no folder above the one the editor has open. With neither, it is the file's own folder, as for zeno check.

Why this is not the walking up that 8.6 turned down. 8.6 is about what a program means, and that must not depend on a folder above. This is about which program a file on the screen most likely belongs to, so that its imports can be found at all. If the guess is wrong the worst is a mistake shown that zeno check on the real first file would not show, and a zeno.package in the right folder settles it.

Every open file is checked at every change. A change in one file can make or mend a mistake in a file that imports it. Programs are small and the checker is fast, so the simple thing is done: everything open is checked again, and only what changed is sent.

9.13 Colours are per editor, and are not the server's work

The repository has a grammar for VS Code and a syntax file for Vim and Neovim, in editors/. Both know Zeno's words from a list.

Why not from the server. The protocol can carry colours ("semantic tokens"), but an editor asks for them after the file is checked, so the text would flash from plain to coloured. A grammar in the editor colours at once and needs no server at all. (Was provisional (11.4): semantic tokens can be added on top for what a grammar cannot know, such as whether a name is a variable or a function.)

10. What real programs needed

The last stage before the language is called version 0.1. Each section is something a program of more than a few files ran into, and the README's roadmap lists them.

Checked against "unambiguous and intuitive". private is one word with one meaning wherever it stands: only the file it is written in can use this (10.1). There is no second level and no exception to remember. The message for a private name says that it is private and what to change, and never "no such name" (10.2).

An interface is declared with one word, and a class says which interfaces it is with one more (10.4). Whether a class is a Shape is written on its first line and checked there. The class writes each function exactly as the interface does (10.6), so there is one way to write it and nothing to work out. A list of circles is not quietly a list of shapes (10.7), and nothing is cast: a list that is written out is told by the place it goes what it is a list of.

A test is a block with a name, and assert says what must be so (10.16, 10.17). There is one way to write each, a failure shows the two values that were compared, and no test runs by surprise: zeno run and a build go past them.

zeno new does one thing, and it is the same thing every time (10.22): it asks nothing, it has one option, and what it writes is a program you could have typed. A name that will not do is refused with one that will, and is never changed quietly (10.23).

x += y means x = x + y, for every operator and every type, and it means it in the same order in every build (10.25, 10.28). Where that reading would do something nobody meant, a call in the place that would run twice, the statement is refused and the message says what to write (10.26). On a sized number it is the checked conversion, the safe one of the two, and cutting stays a word in the program (10.27). A format on a quantity never loses the unit (10.29).

A dict holds what a list holds (10.11), so there is one answer to "what can I keep in a collection" and not two. The one thing it does not hold is none, because get already means something by it. A loop with two names reads the same over everything that can be looped over: the second name is what is at the first (10.12).

10.1 private: for the file it is written in

private let highest = ["temperature": 60.0]

private function wrapped(place: int, size: int) -> int {
    return place % size
}

class Ring {
    private slots: list[int]
    private count: int

    function size(self) -> int {
        return self.count
    }

    private function slot(self, steps: int) -> int { ... }
}

The word goes in front of a declaration at the top of a file (function, class, enum, let, var, const), and in front of a field or a function of a class. What it marks can be used by the file it is written in, and by no other.

Why at all. 7.4 let every file use everything, and said a word that hides could come later without breaking a program. Later is now: a module of any size has parts that hold only as long as nobody else touches them. A ring's count has to agree with its slots. If any file can write queue.count = 0, the module cannot promise that, and whoever reads it cannot tell what is safe to change.

Why the file is the boundary, for a field as well. In Java and C++ a private field is for the class. In Rust and Go it is for the module. Zeno takes the module, because then the word means one thing in both places it can stand, and "who can use this?" has the same answer everywhere: look at which file you are in. It also fits how Zeno code is written: a function that builds a Ring or prints one sits beside the class in its file, is not a method, and needs the fields. With privacy by class it would need a second word such as C++'s friend.

Why the word private. It is the word Java, C++, C#, Kotlin, Swift and TypeScript use, so it is the one most readers know. It is a whole word (principle 2). pub on what is open (Rust) was turned down in 7.4: a mark on nearly every line. A leading underscore and a capital first letter were turned down there too.

Everything is still open unless it says otherwise. A program in one file never needs the word, and a first module works with no mark on it. Hiding is something a module does once it has something to protect.

An enum is private whole. private enum Mode hides the enum and its cases. A single case cannot be private: a match on the enum has to name every case, so a file that can see the enum must be able to see them all.

Not inside a block. private let x in a function is refused, with the reason: a name declared in a block is seen only in the block already.

Turned down. - Three levels (private, protected, public). protected is about inheritance, which Zeno does not have. Two levels need one word. - Private to the package, so that the files of a package share what other packages cannot see. A real need in a big package, and it can be added as its own word. No program here has needed it. - Checking it when the program runs. It is checked by zeno check, as types are. It is there to keep a program's parts honest with each other, and is not a lock.

10.2 A private name is said to be private

error: `wrapped` is private in the module `ring`: only its own file
can use it. To use it from another file, take the word `private` off
where it is declared

Why not "module ring has no wrapped". That would be the safer thing to say to a stranger and the less useful thing to say to the person who wrote both files, which is who reads it. The name is there, the reader can see it, and a message that denies it sends them looking for a spelling mistake.

When a module has no such name at all, the names it is said to have are the ones another file can use.

10.3 Only its own file builds a class that has a private field

# ring.zeno
function ring(size: int) -> Ring raises ValueError {
    if size < 1 {
        raise ValueError("a ring has room for one byte at least")
    }
    return Ring([0 for slot in range(size)], 0, 0)
}

# main.zeno
let queue = ring.ring(4)          # yes
let mine = ring.Ring([1], 0, 1)   # no

A class gets a constructor for free, which takes a value for each field (3.7). Another file cannot see the private fields, so it cannot fill them in, so it cannot call that constructor. The module writes a function that builds one, and the other files call it.

Why not leave the private fields out of the constructor for other files, so that ring.Ring() fills them from their defaults. Then one class would take different arguments depending on which file the call is in, and the third argument here would be the fifth there. One name, one list of arguments.

Why this is no burden. It is what a module with something to protect wants anyway: ring.ring(4) asks for a size, and how the slots, the first place and the count start is the module's business. This is the rule Rust has for a struct with a private field.

A class whose only private parts are functions is built as ever: there is nothing to fill in.

10.4 interface, and a class says which ones it is

interface Shape {
    function area(self) -> float
    function scale(self, by: float)
}

class Circle is Shape {
    radius: float

    function area(self) -> float {
        return 3.0 * self.radius * self.radius
    }

    function scale(self, by: float) {
        self.radius = self.radius * by
    }
}

function total(shapes: list[Shape]) -> float { ... }

An interface is a list of functions. A class says, on its first line, which interfaces it is, and from then on an object of the class goes wherever one of them is wanted. One function, total, serves every class that is a Shape, the ones written and the ones not written yet.

Why the class has to say so. The other design is that any class with an area and a scale is a Shape without a word (Go, and Python's protocols). It saves one line, and costs two things. A class that happens to have an area meaning something else is a Shape by accident. And a class that was meant to be one and has scale misspelt is found out far away, at some call that passes it, with a message about that call. With is Shape the promise is written where the reader of the class sees it, and it is checked there:

error: `Dot` says it is a `Shape`, and a `Shape` has
`function scale(self, by: float)`. Give `Dot` that function

(Principle 1: found before the program runs, at the place the mistake is. And "unambiguous": whether a class is a Shape is written down, never worked out.)

Why the words interface and is. interface is the word Java, C#, Go and TypeScript use for this, and the word a firmware engineer uses for the thing itself: what a part shows to the rest. trait and protocol mean it only to people who already know those languages. is is already a word of the language, it reads as the sentence it is (class Circle is Shape), and it cannot be taken for the test x is none: one comes after the name of a class being declared, the other between two values. implements says the same in four more letters, and a colon (class Circle: Shape) is punctuation where a word will do (1.4).

A class has no parent class. class B is A where A is a class is refused, and the message says what to do instead: declare what the two share as an interface. Inheriting fields and code brings the questions every language with it has had to answer (which area runs, what the object looks like in memory, what happens when two parents have the same field), and none of them is needed to write total. A class can be several interfaces: class Box is Named, Sized.

10.5 An interface has functions, and nothing about how they are done

No fields, no bodies, self first, and a type on every parameter and on the result.

Why no fields. How a class keeps its data is the class's own business, and in firmware the layout of an object is something its author decides. A field in an interface would decide it for every class. If other code needs the value, the interface has a function that returns it.

Why no bodies. A body in an interface is code that every class gets without writing it, which is inheritance again by another door, with its question of which body runs. With none, the code of a function is in exactly one place: the class.

Why everything is typed. Elsewhere a type that is left out is worked out from the body. Here there is no body. The interface is all a caller has, so all of it is written.

Also refused, each with a message that says so (was provisional: 11.5): a default for a parameter, type parameters on an interface or on one of its functions, an interface that is another interface, and a private function (an interface lists what other code may ask).

10.6 The class writes each function as the interface does

The same names for the parameters, the same types, the same result.

error: `scale` of `Dot` is `function scale(self, factor: float)`, and
`Shape` asks for `function scale(self, by: float)`. Write the two the
same

Why the names count. An argument can be given by name (3.6): shape.scale(by=2.0). Through the interface that has to mean the same for every class, so every class calls it by.

Why the types are the same and not merely fitting. A class could safely take more than the interface asks, or return less. Allowing it means a reader has to work out whether two different first lines agree. The same line twice needs no working out. ("One obvious way to write each thing.")

A class may raise less than its interface says, never more. Whoever calls through the interface knows only what the interface says: function send(self, text: str) -> int raises Full. A class whose send never fills up raises nothing, and that is fine. One that raises something the interface does not list is an error at the class, because the caller could not have been warned. In a firmware build this is also what tells the compiler that a call through the interface carries an error as a value (5.5l).

10.7 A class goes where its interface is wanted, and a list of it does not

A Circle is a Shape in a variable, a parameter, a result, a field, and in each of those with or none. A list[Circle] is not a list[Shape].

Why not the list. Whoever holds it as a list of shapes may append a Square, and the first holder would then find a square in a list of circles. This is the reason a list[u8] is not a list[int] (2.8c), and it is given in the message:

error: `shapes` should be list[Shape], but this is list[Circle].
Whoever holds it as a list of Shape could append another class to it.
Say what the list holds where it is made, as in
`let items: list[Shape] = [...]`

A list that is written out is a list of what the place wants. [Circle(1.0), Square(2.0)] has items of two classes, and the items do not say that they are shapes. The place does: a let with a type, a parameter, a result, a field, an append. So the list is told what it is a list of, and each item is checked against that. Nothing new has to be written, and there is no cast.

The same rule repaired something older. show([1, 2]), where show takes a list[int or none], was accepted, and the compiled program crashed: the list was built as a list of ints and read as a list of int or none, which are kept differently (5.5j). It worked in a let and not in an argument. Now a written list is built as what the place wants wherever it is written.

A generic takes the interface from the place its result goes. let pair: Pair[Shape] = Pair(Circle(1.0), Square(2.0)) is a pair of shapes, and let box: Box[int or none] = Box(count) can hold none later. This holds when each argument goes straight into a T. A list[Circle] given for a list[T] stays a list of circles, for the reason above.

10.8 There is no way back from an interface to a class (was provisional: 11.5)

A Shape cannot be asked which class it is, and it cannot go where a Circle is wanted. Through an interface only the interface's functions are there.

Why. With a way back, code that was given a Shape begins to ask "are you a Circle?", and from then on it works only for the classes it asks about. The interface stops being the whole agreement. For a value that is one of a few known things there is already a better tool: an enum, where match has to name every case (3.11). An interface is for the open set, an enum for the closed one, and each is checked in its own way.

It also keeps one promise simple: what a function is given is all it can use.

What it costs. Now and then a program does know what it has and has to keep the object in a second variable of its own class. If real programs keep running into that, a checked way back (match shape { case Circle as c ... }) can be added without changing anything written before. The message says what to do today.

Two shapes are equal when they are objects of the same class and that class says they are equal. An object prints as the class it really is.

10.9 In a compiled program: a number in the object, and a switch

struct z_Circle {
    int32_t zeno_class;     /* 1: this is a Circle */
    double z_radius;
};

static double z_Shape_area(z_Shape *a0) {
    switch (a0->zeno_class) {
    case 1: return z_Circle_area((z_Circle *)a0);
    default: return z_Square_area((z_Square *)a0);
    }
}

A class that is some interface carries a small number as its first field, which says which class the object is. A call through the interface goes to one function per function of the interface, which looks at the number and calls the function of that class.

Why not a table of pointers to functions, which is how C++, Java and Rust do it. Three reasons, and the first is the one that decides.

  • Zeno compiles a whole program at once (7.11), so every class that is a Shape is known. A switch over a known set is something the C compiler and LLVM can see through: they can put the class's function inline, and they know every place a call can go. A call through a pointer hides both.
  • No pointer to a function sits in memory where a stray write can change where the program jumps. Firmware coding rules (MISRA among them) ask for that, and a tool that works out the deepest the stack can get needs to know every call.
  • It costs four bytes in an object, and nothing in an object of a class that is no interface: such a class is exactly what it was. Rust and Go instead make every reference to an interface two words wide, which would change the size of every slot of every list (5.5h).

What it costs. Adding a class to an interface compiles the switch again, which a whole-program compiler does anyway. And the number is in the object, so a class cannot be made an interface after the fact from outside its own declaration, which 10.4 already rules out.

The interpreter and the VM needed no change at all: they look a method up by its name on the object in front of them, and always did.

To every stage after the parser an interface is a class with no fields whose functions have no bodies. That is why the change is small: the checker fills in what the functions are from their first lines, and the two compilers write the switch where they would have compiled a body.

10.10 What interfaces leave out (was provisional: 11.5)

Each is refused with a message that says so.

  • An owned class that is an interface. In a build with no collector an owned object is freed by its one owner, through code written for its class. Freeing through an interface works (the example does it, and gives every block back), and an ordinary class can be used. What has not been thought through is an owned class whose functions take something out of the object, called through an interface that says nothing about it.
  • A function that takes out or replaces what its object owns cannot stand for one of an interface, for the same reason.
  • Type parameters on an interface or on its functions (interface Stack[T]). Bounds on a generic (T is Ordered, 3.16d) wait for the same thing.
  • An interface that is another interface. A class names both.

A dict whose values are of an interface was on this list, and is in the language since 10.11.

10.11 A value of a dict is anything a list can hold, except none

let readings: dict[str, list[float]] = [:]
let limits = ["voltage": Limit(11.5, 12.5)]
let shapes: dict[str, Shape] = ["round": Circle(1.0), "square": Square(2.0)]
let ranges: dict[str, dict[str, float]] = [:]

A list, an object, a value of an interface, an enum, another dict, a quantity, a function, a sized number. Keys are as they were: a str or an int (6.31).

Why. The first real programs with a dict wanted the readings of each channel under its name, and an object for each name. The way round it (the objects in a list, and the dict holding positions) is two collections kept in step by hand, which is where mistakes come from. And "a list holds anything, a dict holds four types" was a rule to remember with no reason a reader could see.

What is under a key is the thing itself, as it is in a list. readings["voltage"].append(12.0) adds to the list in the dict, and limits["voltage"].high = 13.5 changes the object in the dict. There is no copy that the change could be lost in.

Why not none. get(key) gives none for a key that is not there (6.35). In a dict[str, int or none] a none from get would mean two things, "no such key" and "the key is there and holds none", and no program could tell which.

error: a value of a dict cannot be `int or none`: `get` gives `none` for a
key that is not there, and that could not be told from a value that is
`none`. Make it a `dict[..., int]`, and leave the key out where there is
no value

Leaving the key out says the same thing with what is already there. Inside a value none is as anywhere: dict[str, list[int or none]] is fine.

Turned down. Allowing it and saying "use in to tell them apart": that is the surprise this language is meant not to have. A third answer from get for a missing key: one more thing to check on every call, for a dict nobody has asked for.

In a generic the key and the value can be type parameters: function flip[K, V](d: dict[K, V]) -> dict[V, K]. What K and V are is checked for each copy, with the same rules and the same messages, and the message says which copy it is in.

In a compiled program a value that is not a plain one is a pointer in the dict's list of values. How such a value is shown and compared depends on its type, so the compiler writes a function for each, as it does for a list of them, and hands it to the runtime (zeno_dict_str_with, zeno_dict_eq_with).

10.12 for key, value in ages: a loop with two names

for name, age in ages {
    print(name, age)
}
for at, word in words {
    print(at, word)
}

Over a dict the loop is given each key and what is under it. Over a list, text or a range it is given the position, counted from 0, and what is there.

One rule for all of them: the second name is what is at the first. In a dict age is ages[name], and in a list word is words[at]. Nothing else has to be remembered, and it is why the position comes first: it is the key of a list.

Why. for name in ages and then ages[name] on the next line is what every loop over a dict did, and it is a lookup the loop has already done. For a list the position was a var counted by hand beside the loop, or for i in range(len(words)) and words[i]: two ways, both longer than what they mean.

Over a range the first name counts the trips: for trip, n in range(10, 0, -4) gives 0 10, 1 6, 2 2. That is the position of the number in the list the range would be.

The pairs are as they were when the loop began, like the keys of the loop with one name (6.38). The body may store into the dict and take keys out.

Each name is the loop's own, cannot be changed, and is gone after the loop, as the one name is. With no collector the value is looked at, and the dict is whole again after the loop.

Turned down. - for (name, age) in ages, or a pair as a value of its own: Zeno has no tuple, and a new kind of value for one loop is more than the loop needs. - for name, age in ages.items() and for at, word in enumerate(words), as in Python: two more names to learn, and each builds a list of pairs that the language has no type for. - The item first and the position second over a list: then the two names mean one thing over a dict and the other way round over a list. - Three names, or two that are the same, are refused with a message that says what two names are for.

10.13 A dict that is written out is a dict of what the place wants

let shapes: dict[str, Shape] = ["round": Circle(1.0), "square": Square(2.0)]
let bytes: dict[str, u8] = ["start": 200, "stop": 7]

The same rule as for a list (10.7): the place says what the values are, and each written value is checked against that. A dict that is made already stays what it is, so a dict[str, Circle] is not a dict[str, Shape], for the reason a list of circles is not a list of shapes: a Square could be stored into it through the other name.

With no type to go by, all the values are one type, and a mix says so. A message for a number names the fix:

error: the values of this dict are float here, but this one is int.
Write a number with a decimal point, as in `1.0`, and convert a value with `float( )`

10.14 With no collector a dict holds plain values (was provisional: 11.5)

error: with no collector a value of a dict is an int, a float, a bool or
a str for now, and this is list[int]. Keep those in a list, and the
position of each in the dict

In a build with no collector a value of a dict is an int, a float, a bool, a str, a sized number or a quantity: the values that are copied when they are stored. Lists, objects and the rest need the collector.

Why. With no collector everything has one owner (5.15). A dict of lists would own its lists, and each way into the dict needs a rule: d[key] would lend, d[key] = value would free the old value, pop would hand its value over, clear and the end of the dict would free every one, values() could not give a second list of them, and get(key, instead) would have to say who owns instead. Those are the rules of a list of owned items (5.15e) written out again for a second collection, and so far no program for a board has needed them: such programs keep a few objects in a list or in fields, and a dict for looking a number or a name up.

Meanwhile. The objects go in a list, and the dict holds the position of each: channels[index["voltage"]].

When this changes. When a program that has to run with no collector needs it. The rules above are the plan.

10.15 print makes its texts in the order they are written

Not a rule of the language, but a repair that this stage found. The C for print(table, x) made the text of each value inside the call of zeno_print, and C does not say in which order the arguments of a call are worked out. Making the text of a list, a dict or an object takes room for its pieces while it is made. So how much of the text room was in use at most depended on the C compiler, and the report of a build with no collector differed by a few bytes between the C and the LLVM backend, which 5.11t says must not happen.

Where a print has two or more values with work to do, and one of them is a list, a dict, an object, a call or text that is made there, the work is now done on lines of its own, in the order the LLVM backend does it: every value first, and then the text of each, as written. Numbers alone stay inside the call, since their text takes no room while it is made. What is printed never differed, and does not change.

10.16 A test is test "what it checks" { ... }, at the top of a file

test "a full ring keeps what it has" {
    let queue = ring(2)
    assert queue.put(1)
    assert queue.put(2)
    assert not queue.put(3)
}

A block with a name. The name is plain text in quotes, so it can say what is checked in ordinary words, and it is what a failure is reported under. Two tests of one file cannot have the same name.

Where. At the top of a file, and in any file. A test that stands in the file it tests sees what is private to that file (10.1), which is where the things worth testing usually are. A file of tests alone (ring_test.zeno, which imports ring) is the same thing with nothing else in it.

What a test is to the rest. A block of its own: what it declares is gone when it ends. An error that leaves it fails that test, so a test needs no try around what should work. It is checked with the program, by zeno check and zeno run, so a test cannot go stale unseen. It is run only by zeno test.

test is not a reserved word. test " at the start of a statement can be nothing else, since a name followed by text means nothing in Zeno. Everywhere else test is a name, and programs use it as one: examples/generics.zeno has a parameter called test. owned, take and strict are reserved (5.19c) because each stands where a name could, and here no name could.

Turned down. - Functions whose names begin with test_, as in Python and Go: a name that changes what a function is for is a rule nobody can see in the program, and names with spaces say more. - A mark above a function, @test: Zeno has no marks, and one kind of them for one use is a new piece of syntax to learn. - Tests only in files of their own: then nothing private could be tested, or private would need an exception for test files. - Reserving test: it takes a common name away and settles nothing.

10.17 assert says what must be so, and a failure shows both sides

FAILED: a push is counted
error: this is false. The left of `==` is 1, and the right is 2
  --> ring.zeno:33:12
   |
33 |     assert length(r) == 2
   |            ^

assert and a condition. If the condition is a comparison (==, !=, <, <=, >, >=, in, not in, is, is not), a failure says what was on each side, shown as print would show it inside a list, so that text has its quotes.

Why one statement. Other languages grow a family: assert_eq, assert_ne, assertTrue, assertIn. Each exists so that a failure can show the values. The interpreter has the comparison in front of it, so it can show them for the plain assert, and there is one thing to learn.

A failed assert ends its test, and nothing catches it. It is not an error of the program, so except Error does not see it. A test that expects an error can then be written without care for what its own asserts raise.

Only in a test. In the program itself, if and raise say what is wrong and to whom, and can be caught. An assert that vanishes from a build, or that stops a board, would be a second way to report a mistake with rules of its own. The word is reserved, so that assert (a or b) is never read as a call.

Turned down. A message after the condition (assert x > 0, "..."): the test has a name and the failure shows the line and the values. It can be added without changing anything here.

10.18 assert raises ValueError { ... }: the block must raise that

test "a ring of no size is refused" {
    assert raises ValueError {
        ring(0)
    }
}

The test goes on if the block raises an error of that kind, or of a kind that is one (Error takes any). It fails if the block raises nothing, or raises something else, and says which.

Why a form of its own. Fault paths are half of what firmware has to get right, so tests for them should be the easy ones to write. Without this it is try, the call, an assert false that says nothing when it fails, and an except. raises is the word a function's first line already uses for the same thing.

10.19 zeno test runs a file and then the tests written in it

$ zeno test examples/ring
examples/ring/ring.zeno
  ok      a new ring is empty
  ok      bytes come out in the order they went in
  ok      a full ring keeps what it has
  ok      the ring goes round
  ok      a ring of no size is refused

5 tests: 5 passed, 0 failed.

zeno test ring.zeno runs the file as zeno run would, and then each test in it, in the order written. zeno test folder does that for every file in the folder and below it that has a test, and zeno test alone means the folder it is run in. The command fails (for a script or a CI job) if any test failed or any file could not be run.

Each file is tested as a program of its own. Its tests start from the state the file leaves, and the tests of two files cannot disturb each other. A file with no test in it is not run at all, so main.zeno does not blink its LED because its folder was tested.

The tests of an imported file are not run when the file that imports it is tested. They are that file's own, and run when it is tested. So a test runs once, however many files import its module.

Where a file's imports are looked for. In the folder of its program: the nearest folder, from the file's own upward, with a zeno.package or a main.zeno in it. That is the rule the language server has (9.12), and it is what lets drivers/uart.zeno be tested with the import sensors it has in the real program.

Tests of one file share what the file declares. A test that changes a variable of the file leaves it changed for the next. That is what a var at the top of a file is, and a test that needs a fresh one makes it inside itself.

The tests run in zeno run's engine. The compiler's own tests hold every engine and build to the same output, so a second run of a program's tests in the VM or in C would test Zeno and not the program.

10.20 A build leaves the tests out

zeno build, with any of its options, takes the tests out before the program is checked. They are not in the C, the IR or the program, so a board pays nothing for them. And they are not held to the rules of the build: a test may share a list, or use a dict of lists, in a program that is built with no collector. zeno check --no-collector checks what that build would build, so it leaves them out too. zeno run and plain zeno check check them.

Why. A test is run on the computer where the program is written. Asking it to follow the rules of a board would make tests harder to write than the code they test, and fewer would be written.

10.21 What tests leave out (was provisional: 11.5)

  • A message on assert (10.17).
  • assert in a function outside a test. A helper gives back a bool and the test asserts it.
  • Lines to run before and after each test. A function that builds what the tests need does that today.
  • Choosing tests by name on the command line, and running them side by side.
  • Running the tests of a compiled program, on a board or in the VM.

10.22 zeno new blinker makes a program that runs and has a test

zeno new blinker

makes the folder blinker, and three files in it:

File What it is
zeno.package package blinker, under a comment that shows the two kinds of needs line
main.zeno imports greeting and prints what greeting.hello("world") gives
greeting.zeno the function hello, and one test of it

Then it says what it made, and the two commands to try: zeno run blinker/main.zeno and zeno test blinker.

Why these three. Whoever runs zeno new wants to see something run, and then wants to know where things go. A lone main.zeno with a print in it answers the first and not the second, and nobody needs a command to write one line. The three files are the three things a program of more than one file is made of: the file it starts in (7.2), a module that is imported (7.1), and the project file (8.1). The test is there so that zeno test works in the first minute, and so that the first test a person writes has one to copy that stands where tests stand (10.16).

Why it is so small. What is written is read once and then changed. Every line more is a line to delete. Each file starts with a comment that says what the file is, and that is all the teaching there is room for.

It is checked. The compiler's own tests make a program and a package with zeno new, check them, run them, run their tests and compare each file with what zeno format would write. What zeno new writes cannot fall behind the language without a test failing.

Turned down.

Idea Why not
Templates: zeno new --template firmware Several starting points means choosing one before knowing the language, and each one is a program that has to be kept right. A program for a board differs in what main.zeno says, and board.md says it
Questions at the prompt (name? licence? author?), as npm init asks The command would have two ways to be run, and a script could not run it. The name is the one thing needed, and it is on the command line
git init and a .gitignore, as Cargo does Whether a program is in git, and in which repository, is not the compiler's to decide. A folder made inside a repository would get a second one
A README.md, a licence, a src/ folder Not Zeno's files. The folder of a program is the folder its main.zeno is in (7.2), so a src/ would be a second answer to "where does the program start"
zeno init, into the folder you are in See 10.23
zeno add units ... 8.9: a needs line is one line, and the new zeno.package shows it

10.23 The name is a Zeno name, and a folder that is there is left alone

The last part of the path is the name: of the folder, of the program on the package line, and, for a package, of the module import reads. So it has to be a name of the language. These are refused:

zeno new ... Why
my-app, 2fast not a name: "A name is letters, digits and _, and does not start with a digit ... Try my_app"
while a word of the language
math a module that comes with Zeno, which an import math already means
a path where something already is "zeno new makes a new folder, and writes nothing into one that is there"

Why refuse and not repair. Cargo takes my-app, keeps it as the package's name and calls the crate my_app, so one thing has two names from its first day. Here the folder, the package line and the import say the same word. The message gives the word to use, so the repair is one more try.

Why nothing is written into a folder that is there. A command that makes files must never be able to replace one. If the folder is there, either it has work in it or the name was a slip, and in neither case is writing into it what was wanted. Checking each file instead ("write main.zeno unless there is one") leaves a folder that is half what was there and half new, which nobody asked for.

Turned down. --force. zeno init for a folder that is there: the files are three, and in a folder with work in it the right ones to add depend on the work.

10.24 zeno new --package filters makes a package

zeno new --package filters

makes filters/zeno.package and filters/filters.zeno: the module with the package's own name, which is the file import filters reads (8.5), with the same hello and the same test. There is no main.zeno, because a package is not run. The comment in its zeno.package shows the line a program writes to need it.

Why an option, and why this word. A package and a program differ in which files they start with, and in nothing else, so one command with one option says it. The option is the word the language already uses for the thing: package is the first line of the file that makes a folder one.

Turned down. zeno new package filters: then zeno new package would have two readings, a program called package or a package with no name. --lib, Cargo's word: Zeno has no libraries, it has packages. Writing both a main.zeno and the named module into every new folder: a program would start with a file it does not use.

10.25 x += y is x = x + y: what is there is read first

var count = 1

function bump() -> int {
    count = 10
    return 1
}

count += bump()     # 2

count is read, then bump() runs, then the two are added and the sum is stored. It is the order of count = count + bump(), which is left to right like every other expression.

What was wrong. This program printed 2 when it was built through C, and 11 in the interpreter, in the VM and when it was built through LLVM: those three worked the value out first and read count afterwards. No test had a value that changes the place it is added to, so the four engines had never been asked. It was found while += on a sized number was being made (10.27), and it was mended first.

Why this order. x += y is explained everywhere in Zeno as "x = x + y" (5.8k), and now it is that, instruction for instruction: the VM's code for the two statements is the same four instructions. It is also the answer of Python, Java, JavaScript and C#. The other order has nothing to explain it by.

Turned down. Leaving it to each engine, as C leaves it to each compiler: a program must do the same thing however it is run.

10.26 The place of a += has no call in it

xs[next()] += 5
error: `+=` reads this place and then writes it, so a call in the
       place would run twice. Keep what the call gives in a `let`
       first, and use that name here
let position = next()
xs[position] += 5

What was wrong. x += y is x = x + y, so xs[next()] += 5 was xs[next()] = xs[next()] + 5: two calls. With a next that counts, it read item 1 and wrote item 2. Every engine did this alike, so no test caught it, and nobody who writes that line means it.

Why refuse. There are two things the line could mean, one call or two, and the one that follows from the rule (two) is the one nobody wants. A line with two readings is refused, and the message gives the line that has one. The let is what a compiler would have to add in secret to call once. Here it is in the program, where the reader can see which value was kept.

It can be lifted. If working the place out once turns out to be wanted, allowing it later changes no program that is accepted today. Keeping the two calls would have been the choice that cannot be undone.

Cost. xs[len(xs) - 1] += 1 needs a let too, though len changes nothing. The rule is "no call", with no list of calls that are harmless to remember, and a function of the program could never be on such a list.

Turned down.

Idea Why not
Work the place out once, with a hidden variable (C, Python, Rust) Right, and four engines each have to do it and be kept alike. The let costs one line and is visible
Allow calls that change nothing, such as len A list to learn, and the checker cannot know it of a function the program wrote
Leave it: the rule says two calls The rule is for reading programs, and this reading is a trap

10.27 count += 1 on a sized number is count = u8(count + 1)

var count: u8 = 250
count += 5      # 255
count += 1      # OverflowError: 256 does not fit in a u8 (0 to 255)

The sum is an int, as 2.8a says every sum is. Then it is stored through the checked conversion (2.8b): it goes in if it fits, and it is an OverflowError if it does not. This is so for every operator with an = form, and for every place a sized number is kept: a variable, a field, an item of a list[u8].

flags |= 0x80                       # always fits
left -= 3                           # an error below 0, for a u8
count = u8.wrap(count + 1)          # going round is still said in a word

Why. 2.8a made the place a number is stored the one place it may not fit, "where the program has to say what it wants". count += 1 names the place and what goes into it. The only thing it leaves out is whether to check or to cut, and 2.8b already answers that: the short spelling is the safe one, and cutting is a word that a reviewer can search for. So the shortest spelling of all is the check. A counter that passes 255 without anybody deciding it should is a bug in most programs, and the ones that mean it say wrap.

Before this, the most common thing done to a counter needed the longest line, count = u8(count + 1), and the first programs with a u8 in them ran into it.

/= is still refused on a sized number, as on an int: / gives a float. //= is the one that divides whole numbers (10.28).

How it is built. The checker is the only stage that knows count is a u8. It notes each such statement, and checker::checked rewrites it into the long form before anything runs or compiles it (Program::with_fits). The interpreter, the VM and both compilers only ever see count = u8(count + 1), which they could all do already, so there is nothing new that four engines must do alike. zeno parse and zeno format show the program as it is written.

Turned down.

Idea Why not
+= cuts at the size, as in C 250 + 10 is quietly 4. That is the bug 2.2 and 2.8a exist to stop
Keep refusing it It refused the line everybody writes, to protect a choice that 2.8b had already made
