A second operator for cutting, such as Zig's +%= New punctuation where a word, wrap, is there already
Teach each engine the check Four pieces of code to keep alike, for what one rewrite says once

10.28 Every operator on two numbers has an = form

+=, -=, *=, /=, //=, %=, **=, &=, |=, ^=, <<=, >>=. Each is x = x op y.

//=, %= and **= were missing. count //= 2 was "expected an expression, found =", and /= on a whole number can never be right, because / gives a float. Halving an int in place had no short spelling at all.

Why. The rule is now one sentence with no exceptions to remember. Whoever knows // and += will write //= without looking it up, and it should work.

Left out. and= and or=: they are words, they work on bool, and nobody expects them. = forms of the comparisons mean nothing.

10.29 A format on a quantity is the number's, and the unit stays

let angle = 30.456 deg
let supply = 3.3 V
print(f"{angle:.1f}")        # 30.5 deg
print(f"[{supply:8.2f}]")    # [  3.30 V]

A quantity takes the formats a float takes. The decimals are the number's. The unit follows it after a space, as it does whenever a quantity is shown. The width counts both, and a quantity lines up on the right, as numbers do.

Before, f"{angle:.1f}" was refused (".1f needs a number"), and the way round it was f"{angle / 1 deg:.1f} deg": the unit taken off by hand and written back as text, where nothing checks that the two agree.

Why the unit stays. A quantity that prints as 30.5 has lost the one thing that made it a quantity, at the moment it reaches a person. Whoever wants the bare number divides by one of the unit (angle / 1 deg), which says which unit the number is in.

Why the width counts the unit. "At least this many characters" should be true of what is printed. With it, a column of quantities ends at the same place whatever their units are, when the width is enough for the longest.

Changed with it. f"{wait:10}", a width and nothing else, was already allowed, and laid the quantity out as text, on the left. It is on the right now, like f"{n:10}" for a number. x, b and d are still refused: a quantity is not a whole number.

Turned down. Dropping the unit when there is a format. A second format for the unit ({angle:.1f:unit}): there is nothing to choose about a unit except whether it is there, and it always is.

11. Version 0.1: every provisional decision, read again

A decision was marked provisional when it was made on less than it deserved: before a later stage existed, before a real program had tried it, or to keep a stage small. The last thing on the list for version 0.1 was to read each one again and say what it is now. There were about eighty. This chapter has every one of them.

Each has one of three answers.

  • Settled. It is the rule, and it is no longer waiting for anything. The reason says what was learned since.
  • A limit of 0.1. The thing that was left out stays out of this version. It is a known edge of the language and not an open question about it: the program is refused with a message that says so, or the page that describes the feature says what to write instead. The reason names what would bring it in.
  • Changed. It is not as it was. The row says which decision changed it.

A mark in the chapters above now reads "was provisional", and points here. The text around it is left as it was written, because the reasons a thing was once uncertain are worth keeping.

How each was judged. By the two tests every decision has had since 2026-11-14. Is the rule one sentence that a reader can hold, with no second reading? And where something is left out, does Zeno say so in plain words, with what to write instead? A provisional decision that passes both is settled. A thing left out that would need a new rule is a limit, and adding it later must not change what any program that is accepted today means. Nothing on the lists below fails that last test, which is why none of them had to be decided in a hurry.

One limit is silent, and it is stated here because the rest are not. In a program that is not strict, a value whose type is not known until the program runs can be put where a u8 is wanted, and nothing checks it, just as it can be put where an int is wanted. --strict, a file that begins with strict, and every build check it. (5.13, sized-ints.md.)

11.1 The language: chapters 0 to 3

Decision What was left open Now The reason
0.1, 3.7 Classes have no inheritance Settled: there is none Interfaces (10.4) give what inheritance is mostly used for, one function that serves many classes. What they leave out is a class whose fields and behaviour are written in another class, and that is the part that makes a class hard to read. A class in Zeno is read in one place
1.13 General unions, int or str A limit of 0.1 T or none and an enum whose cases carry values cover the uses, and each has one way to be taken apart (is none, match). A union would be a second way to write an enum
1.15 Derived units (V * A is W), and units a program declares A limit of 0.1 Two quantities cannot be multiplied, and the message says so. Doing it right is an algebra of dimensions, which is a feature of its own size
2.8c A u8 could go where a u16 is wanted without u16( ) Settled: it is written "A sized number is an int for free, and everything else is written" is one rule. The loosened rule is a table of thirty pairs (does a u8 fit an i8? an i8 a u16?) that every reader would have to know
2.8d No u64 A limit of 0.1 Half its values do not fit in an int, so "the answer of an operator is an int" (2.8a) would stop being true. It needs its own answer to what u64 + u64 is
3.6a A shorter way to write a one-line function Settled: there is one way to write a function Comprehensions and a named function handed to map cover the short cases in every example. A second syntax for the same thing is what principle 4 is against
3.7 No constructor written by the program Settled: a function builds one The fields, in order, are the constructor. A class that must check what it is given keeps a field private and offers a function with a name (ring.ring(4), 10.3), which says what it does better than a constructor could
3.9 let fixes the name, not what it names Settled It is one sentence: the name always means the same thing. It is what final means in Java and const in JavaScript, so it is what most readers expect. A list that nobody may change would be a second kind of list, and no program has asked for one
3.10 Lists and objects are shared Changed, long ago: kept, with a collector to make it safe (5.7), and with one owner where there is none (5.15)
3.10a The copy a for loop takes could be skipped Settled as it is The loop sees the list as it was when it began, always. The copy is given back when the loop ends (5.8j). Skipping it where the body cannot touch the list would change no program, so it is not a question about the language
3.12 A shared variable that was narrowed is looked at again as it is used Settled The other rule, Kotlin's, refuses to narrow such a variable at all, which makes if port is not none useless for every var at the top of a file. The second look is one comparison, made only for variables that something else can reach
3.14 upper, lower, strip and split know plain letters and blanks Settled for the language The full rules need tables of tens of kilobytes, and a method must do the same on a board as on a computer. They belong in a module for programs with the room
3.15b One LED and numbered pins, and no inputs A limit of 0.1, and the one item taken off the list for it Reading a pin, an ADC or a UART is designed against a real board. A design made without one would be a guess, and the simulated board would agree with whatever was guessed
3.16d A generic has no bounds: its body is checked for each type it is used with A limit of 0.1 The error is in the generic, with a note that names the copy and the call. Bounds (T is Ordered) wait for interfaces that have type parameters (10.10), since a bound is an interface
3.16e Generic enums, a method with type parameters of its own, a generic kept as a value, an owned value as a type argument A limit of 0.1 Each is refused with a message that says so

11.2 How it is built: chapter 5

Decision What was left open Now The reason
5.5c A value of unknown type in a compiled program Settled: a build is strict Types that are not written are worked out, so most programs with none written build as they are. One that truly needs a value of any type runs with zeno run
5.5e Checked arithmetic uses built-ins of GCC and Clang A limit of 0.1 They cover the toolchains for ARM, RISC-V, AVR and ESP. Another C compiler needs the checks written out, in one place in runtime.c
5.5g An object that never leaves its function could live on the stack Settled as it is It would be an optimisation that changes no program. With no collector the object is freed where its owner ends in any case
5.5k A function value is always a block, even one that keeps no variables Settled Two function values are equal only when they are the same one, in every engine. The fixed value would save a block and break that for a function written in a loop
5.5l A function that can raise cannot be used as a value in a firmware build A limit of 0.1 The type function(str) -> int does not say whether it raises, and the two sorts are called differently there. The way in is raises in a function type
5.7 Three things about the collector Changed, twice, and settled once A build with no heap at all exists: --no-collector (5.9). It takes its memory from one fixed pool (5.9h). Counting references was not needed: a program that cannot pause is built with no collector, and one that can keeps the collector as it is
5.8a, 5.19a No mark at the call for a value that is taken (send(take p)) Settled The function's own header says take (5.19b), and a use after it is an error that names the line of the call. One way to write a call was worth more than a second reminder
5.8c, 5.15b Whether a plain parameter takes or looks Changed in 5.19a: a plain parameter looks, and take takes The count asked for in 5.15b was made in the audit: most parameters only look
5.8i What an owned value cannot do: sit in an or none, an enum case or a function type, be moved out of a field, be replaced through a borrow, be matched, be raised A limit of 0.1 Each is an error that says what to do instead, and allowing any of them later breaks no program
5.9d raise e, to pass a caught error on, is refused with no collector A limit of 0.1 The except that caught it frees it. Raise a new error, or lend the caught one to a function
5.9g, 5.15j, 5.16i, 5.17g What a build with no collector refuses A limit of 0.1. The list is the one in 5.17g, with 5.16h and 10.14 Two of them are the design and not a gap: a function that shares a var with the function around it, and keeping a function that only looks. The rest each need a rule for one more kind of owner
5.9h The pool does not join spare blocks back together Settled Sizes double, so a spare block is used again by the next thing of its size. Joining is three times the code, and no example is short of memory for the lack of it
5.9j The pool's size is one number in the C, and not an option of zeno build A limit of 0.1 --memory prints the number to change it to. --pool N is an exercise in pool.md
5.9k Spare blocks are not poisoned in a checking build A limit of 0.1 A block given back twice is caught already. The rest is an exercise in the same page
5.9l With no collector text is kept by copying it, not by counting who holds it Settled A count would keep text alive by a route no line of the program shows, which is what this build exists to rule out
5.9o Text read from a place a function can change is copied even when nothing is called Settled "Read from a place, it is copied" is one rule. Whether a copy is made should not depend on what else is on the line
5.9p The copy a for loop takes of a list of text is deep Settled The body may replace an item while the loop still holds it
5.9s A plain list parameter could not be given a list that was just made Changed in 5.15: a list has an owner in this build, and since 5.19a the parameter looks at it for the length of the call
5.9w for ch in text makes a list of the characters Settled It is a made list like any other, and it is freed when the loop ends. Counting with range(len(text)) is there for the program that cannot spare the blocks
5.10c A bytecode instruction is a Rust enum, not bytes Settled The VM is there to be read and to check the interpreter against. Speed is what the two compilers are for
5.10g zeno run uses the interpreter unless told --vm Settled It is the plainest statement of what a program means, it is the engine zeno test uses, and every other engine is tested against it
5.11d The LLVM build's runtime is the C build's, with static taken off Settled One runtime means one place for a mistake to be, and the test that the two builds use memory alike depends on it
5.11i A list item crosses into the runtime as an i64 Settled One list type in the runtime (5.5h) is what both builds share
5.11l _setjmp is the name on Linux and macOS A limit of 0.1 Those are the systems Zeno is built and tested on. A firmware build has no setjmp at all
5.11o In --llvm --firmware an error comes back through a pointer and not as a pair Settled until a board says otherwise A pointer is the same on every processor, and LLVM removes it for small functions
5.11p The drop of a list that is certainly empty stays a call Settled as it is It is a call that does nothing, and removing it changes no program. It is an exercise in llvm.md
5.13a Only a field of an object is stored small. A list[u8] takes eight bytes an item A limit of 0.1 The runtime has one list type. A packed list of bytes is the first thing a real driver will ask for, and is a stage of its own
5.14c Two copies of a generic that would get the same C name are refused Settled Names that could never clash would be unreadable, and reading the C is half the point of that build
5.15e A method cannot take out what its object owns while the object is lent A limit of 0.1 The four rules of 5.8d stay four. The smallest growth is written in that section
5.16h list[int or none] with no collector A limit of 0.1 A number that may be none sits in a block of its own inside a list, and those blocks have no owner yet
5.17d A function that uses another function value which has an owner cannot be returned A limit of 0.1 "compose" needs a way to take a value into a function
5.18d allocate that finds no memory stops the program Settled for 0.1 Giving back none would put a check on every allocate for a case most programs cannot recover from. Firmware that must survive it will want pointer[u8] or none

11.3 The standard library: chapter 6

Decision What was left open Now The reason
6.6 sort() by a function the program gives A limit of 0.1 Numbers and text have one order that nobody has to be told. Tasks could be ordered by name or by priority, and a sort() that picked one would be a guess
6.8 char(0) is refused Settled In a compiled program text ends at the first zero byte. A byte that may be zero belongs in a list[u8]
6.10 The methods of text and lists are written in Rust and C, not in Zeno Settled for 0.1 Each is written once for the interpreter and once for the runtime, and the tests hold the two together. A library in Zeno is the natural next thing after 0.1, and 7.2 waits for it
6.12, 6.20 A file is read whole. One too large to hold cannot be read in pieces A limit of 0.1 Reading in pieces needs an open file, which is a value with an owner and a moment at which it closes
6.14 One kind of error for a file, FileError Settled The message says which it was, for a person. A program that must tell them apart asks first, with files.exists
6.16 A file holds text. There is no files.read_bytes A limit of 0.1 It gives a list[u8], and is worth having when that list is packed (5.13a)
6.19 No program.exit(code), and no environment variables A limit of 0.1 A program ends by reaching its end, or with an error nobody caught, which ends it with status 1
6.21 hypot, the hyperbolic functions, gcd, clamp, and random numbers A limit of 0.1 Each is a few lines of Zeno today. Random numbers want a module of their own, and on a board a source of them
6.24 asin, acos, atan and atan2 give a quantity in rad Settled A float that is secretly an angle is what units are there to stop. Since 10.29 it prints with any number of decimals, which was the one thing that made it awkward
6.24 atan2 takes plain numbers, not two lengths A limit of 0.1 Divide each by one of its unit first
6.32 A dict held only ints, floats, bools and text Changed in 10.11: it holds what a list holds

11.4 Files, packages and editors: chapters 7 to 9

Decision What was left open Now The reason
7.2 The five modules that come with Zeno keep their names Settled zeno new refuses those names for a program (10.23), so the rule is met on the first day and not by an import that finds the wrong thing
7.3 No import drivers.uart as uart Settled One module has one name. from drivers.uart import send gives the short form of what is used, and since this pass an as after an import gets a message that says so
7.4 Everything at the top of a file could be imported Changed in 10.1: private
7.11 The files of a program are joined and compiled together A limit of 0.1 Compiling each file by itself needs a written description of what every module offers. It is what a large program will want, and no program is that large yet
8.3 No registry of packages with short names Settled for 0.1 A package is a folder or a git address. A registry could be added as a third kind of needs line, and the two that exist would stay as they are
8.9 No zeno new, no zeno add Changed in 10.22: zeno new. zeno add is settled: there is none A needs line is one line, and a new zeno.package shows both kinds
9.3 The formatter never joins or splits a line Settled Where a line breaks is the one thing about layout that carries the author's meaning. Everything else has one answer
9.8 The formatter does not line comments up Settled It keeps the distance the author chose, and never fights a column somebody made on purpose
9.11 Hover on a field or a method (p.x, queue.put) gives no answer A limit of 0.1 The checker notes names, and does not yet note what a dot found. An empty answer is better than a guess
9.13 Colours come from a grammar in the editor, not from the server Settled The text is coloured at once and needs no server. Colours from the server could be added on top without changing either

11.5 What real programs needed: chapter 10

Decision What was left open Now The reason
10.5 A function of an interface has no default for a parameter, no type parameters, and cannot be private A limit of 0.1 Each is refused with a message. The last is settled: an interface lists what other code may ask
10.8 No way back from an interface to a class Settled A function that needs to know the class is a function the interface should have. Asking "which class are you?" is how code that was written against an interface comes to depend on every class behind it
10.10 An owned class as an interface, a function that takes out of its object as one of an interface, type parameters on an interface, an interface that is another interface A limit of 0.1 Each is refused with a message. The first two are the same question about who frees what, and need the same answer
10.14 With no collector a dict holds ints, floats, bools, text, sized numbers and quantities A limit of 0.1 A list or an object under a key needs the dict to be its owner, as a list is of its items (5.15c). That is one more owner, built the same way
10.21 No message on assert, no assert outside a test, nothing run before each test, no choosing tests by name, no tests in a compiled program A limit of 0.1 The failure already shows both sides of the comparison (10.17), which is what a message is usually written to say

11.6 What 0.1 is, then

The language is what chapters 0 to 10 decide, with the limits in the tables above, and nothing in it is waiting to be decided.

Everything marked "a limit of 0.1" is one list, and it is the list for whatever comes after: inputs on a board, a packed list[u8], the last few things a build with no collector refuses, interfaces with type parameters and the bounds that come with them, a file read in pieces, and a library written in Zeno. Each can be added without changing what a program written today means, because each is refused today and not quietly given some other meaning.

12. Releases

The walkthrough is release.md.

12.1 A release is a tag, and a machine builds it

git tag v0.1.0
git push origin v0.1.0

Pushing a tag starts .github/workflows/release.yml, which runs the tests, builds zeno for every kind of machine, checks each build, packs it and makes the release. Nobody builds a released zeno by hand.

Why. A release built by hand depends on what was on that machine that day, and nobody can say afterwards what that was. Built from a tag, a release is exactly the source at that tag, built the same way each time, and the file that says how is in the repository beside the source.

The tag and Cargo.toml must agree. The tag is v and the version in Cargo.toml, and the workflow stops if they differ. Otherwise a zeno that prints 0.1.0 could be on a page that says 0.2.0, and the first question about any problem ("which version?") would have two answers.

All or nothing. If the build for one kind of machine fails, no release is made. A table with a hole in it tells somebody with that machine that Zeno is not for them, when the truth is that a build broke.

Started by hand, it releases nothing, unless asked. The same workflow can be started from the Actions page. It then builds and checks everything and stops, which is how a change to the workflow is tried. With the "publish" box ticked it goes on to make the release, and the tag with it, from the version in Cargo.toml. The box is there because a tag cannot be pushed from everywhere the source can be, and a release should not wait for one machine. It is off unless ticked, so a trial can never turn into a release by accident.

Turned down. Building everything on one Linux machine: macOS programs cannot be linked there without Apple's files, and nothing would have been run on macOS or Windows. A curl ... | sh installer, and packages for apt, Homebrew and winget: each is a thing to keep working, and one file to unpack is enough for 0.1.

12.2 Ten kinds of machine

Linux on x86-64, ARM64, 32-bit x86, ARMv7 and ARMv6. macOS on Apple silicon and Intel. Windows on x86-64, 32-bit x86 and ARM64.

The rule. A kind of machine is in the list if Rust builds for it with nothing more than Rust brings, and people have it on a desk. The two 32-bit ARM builds are there for the Raspberry Pi, which is where firmware people are most likely to meet Linux on ARM.

Left out. Linux on RISC-V: Rust needs a separate C toolchain to link it, and no build would have been run. The BSDs, for the same reason. Both can build Zeno from source with cargo build --release, because Zeno depends on nothing but Rust.

The names say the system and the processor. The file is zeno-0.1.0-linux-arm64.tar.gz, not zeno-0.1.0-aarch64-unknown-linux-musl.tar.gz. The second is Rust's name for the same thing, and only means something to a person who already knows Rust. (Principle: unambiguous and intuitive.)

12.3 A released zeno is one file that needs nothing

The Linux builds are linked against musl, a C library that is put into the program. The Windows builds carry Microsoft's C library the same way.

Why. A Linux program normally borrows the C library of the system it runs on, and runs only where that library is at least as new as the one it was built against. A zeno built on this year's Ubuntu would not start on a Raspberry Pi with a system from three years ago. With the library inside it, one file runs on every Linux. On Windows the same choice means no "install the Visual C++ runtime" before the first run.

What it costs. A few hundred kilobytes in each file.

The names for a debugger are taken out (strip = true in Cargo.toml), which makes each file about a seventh smaller. A report of a crash in zeno itself then has no function names in it. Zeno's own mistakes are reported as messages, not crashes, so this costs little.

12.4 A build is run before it is released

tools/release-check.sh runs each build: the version it says, every example through both engines, the tests in the examples, the formatter, and zeno new. The ARM builds for Linux run under an emulator. The Intel build for macOS runs under Apple's translation.

Why. "It compiled" says that the compiler was content. It does not say that the program starts. The check is small on purpose: it needs no C compiler and takes seconds, so it can run ten times.

The one build that is not run is Windows on ARM64: there is no machine to run it on and no emulator there. It is released anyway, because the same source built by the same compiler for the other two Windows machines does run, and the release page says plainly that this one was not. Leaving it out would help nobody who has such a machine, and saying nothing would be claiming a check that was not made.

The whole test suite must pass on Linux before anything is released. It is also run on macOS and on Windows, where a failure is shown and does not stop the release: those runs are there to find out what is different on those systems. For 0.1.0 all the tests pass on macOS, and what Windows says is in 12.7. On macOS three examples are also compiled through C and through LLVM and must print what zeno run prints.

12.5 A compiled program rounds as zeno run does

Making the first release found a program that printed 74.30000000000001 with zeno run and 74.3 when compiled on a Mac. The line was c * 1.8 + 32.

Some processors have one instruction for "multiply, then add" that rounds once instead of twice. It is faster, and now and then the last digit is different. ARM64 has it, and a C compiler is allowed to use it without being asked. On 32-bit x86, gcc does a similar thing for another reason: it uses the old float instructions, which work to more digits than a float holds.

The decision. The C that Zeno writes tells the C compiler not to do either. Every float operation is one operation, rounded once, on every machine, as it is in zeno run.

Why. "A compiled program prints what zeno run prints" is the promise the tests are built on, and a program that gives one answer on the desk and another on the board is exactly what Zeno is for avoiding. The speed given up is small, and code that needs the fused instruction can ask for it by name when Zeno has a function for it.

Where it is said. In the C itself (two #pragma lines at the top of the runtime), not in a flag that zeno build passes. A firmware build is usually compiled by the board's own toolchain from the C that Zeno wrote, with flags Zeno never sees. What is written in the file goes wherever the file goes.

A limit of 0.1. clang on 32-bit x86 has no such line to be given, and on the systems where it is set up for processors older than 2001 (Debian and Ubuntu) it needs -msse2. gcc on the same machine is told by the file.

12.6 A compiled program on Windows prints \n

On Windows a C program that prints "\n" writes "\r\n", unless it asks for its bytes to be left alone. zeno run writes "\n" on every system. So a compiled program asks, before main starts.

Why. The same promise as 12.5. A program whose output is compared, counted or sent down a wire should give the same bytes however it was run. A Windows terminal shows both the same way.

Reading is unchanged. A program in a file with Windows line ends runs (the lexer skips "\r"), lines() and input() take a "\r\n" as one line end, and zeno format writes "\n", so a file has one layout on every system.

12.7 What Windows does not have yet

zeno.exe is checked like every other build, and zeno build makes programs that print what zeno run prints, through C with MinGW's gcc and through LLVM with the clang that comes with Visual Studio (three examples are compared each way). These are the limits:

  • zeno build needs a C compiler that takes gcc's options. Microsoft's own compiler (cl) takes different ones and has not been tried.
  • A path in a Zeno program or a zeno.package is text, where \ starts an escape, so it is written with /, which Windows accepts.
  • What a compiled program prints to a Windows console has not been looked at. The bytes are UTF-8, as they are into a file or another program, where they are the same as zeno run gives. A console set to an older code page may show a word in Tamil wrongly. No machine at hand has a Windows console to look at, and a mend that cannot be tried is not one to put in.

The first release (0.1.0) had two more, both mended since: 12.8 and 12.9.

12.8 A compiled program on Windows asks Windows

The first trial on Windows found that zeno build --llvm did not work there. Four things were in the way, and each is a place where C on Windows is not C elsewhere.

Waiting and the clock. The runtime used nanosleep and clock_gettime. Linux, macOS and MinGW have them. Microsoft's C library does not. On Windows the runtime now calls Sleep and the performance counter, which every C compiler there can reach.

setjmp. A try is built on it. Microsoft's takes a second thing, the frame of the calling function, which its longjmp uses to run clean-up code on the way back. A C compiler adds it unasked, because in C setjmp is a macro. LLVM IR has no macros, so on Windows the IR that Zeno writes passes null there, which asks for a plain jump, as on every other system.

-lm and .exe. The maths library is part of the C library on Windows, and Visual Studio's linker stops when asked for it. And a program there is called hello.exe, which gcc adds and clang does not. Zeno leaves out the first and writes the second.

Arguments. main is given its words through the machine's code page, so a word in Tamil arrived as ?????. The runtime asks Windows for the command line as Windows keeps it and turns each word into UTF-8, in a buffer on the stack: a build with no collector has no heap on Windows either.

Why this way. One runtime, with the Windows calls behind #ifdef _WIN32, for both compilers. The three functions are declared by hand in the runtime and <windows.h> is not read: it is very large and defines thousands of names, min and max among them, that a program's own names could meet.

Turned down. Using MinGW's nanosleep where there is one and Sleep otherwise: two ways to wait on one system, and only one of them tried by any given build. Telling people to use MinGW's clang: the clang most Windows machines have is the one Visual Studio brings.

Tried how. Here, with a Windows compiler and an emulator. Then on a Windows machine (12.10), where every test of Zeno now passes.

12.9 A path Zeno makes is written with /

import drivers.uart reads drivers/uart.zeno. On Windows that was drivers\uart.zeno, since Windows puts \ between the parts of a path, and takes / as well.

The decision. Every path Zeno puts together is written with /, on every system: in a message, and wherever a path is kept as text (modules::shown). A path the person typed is shown as they typed it.

Why. Three things went wrong with two ways. A path in a zeno.package is written with / (12.7), so joined the Windows way it came out as lib/filters\zeno.package. An editor names a file with / on every system, so the file it has open and the file an import asks for were two names for one file, and the editor's text was not used. And every message with a path in it had two spellings, one of which no test saw. It is the reasoning of 12.6: one way, on every system.

Turned down. Writing \ throughout on Windows. It is what Windows people are used to, but the zeno.package would have to be turned round too, and \ in Zeno text is an escape.

With it. zeno fetch tells git to leave line ends alone, so a fetched package is the same bytes on every machine. Git on Windows is usually set to write \r\n.

12.10 A trial on one machine

.github/workflows/trial.yml builds Zeno on one kind of machine, chosen when it is started, compiles three examples through C and through LLVM, and runs every test. It writes what it found on the summary page of the run. Nothing is packed or released.

Why. A mend for Windows can only be seen to work on Windows. The release workflow shows it too, on ten machines, and a month of GitHub's free time is about twenty such runs. A trial on Windows costs a quarter of one, and on Linux less.

13. What writing the book found

The Zeno Book runs every sample it prints through the real compiler. Writing it was the first time the whole language was used by somebody who was trying to explain it and not to build it, and it found places where the checker let through what the rest of Zeno then refused, or said less than it knew. Each is a place where a program was accepted and then failed, which is the thing the checker is for preventing.

13.1 zeno check --no-collector says what the build would say

var readings: list[int or none] = [1, none, 3] passed zeno check --no-collector and was refused by zeno build --no-collector.

Most of what a build with no collector refuses, the checker finds. A few things only the compiler meets, as it works out where each value is kept. check --no-collector now runs the compiler as well and throws the C away.

Why. "No errors found" from a command named after a build has to mean that the build goes through. Otherwise the command answers a question nobody asked.

Turned down. Teaching the checker each of the compiler's refusals: two lists of the same rules, which would drift apart again.

13.2 none is tested for with is, and only where it can be none

let x: int or none = find(xs, 3)
if x is none { ... }       # the way to ask
if x == none { ... }       # refused: write `is none`
let n = 3
if n is none { ... }       # refused: an int is never `none`

Both were accepted before. x == none gave the same answer as x is none, so there were two ways to ask one question. n is none gave false every time, which is a mistake that looks like a test.

Why is only. == compares two values, and none is the lack of one. Decision 1.13 says absence is tested with is none, and the parser already refuses is in front of anything but none. The other half belongs with it: none is tested with is and nothing else. Two values that may each be none are still compared with ==, because that compares values.

Why n is none is an error. It is the rule of 2.5a: a comparison whose answer is already known is a mistake.

A name that was narrowed may be tested again. After if x is not none, or after x = 3, the checker knows x is there. x is none is then also always false, but it is not refused: the name was declared int or none, and a second test is caution, not a mistake. The rule looks at the type the name was declared with.

13.3 A field of an enum case is taken out with match

enum Shape {
    Circle(radius: float)
    Square(side: float)
}
let shape = Circle(2.0)
print(shape.radius)        # refused

This was accepted outside strict mode and failed when the program ran, if the value was a Square. It is refused now, in every mode, and the message writes the match out:

`radius` belongs to the case `Circle`, and which case a `Shape` is, is
only known inside a `match`. Take it out there:
`match ... { case Circle(radius) { ... } }`

Why. An enum value is one of its cases, and match is where the program finds out which. A field read outside it is a guess about the case that nothing checks. There is one way to open an enum value now, and it is the one that cannot be wrong.

13.4 A const is written from what the compiler can work out

Decision 1.7 says a const is fixed when the program is built. The checker treated it as a let, so const N = len(name) and const P = k for a var k were accepted.

A const is now written from: numbers, text, true and false, quantities, other consts, a case of an enum that carries nothing, a constant of a module (math.pi), operators on those, a list or a dict of them, and the conversions between kinds of number (u8(n), u8.wrap(n), int(x), float(n)). Anything else is refused, with let named as the word for a value worked out once while the program runs.

Why a list, when the list can still be added to (3.9). A table of numbers is the commonest const in firmware, and what const promises is where the value comes from, not that it never changes. A list nobody may change is still the open question of 3.9.

Why no calls. len("abc") could be worked out by the compiler, and so could any function of constants, with enough work. The rule that fits in a sentence is "no calls, except to change the kind of a number", and nothing has needed more.

13.5 A parameter with a default comes after those without

function f(a = 1, b) is refused where it is declared.

Why. A call gives its arguments in order, so f(2) gives a and leaves b without a value: the default of a could only ever be used by naming every argument after it. It is the rule of Python and C++, and for the same reason.

Not for the fields of a class. A class lists what it holds in the order it holds it, and objects with several fields are usually made with names. class P { x: int = 0 y: int } stays.

One mistake, one message. f(b=5, 1) used to give three errors: the order, "b was given twice", and "needs a value for a". The second and third came from counting a plain argument that was in the wrong place. After the first, nothing more is said about that call.

13.6 Messages that say what Zeno writes

  • None, null, nil, NULL, nullptr and undefined are "not defined. Zeno spells it none", and True and False the same for true and false. A program may still use those names for things of its own: the hint is only given when the name means nothing.
  • elif at the start of a line gets "Zeno spells elif as else if". It did only after a } on the same line.
  • A whole number where a float goes: "Write a number with a decimal point, as in 1.0, and convert a value with float( )". Before, only the first half was said, also when the number was in a variable and had no decimal point to add.
  • min, max and sum on quantities say that they work on plain numbers, and that quantities are compared with < and > and added with +. They do not take quantities yet (a limit of 0.1).

13.7 A whole quantity prints without .0

print(3.0) is 3.0, and print(3 V) is 3 V, although a quantity is a float inside.

Settled as it is. The .0 of a float is there to say "this is a float and not an int", because both exist and behave differently. A quantity has only one kind, so there is nothing to tell apart, and 3 V is how a person writes three volts. A format gives the decimals when they are wanted: f"{supply:.1f}" is 3.0 V (10.29).

13.8 Found and left for later

(Three more were on this list at first, and are in 13.9. The three below were taken up after the release: chapter 17.)

  • For let x: str = first([1]) the error is reported at the argument (items should be list[str]), not at x. A list that is written out waits for the place to say what kind of number it holds (3.16b, so that [1, 2] can be a list[u8]), and here the place said str. The message is true and in the wrong order.
  • Box([]) outside strict mode has a T that nothing says, and becomes a value of unknown type without a word. Strict mode asks for the type.
  • min, max and sum do not take quantities. The message says what does (13.6).

13.9 Three messages that were left, and then were not

13.8 first listed five things. Three were mended a few hours later, once the release was no longer waiting on them.

A field that may be none. After if user.name is not none {, user.name + "!" is still refused: a field belongs to an object that any call may reach and change, so a test of it is not remembered (3.12 is about what a test says of a variable). The message said "Check for none first", to somebody who just had. Now it says what to do:

`name` is a field, and a field can be given a new value between a
test and its use, so a test of it is not remembered. Copy it into a
variable with `let`, and test that for `none`

Why not remember the test. It would be right only until the first call between the test and the use, and the checker would have to prove there is none. A variable of the function's own cannot be changed by anybody else, which is the whole reason a test of one is remembered.

Two enums with a case of one name. Dot in Shape and Dot in Mark gave "Dot is already declared in this block", which is true of a let and says nothing about enums. Now: "Dot is already a case of the enum Shape. A case is written by its name alone, so two enums in one file cannot both have a Dot".

A method without self. It was shown on the line of the class, with every other mistake in a method's first line. A function in the syntax tree now knows where it is written (ast::Function::span), and the mistake is shown on the method's own line.

14. A program in a web page

Zeno was made for firmware, and is not a language for the web. But a language somebody is learning to build should be able to show something, and the thing everybody can open is a page. The question of this chapter is what the least is that makes a page written in Zeno, without a second Zeno to keep in step with the first. web.md is the walkthrough.

14.1 The interpreter runs in the page, as WebAssembly

zeno build --web compiles nothing. The page it writes carries zeno.wasm, which is Zeno itself (lexer, parser, checker, interpreter) compiled by Rust for a browser, and the program as the text that was written. The browser runs the program as zeno run does.

Why. Every other way is a new engine. Zeno already has five that must agree (the interpreter, the VM, C, LLVM, and each of the last two in its firmware forms), and most of the work of every stage since the first has been holding them to one answer. This way adds no engine. A program in a page means what it means on a desk because the same code decides it, and the whole language is there on the first day: classes, generics, quantities, modules, packages.

What it costs. zeno.wasm is 1.1 MB and every page carries it. A program runs at the speed of zeno run. Both are fine for a page that waits for a click.

Turned down. - Compile Zeno to JavaScript. A sixth engine, and JavaScript's numbers are not Zeno's: no 64-bit whole number without BigInt, so int would be slow or wrong. - Compile through C, and C to WebAssembly. It reuses emit_c, and it needs clang with a WebAssembly target or Emscripten on the machine of everybody who builds a page. Compiled Zeno is also strict Zeno (the builds refuse some of what zeno run runs), so a page would have been a smaller language than a desk. - Emit WebAssembly from emit_llvm. The same, with LLVM in place of C.

One of the last two is the way to a page that is small and fast, if that is ever wanted. Nothing here is in its way: the web module would be the same.

14.2 The page is a module, and an element is a value

import web, then web.element("#count") gives an Element, which has fields and methods. It is the shape of board and Pin (3.15): the outside world is a module, and a thing in it is a value of a type the checker knows.

Why. It was already decided once, and a second answer to the same question would be the ambiguity the language is trying not to have. It also means the checker checks a page: count.text = 3 is refused before anything runs.

Turned down. - The browser's own names (document.querySelector, addEventListener, classList.add). Familiar to those who know them, and three ways of spelling for those who do not. - A way to call any JavaScript (js.call("alert", ...)). It makes everything possible and nothing checked, and under zeno run there is no JavaScript to call. - Describing the page in Zeno and letting a library work out what to change, as the big front-end libraries do. That is a library to write in Zeno one day, on top of this.

14.3 An element that is not there is an error

web.element("#count") raises a ValueError if the page has no such element. web.elements("li") gives a list, which may be empty.

Why. The usual cause is a misspelt id, and the first run finds it. Giving Element or none would make every program test for none around every element it uses, for the sake of a mistake that shows itself. pin(64) on a board with 64 pins is an error for the same reason.

Turned down. Element or none (above). A silent element that swallows what is done to it, which hides the misspelling.

14.4 text and value are fields. The rest are methods

count.text = "3" and box.value are read and written as fields. Everything else (add_class, hide, append, on) is a method.

Why. A field is something an object has, and a method is something it does. The text of an element and what has been typed into it are had: a program reads them and writes them, and count.text += "!" works with no new rule. A class is added and taken away, which is doing.

Turned down. set_text("3") and get_text(): two names for what = says. Fields for everything (item.hidden = true, item.classes.append("done")): a list of classes that the program changes would have to be watched for changes.

14.5 A function the page calls takes nothing

button.on("click", pressed) calls pressed(). web.after(500 ms, tick) calls tick(). Only web.on_key(pressed) hands something over: the key, as a str. The checker says so, in its own words, if the function takes anything else.

Why. An event object is mostly a way to find out which element it happened to, and a Zeno function knows that already: it is written where the element is in reach, and keeps it (5.5k is how a function does). What is left is what cannot be known any other way, and of that only the key was needed to write a page. So that is all there is, until a program needs more.

Turned down. An event object with fields (event.key, event.x, event.target): a new type, most of whose fields mean nothing for most events.

14.6 On a desk the page is simulated, and calls nothing

Under zeno run and zeno test there is a simulated page (src/page.rs). It has whatever element is asked for, keeps what is done to it, and prints a line for each change that could be seen, as the simulated board prints a line when a pin changes (3.15b). It never calls a function it was given.

Why. A program for a page should be testable where every other program is, in the same zeno test, with no browser installed. And the two engines are held to one output for a page as for everything else. Calling nothing is the honest version of "nobody is here": a test calls the function it wants to test, by its name.

A selector the simulated page cannot read (a[href], ul > li) is an error in web.elements, and not an empty list. An empty list would say the page has none, and the simulated page does not know.

Turned down. - Refuse to run a program that imports web. Then nothing of it could be tested without a browser. - Read the HTML file, to know which elements there are. It would find the misspelt id, which is worth having. It needs an HTML parser, which is larger than everything else in this stage. Left for later (14.13). - Call each function once, to see that it runs. A click that nobody made is not something the program can explain.

14.7 A built page is one file

todo.web.html holds the page, the program, web.js, and zeno.wasm written as base64.

Why. A page opened from the disk may not load another file: the browser refuses, to keep a page from reading the disk. With three files, the first thing a learner would see is a blank page, and the fix is to run a web server. One file opens with a double click, and is hosted by copying it.

What it costs. Base64 makes zeno.wasm a third larger (1.5 MB), and two pages on one site each carry their own.

Turned down. Three files and a small web server in zeno (zeno serve): a server is a lot to add to make a file open.

14.8 todo.html is written, and todo.web.html is built

The page for todo.zeno is todo.html, beside it. If there is none, the page is an empty one with a place for what the program prints. The built page is todo.web.html, in the folder the command is run in, where every build puts what it makes.

Why. The file somebody writes has the plain name, as hello.zeno does, and what is built is named after it. They cannot be the same name: built in the program's own folder, the page would be written over its own source.

The scripts go at the end of the body, so that every element is there when the program's first line runs.

Turned down. A name for the page given on the command line, and an -o for where the result goes: two more things to type, for a choice with one sensible answer. A line in the HTML that names the program (<script src="todo.zeno">): then the page would say which program it has, the command would say which program to build, and they could disagree.

14.9 The program travels as its text, named from its own folder

The page carries every file of the program as it was written, under the name it has from the program's folder: todo.zeno, drivers/uart.zeno, ../filters/zeno.package. The browser reads, joins and checks them as a desk does.

Why the text. It is what zeno.wasm already knows how to read, and an error in the page can show the line it is on. A syntax tree written out and read back would be a new format to keep right.

Why from its folder. zeno build --web /home/partha/site/todo.zeno would otherwise write /home/partha/site/ into a page that is then put on the web. The names are also the ones the browser's reading of the program asks for, so nothing has to translate them.

< in the program is written < in the page. A browser ends a script at </script> wherever it finds one, even in a str.

14.10 An error that nothing catches stops the page's program

It is shown in a box at the foot of the page, with its line, and in the console. After it, the page calls no more of the program's functions.

Why. It is what zeno run does, and for the reason it does it: the function that failed left its work half done, and whatever runs next runs on that. A page that goes on after an error is a page that is wrong in a way nobody is shown.

Turned down. Only the console: a learner does not have it open. Going on with the next click: above.

14.11 No sleep in a page, and input is the browser's question

time.sleep in a page stops the program with a message that says to use web.after. input("Name: ") asks with the browser's own question box.

Why. A page has one thread. A program that waits on it stops the page from drawing, and a sleep in a loop, which is what blink.zeno is, would hang the tab. input can work, because the browser has a question box that waits; it is ugly, and it lets every program that asks for a name run in a page.

14.12 200 nested calls in a page

On a desk a program may nest 1000 calls. In a page, 200.

Why. The interpreter calls itself for each nested call, on the browser's stack. Chromium gave out between 700 and 1000 calls of a plain function, and between 400 and 500 of one that calls itself from inside a loop, a match and a map. When the browser stops a program, Zeno cannot say which line it was. 200 is where it always still can, with room for a browser whose stack is half the size.

14.13 --web is a build of its own

zeno build --web goes with none of --llvm, --firmware, --no-collector and --memory, and says so. zeno build without it refuses import web, and says to use --web.

Why. Those options say how to compile, and a page is not compiled. A page built with --firmware would be a page that is no different, from a command that suggested it was.

14.14 Left for later

  • The simulated page does not read the HTML, so it does not find a misspelt id (14.6).
  • Events carry nothing but the key (14.5): no place of the mouse.
  • No style of one element, no drawing, no network, nothing kept between visits. Each is a name in web.js and a few lines in page.rs.
  • Only Chromium has run it.
  • A compiled page, small and fast (14.1).

14.15 This is version 0.2

Cargo.toml says 0.2.0 from the commit that brought the web module into main, which was the first commit after 0.1.0 was published.

Why 0.2 and not 0.1.1. It adds a module, a type and a build. A third number is for a mend, and nothing here mends anything.

Why not 1.0. Nothing that ran under 0.1 changes, so nothing was learnt about whether the language holds up. 1.0 should mean that programs written by somebody else have been through it.

15. Installing

A release is ten archives (chapter 12). This chapter is about not having to know which one is yours. install.md is the walkthrough.

15.1 Two installers: a setup program on Windows, a line elsewhere

Windows gets zeno-X.Y.Z-windows-setup.exe. macOS and Linux get a script, install.sh, that one line downloads and runs.

Why two. Each is what its system's users already do. On Windows a program arrives as a setup .exe with Next buttons, and a line to paste into a terminal is foreign. On macOS and Linux the people who would install a compiler have a terminal open, and the line is how Rust, Homebrew and Deno arrive.

Turned down. - A setup program for macOS too (.pkg). macOS refuses an unsigned one more firmly than it refuses a script, and signing wants a paid Apple account. - A script for Windows too (PowerShell). The setup program does more (an uninstaller in Windows' own list, a Start menu entry), and two ways to install on one system is one too many. - Packages for Homebrew, apt, winget and the rest. They are the best answer in the end, and each has a place where it must be published and kept current. Not yet (15.7).

15.2 For one user, with no administrator

The setup program installs into %LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Zeno, the script into ~/.zeno, and each changes that user's PATH only.

Why. Nothing about Zeno needs the whole machine, and a password prompt is a reason to stop. It also means a student on a machine that is not theirs can install it.

Turned down. C:\Program Files and /usr/local/bin: an administrator for no gain. A choice between the two on a page of the setup program: a question most people cannot answer.

15.3 The PATH is added, and the box is ticked

Both installers put Zeno's folder on the PATH. In the setup program it is a box, already ticked. The script just does it, and says which file it changed.

Why. A zeno that the terminal cannot find is not installed in any sense a beginner means. Python's installer leaves its box unticked, and the result is the best-known first error in Python.

The script adds one line to one startup file, the one for the shell in SHELL, and ends the line with a comment that names the installer. That comment is how it knows not to add the line twice and how --uninstall finds it again.

15.4 The tools Zeno hands work to are offered, never assumed

If there is no C compiler, or no git, the installer says which command needs it and offers to install it: with winget on Windows, with the system's package manager on Linux, with Apple's command line tools on macOS. On Windows the box is ticked, and can be unticked. In the script it is a question, and with nobody to ask (no terminal), the answer is no and the command is printed.

Why offer. Partha asked for an installer "which installs whatever needed", and without a C compiler zeno build, which is what Zeno is for, does not work.

Why not just install. A compiler is hundreds of megabytes, and on Linux it is installed as root. Doing that unasked, from a script piped out of curl, is the thing people are right to fear about such scripts. And everything but build and fetch works without.

Why not carry a compiler inside the installer. A small one exists (TinyCC), and Zeno's C has not been tried with it. gcc is too large to carry and has to be kept current. The system's package manager already does both.

15.5 zeno build looks for cc, then gcc, then clang

With no CC, the first of the three that is installed compiles the C.

Why. Windows has no cc, so every Windows user had to learn set CC=gcc before the first build. An installer that installs gcc and then needs a setting to use it has not finished its job. The order puts the system's own choice first.

15.6 Not signed

Neither the setup program nor zeno is signed. Windows shows a warning the first time, and the walkthrough says what it is.

Why. A certificate for Windows costs a few hundred dollars a year, and Apple's needs a paid developer account. The goal of Zeno is to learn by building it. If strangers start to install it, this is the first thing to change.

15.7 One setup program for three processors

The setup program holds the x86-64, the x86 and the ARM64 zeno.exe and installs the one for the machine.

Why. Three setup programs would bring back the question the installer exists to remove. The cost is a file a few megabytes larger.

15.8 The script carries its version, and the release checks it

install.sh has version=0.2.0 written in it, and is a file of the release of that version. tools/release-check.sh refuses a release in which that line and Cargo.toml differ.

Why. The script has to know which files to ask for, and their names have the version in them. Asking GitHub which release is the newest needs a second request and a way to read its answer, in a script that should need nothing but curl and tar. A plain line that a check keeps true is less to go wrong.

15.9 The download is checked before it is unpacked

The script compares the archive's SHA-256 with the line for it in SHA256SUMS.txt, and stops if they differ or if there is no way to compute one.

Why. A download that was cut short unpacks into a zeno that fails in some way nobody can explain. The check does not protect against somebody who can change the release itself, since they would change both files. It protects against everything less than that.

15.10 Left for later

  • Signing (15.6).
  • Packages for package managers came next: 15.12.
  • The winget step of the setup program is not run by any test: GitHub's Windows machines have no winget.
  • zeno does not update itself. The installer, run again, does.

15.11 An earlier release gets installers added, and nothing in it changed

Zeno 0.1.0 was released before the installers. A workflow, add-installers.yml, makes them for it from the files of that release: the setup program from the three zeno.exe that were released, and install.sh with version=0.1.0 in it. It adds the two files to the release, and two lines to the list of checksums.

Why. Somebody who has an archive of 0.1.0 and its checksum must find the same file and the same checksum there tomorrow. So a release is added to and never changed. Making the installers from the released files, and not from the source of that day, means there is one zeno 0.1.0 and not two that should be the same.

Turned down. A 0.1.1 that differs only by having installers: a new version says that Zeno changed, and it did not. Building 0.1.0 again from its tag: a second build is not the first one byte for byte, so the setup program would install a zeno that nobody checked. Leaving 0.1.0 as it was: it is the only release there is until 0.2.0 is published.

The installers are today's, so they had to allow for what 0.1.0 lacks: no zeno.wasm, and a zeno build that knows only cc (15.5). The setup program leaves the first out and says the second in the box that offers a compiler.

15.12 Packages for six package managers, made from the files of a release

tools/package.sh makes a package of Zeno for apt (a .deb), pacman, snap and flatpak, and writes the recipe that Homebrew and winget read. Each holds the zeno of a release's archive. Nothing is built for a package.

Why. People who use a package manager want to use it for everything: it knows what is installed, updates it and removes it cleanly. And each of these is the usual way to install a program on some system that Zeno runs on. Making them from the released files, as the installers are (15.11), means one zeno for each processor and not seven that should be the same. It also means the script can be tried on a release that exists, which is how it was tried.

Turned down. Building in each manager's own way (a Debian source package, cargo build in the PKGBUILD and the formula, snapcraft, a flatpak manifest): each would compile Zeno again, in a way that nothing else here checks, and four of the six would need Rust on the machine that installs. Only the two or three most used: all six were asked for, and after the first, each was an afternoon and not a week.

docs/packages.md walks through it.

15.13 The .deb recommends a C compiler and git

Its Recommends line is gcc | c-compiler, libc6-dev | libc-dev, git, and clang is in Suggests.

Why. apt installs what a package recommends unless it is told not to, and that is 15.4 in apt's own words: what Zeno hands work to is offered, and somebody who does not want it says so. Depends would force a compiler on a machine that only runs zeno run. gcc | c-compiler leaves a machine that has clang alone.

pacman has only optdepends, which it shows and does not install. Nearly every Arch machine that builds anything has both programs.

15.14 The .deb for armhf holds the build for ARMv6

Why. Debian's armhf is ARMv7, and Raspberry Pi OS gives the same name to a Pi 1 and a Pi Zero, which are ARMv6. A package cannot ask which. The ARMv6 build runs on both. The ARMv7 build would stop on a Pi Zero with "Illegal instruction", which says nothing to anybody. What is lost is a little speed on ARMv7, in an interpreter that was never the fast way to run a program.

The snap for armhf holds the ARMv7 build: Ubuntu, where snaps are at home, has no ARMv6.

15.15 Where a package puts Zeno

/usr/lib/zeno/zeno and zeno.wasm beside it, a link at /usr/bin/zeno, the examples and the editor setup in /usr/share/zeno, the README and the docs in /usr/share/doc/zeno.

Why. zeno build --web looks for zeno.wasm beside the program (docs/web.md), and /usr/bin is for programs only. A link keeps both rules: Linux tells a program where it really is, so Zeno finds its neighbour. The examples are apart from the docs because small systems, containers above all, are often set up to leave /usr/share/doc out, and the first thing anybody is told to do is run an example. The first trial of the .deb, in a container, found exactly that.

15.16 The snap is classic

Why. A snap is otherwise shut in, and a compiler cannot work shut in. It reads a program from wherever it is kept, and runs the C compiler of the machine. The strict kind can be given the home folder and nothing more. classic has a price, which is that the Snap Store gives it only on request (15.21).

15.17 The flatpak runs on the Sdk

Its runtime is org.freedesktop.Sdk, and it is given the home folder and the network.

Why. A flatpak has no classic: it cannot see the machine's compiler at all. So either zeno build does not work in it, or the compiler is in the box. The Sdk is the runtime that programs are built on, and it has gcc and git. With it, everything Zeno does works in the box with nothing else to install, which is the whole point of the installers (15.4).

Turned down. The Platform runtime, which is a third of the size: then zeno build says there is no C compiler, to somebody who can see one on their machine. Running the machine's compiler from inside the box (flatpak-spawn --host): it needs the box opened so far that it is not a box.

Flatpak is made for programs with a window, and is the poorest fit of the six. It is here because it was asked for and because it works.

15.18 The recipes are kept in this repository, and written after a release

Formula/zeno.rb, packaging/arch/PKGBUILD and packaging/winget are written by tools/package.sh and pushed to main by the last step of a release (tools/keep-recipes.sh).

Why here. Homebrew reads a formula from a git repository and from nowhere else. Keeping it in this one means there is nothing more to make: brew tap is told the address. A repository of its own, named homebrew-zeno, would make the command shorter, and is one of the steps left for later.

Why after. A recipe holds the checksums of the files it downloads, so it cannot be written before they exist. The alternative, writing it by hand after each release, is the kind of step that is forgotten.

The commit is made in Partha's name, as every commit here is.

15.19 The MIT licence, from 0.2.0

Zeno is under the MIT licence. The file LICENSE is in the repository, and from 0.2.0 it is in every archive, in what the setup program installs, and in every package. The recipes and the packages name it.

Why MIT. Partha chose it, on 16 November 2026. It is the shortest of the common licences and the one most people have already read: use it, change it, pass it on, and keep the notice. That suits a language that was built to be learnt from. The stores of 15.21 each ask what licence a program has, which is why the question came up when it did.

Turned down. MIT or Apache-2.0, as Rust has: Apache adds words about patents, which matter to a company and are two more pages to read. GPL: a compiler under it is fine, but the runtime that every compiled program carries (runtime.c) would then need an exception written for it, or every program built with zeno build would be bound by it. None: then nobody may lawfully do anything with Zeno, which is not what a language for learning wants.

0.1.0 is left as it was. It was released the day before, with no licence in it, and a release is added to and never changed (15.11). So tools/package.sh asks the archive: one that has LICENSE in it gets packages that say MIT and carry the file, and one that has not gets packages that say no licence was chosen. Each says what was true of that release.

15.20 Run by winget, the setup program installs Zeno and nothing else

The winget files pass /MERGETASKS="!compiler,!git".

Why. The setup program's two boxes install a compiler and Git by running winget (15.4). winget runs the setup program without showing it, so the boxes would be ticked with nobody asked, and a second winget would start inside the first. Somebody who installs with winget installs those two with winget.

15.21 What packages do not do yet

  • Only Homebrew finds Zeno with no file. The others are ready up to an account, a key or a pull request, each of which is in Partha's name and so is his to do (15.22 to 15.28, and docs/stores.md).
  • The winget files are written and not run: GitHub's Windows machines have no winget.
  • The packages for processors other than x86-64 are made and not installed by a test.
  • No .rpm, Nix, Scoop or Chocolatey.

15.22 The apt repository is the release itself

Decision. What apt reads to find the .deb files, a list of them and the list signed, are four more files of each release (tools/apt-repo.sh). apt is given the address that the newest release's files are downloaded from, as a flat repository.

Why. apt needs only three things at one address: InRelease, Packages and the .deb files, and a release is an address that has its .deb files already. So nothing is hosted and nothing is copied, and the list cannot fall behind the release or point at a file that is gone. It was tried: apt reads it, checks the signature and installs.

What it costs. apt sees the newest version only, which is what apt upgrade wants. And once people have added the address, every release must have the four files, or their apt update complains. So the release workflow makes them in the same step that publishes.

Turned down. - A tree of folders on zeno-lang.org (dists/stable/main/...), the usual shape. It is a second place that has to be built and deployed for each release, by another repository, and it either copies the .deb files or redirects each one back to the release. - A PPA on Launchpad. It builds from source on Launchpad's machines, from a Debian source package that Zeno does not have, and serves Ubuntu only: no Debian, no Raspberry Pi OS. - A service that hosts apt repositories. One more account, and a company to depend on, for four small files.

15.23 The key that signs for apt is a secret of the repository

Decision. The private half of the key is the secret APT_SIGNING_KEY. It has no passphrase and does not expire. The public half is a file of the release, zeno-apt-key.asc, written from the private half each time.

Why. A workflow has to sign with no person there, and a secret is the one place a workflow can read and nobody can read back. A key that expired would stop every machine's apt update on a day nobody remembers. Partha makes the key, on his own machine, because whoever has it can sign for Zeno.

Turned down. A key made by a workflow: it would be gone when the run ends, or would have to be written somewhere to be kept. Asking people to trust the address with no key (trusted=yes): then anybody between the release and the machine could hand out another zeno.

15.24 On Arch the package is zeno-lang-bin

Decision. pkgname=zeno-lang-bin, from the next release on, and in the AUR from the start. It says conflicts=('zeno-bin').

Why. The AUR has a package zeno already, a node system for simulation, which has nothing to do with this. On Arch foo-bin means "foo, already built", so zeno-bin would have claimed to be that program. zeno-lang is the name the repository and the website have. The package files of 0.1.0 and 0.2.0 are named zeno-bin and stay as they are (15.11). The new one takes the place of such a one.

Turned down. Keeping zeno-bin and seeing whether anybody minds: a name in the AUR is hard to change after people have installed it.

15.25 The recipe for the AUR: .SRCINFO from makepkg, and a licence of its own

Decision. tools/package.sh has makepkg --printsrcinfo write .SRCINFO in the Arch container that makes the package, and it is kept beside the PKGBUILD. packaging/arch/LICENSE is 0BSD.

Why. The AUR reads .SRCINFO and does not run a PKGBUILD, so the two must say the same, and only makepkg knows for certain what a PKGBUILD says. The stores workflow compares them before it sends anything. The AUR asks that a recipe has a licence, apart from the licence of the program, and suggests 0BSD: the recipe is twenty lines that anybody may copy.

15.26 A store is switched on by its secret, in a workflow of its own

Decision. The workflow stores tries each store with no login, and with "send" ticked sends to each store that the repository has a secret for: APT_SIGNING_KEY, AUR_SSH_KEY, SNAPCRAFT_STORE_CREDENTIALS. A job with no secret says so and does nothing. A release starts it as its last step. The snaps are uploaded as snap pack made them.

Why. Each store needs an account that only Partha can make, and they will not all be made on one day. So each has to work alone, and giving the secret is the whole of switching one on. It is a workflow of its own because a version that is already released has to be sent too, 0.2.0 first of all, and because a store that is down must not stop a release.

Turned down. - Steps in the release workflow only. Then nothing could be sent without a new release. - A snapcraft.yaml, which is how most snaps are built. It would be a second way of making the same file, and the one way there is has been tried. - Sending by hand, from a list of commands. It would be done once and forgotten at the next release.

15.27 winget: today's form, and a pull request by hand

Decision. The three files are in the form 1.28.0, name the schema they are checked against, and have ProductCode: the id of the setup program with the _is1 that Inno Setup adds, which is the name Windows keeps the installed Zeno under. Sending them to Microsoft's list stays a pull request that Partha opens.

Why. The list wants the newest form, and its own schema says what that is, so the files are checked against it in the stores workflow. ProductCode is how winget knows that a Zeno installed by the setup program is the same one. The pull request is made from his account and signs Microsoft's terms for contributors in his name, which a secret in this repository should not do for him.

15.28 Zeno is not asked for on Flathub

Decision. No request to Flathub. The .flatpak stays a file of each release.

Why. Flathub's rules say that programs run in a terminal are not taken, with an exception only for tools about flatpak itself, and that everything must be built from source on its own machines. Zeno is run in a terminal, and its flatpak holds the zeno of the release.

Turned down. Asking anyway. It would spend the time of the people who review there on a request their rules already answer.

16. A page that does more

Chapter 14 made the least that is a page. Its last section (14.14) listed what a real front end would miss: where the mouse is, the style of one element, drawing, the network, something kept between visits, a simulated page that knows the page, and a smaller file. This chapter is those, and a playground. web.md and playground.md are the walkthroughs.

16.1 A function given to on may ask for the Event

button.on("click", function() { ... }) is called with nothing, as before. paper.on("click", function(event: Event) { ... }) is called with what happened. The checker allows those two shapes and says so if it is given a third.

Why. 14.5 held that a function knows its element already, and that stays true for most of a page. It is not true of where the mouse was, and not of which item of a list was clicked when one function is given to the whole list. Those can be known no other way, so there has to be a way to be told.

Letting the function ask keeps 14.5 for everybody who needs nothing: no program that ran under 14.5 changes, and the first example anyone reads still has function().

Why by the number of parameters. The function's first line already says what it wants. A second method (on_event) would be two names for one thing, and a flag would say twice what the parameter says once.

Turned down. web.mouse(), a question the function can ask: it answers for "now", and a function called a moment after the click, from the queue (14, waiting), would be told where the mouse went since. Two parameters (x, y): right for the mouse and wrong for every other event, and no room for the target.

16.2 An Event has five fields, and they are read only

kind, target, x, y, key. x and y are floats, in pixels from the top left corner of the element that on was given to. For an event with no mouse they are 0, and key is "" for an event with no key.

Why these five. Each is something a program cannot find out any other way, and each was needed by an example: the sketch needs x and y, one function for a whole list needs target, a function given to two events needs kind, and key is what on_key hands over, for an element that wants its own keys.

Why from the element's corner. A program draws on the element it listens to, and a canvas counts from its own corner. From the corner of the window, every program would first have to take away where the element is, which it has no way to ask.

Why 0 and "" and not none. 14.5 turned down an event object because most of its fields mean nothing for most events. none would make every use of event.x begin with a test that a click never fails. A program that asks for x of a "submit" has made a mistake that 0 shows as well as anything.

Why read only. It says what happened. Changing it would change nothing on the page, and a field that can be written and does nothing is a trap.

Left out for now. Which button of the mouse, Shift and Ctrl, the wheel, touches. Each is a field, three files away.

16.3 fire calls the program's functions, at once

button.fire("click") calls every function that on was given for "click" on that element, in the order given, before it returns. One that asks for the Event gets one with the element as target and 0 for x and y. It does the same in a browser and on the simulated page.

Why. 14.6 left a test one way to press a button: call the function by name. A function written in place (item.on("click", function() { ... })) has no name, so the most natural way to write a page could not be tested. fire is the press.

Why it does not send the browser an event. In a browser an event that is sent is handled later, after the function that sent it has returned (the queue, 14). On a desk it would have to be handled at once, because there is no later. A test that passes on a desk and means something else in the browser is worse than no test. So fire is the same everywhere: it is a call. It does not tick a checkbox or send a form, and docs/web.md says so.

Turned down. button.click(): one method for each event, and the browser has hundreds.

16.4 The style of one element: set_style and get_style

e.set_style("color", "red"), with the name CSS uses. e.get_style("color") gives what was set on the element itself, or none.

Why at all. 14 said "use a class", and that is still the right answer for a look that is known when the page is written. It cannot do a look that is worked out: a bar as wide as a reading, a colour the person chose.

Why CSS's names. background-color, and not backgroundColor or background_color. The page beside the program is written in CSS, and one thing should have one name in both files.

Why a wrong name is an error. A browser ignores a style it does not know, and the program goes on with nothing changed and nothing said. Zeno stops with a ValueError. (The simulated page does not know CSS and cannot do this. It is found when the page is opened.)

Why get_style is only what was set. What an element finally looks like comes from every rule of the page, in units the browser chooses (rgb(255, 0, 0) for red). Handing that back would be a second, bigger thing with the same name.

16.5 Drawing is five methods of an element

draw_rect, draw_circle, draw_line, draw_text, erase. Places and sizes are numbers (int or float), in pixels from the top left corner. The colour is last, and is whatever CSS calls a colour. On an element that is not a <canvas> they raise a ValueError.

Why so few. A browser's canvas has a pen with a state: a colour, a width, a path that is begun and ended. Every call depends on the calls before it, which is exactly what makes drawing code hard to read and to test. Here each call says everything it needs and leaves nothing behind, so a line printed by the simulated page (#paper draw_rect 10 10 50 20 red) is the whole of what happened.

Why the colour is last and not left out. A default colour is a state by another name: the reader has to know what it is.

Why draw_text counts from the top left. A browser counts text from its baseline, so text drawn at 0, 0 is off the top of the canvas. Every other shape here starts at its corner.

Why methods of Element, and no Canvas type. A canvas is an element: it has a class, can be hidden, hears clicks. A second type would need a way to get from one to the other, for no gain but a check the page cannot make anyway (the checker does not read the page).

Left out for now. Curves, images, line width, drawing that is not filled. Games that redraw everything sixty times a second are too slow for an interpreter in any case (14.1).

16.6 web.every, beside web.after

web.every(1 s, tick) calls tick that often until the page is closed. Less than 1 ms is a ValueError.

Why. A clock, a poll, anything that moves. With after alone it is written as a function that asks for itself again, and every program that does it gets it slightly wrong (the time drifts by however long the function takes).
