Why it cannot be stopped. Stopping needs a value to stop (let timer = web.every(...), timer.stop()), which is a new type. A function that has nothing to do can return at once, and that has been enough. It is the first thing to add when it is not. (It was: chapter 19.)

16.7 What is kept between visits: save, load, forget

web.save("name", text), web.load("name") (a str, or none), web.forget("name"). Names and values are text.

Why text only. The browser keeps text. A number is str(n) on the way in and int(text) on the way out, where the program can see it and where a wrong value raises the error it should.

Why load gives none. Nothing kept is the ordinary case, on every first visit, and the checker then makes the program say what happens.

Why a browser that refuses keeps nothing, silently. A private window may refuse. The program already has to work on a first visit, when nothing is kept, so "nothing is ever kept" is a case it handles already.

On a desk what is saved is kept until the run ends, so a test can save and load. It is not written to the disk: a test should not depend on the run before it.

16.8 The network: fetch and post, with a function for the answer

web.fetch(address, done) and web.post(address, text, done). done is called later with two things: the status (an int: 200, 404) and the body (a str). An address that could not be reached at all gives status 0 and "".

Why a function for the answer. The answer comes later, and a page must not wait (14.11 is the same rule for sleep). Zeno has one way to say "later": hand over a function, as on and after do. A second way (a value that stands for an answer to come) is a feature of a language, with rules of its own, and this is one module.

Why two parameters and no Reply type. There are two things to know. A type with two fields is longer at every use and adds a name to learn.

Why status 0 and no error. An error raised later, inside nothing the program called, has nowhere to be caught. And "could not be reached" has to be handled beside 404 and 500 anyway, in the same if.

Why the body is text. There is no JSON in the library yet. When there is, it reads a str.

On a desk nothing is fetched and done is not called, as a timer is not (14.6). A test calls done itself with the answer it wants to try, which is the test worth writing: what the page does with a 404.

16.9 The simulated page reads the page that was written

If todo.html is beside todo.zeno, zeno run and zeno test read it, and the simulated page has those elements and no others. With no such file it has every element that is asked for, as in 14.6.

Why. 14.6 said that the simulated page "cannot say whether the page has the elements the program asks for", and a misspelt id is the commonest mistake there is in a page. It was found only in a browser, by hand.

Why by the file's name. It is the file zeno build --web already takes as the page (14.8), so the page that is tested is the page that is built, and there is nothing to configure.

Why not in the checker. The checker could look up web.element("#list") when the selector is written out. It would then need the page, which the language server and zeno check do not have in hand, and it would still miss a selector that is built ("#row" + str(n)). On the first run, the same message comes with the same line.

Why a reader of our own (src/html.rs). Zeno has no dependencies (5.1). The reader is 200 lines, because it reads pages that are written right and does not mend the others as a browser must. Text that is only the spaces a page is laid out with counts as none.

16.10 zeno.wasm is built for size, where that costs no speed

.cargo/config.toml builds it with opt-level=s, one code generation unit and whole-program lto. 1.1 MB became 800 KB.

Why s and not z. Measured, on a loop of 300,000 steps and a recursion: as built before, 173 ms; s with the rest, 167 ms; z, 262 ms, for 650 KB. A page that is 150 KB smaller and half again as slow is a bad trade once the page is packed (16.11), when the difference is 40 KB.

Why in .cargo/config.toml. It holds for this one target, and cargo build --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown stays the command, in the docs and in the release workflow. zeno for a desk is built as before.

16.11 zeno.wasm is packed in the page, by a packer of our own

A built page holds zeno.wasm as DEFLATE in base64, and the browser unpacks it with DecompressionStream("deflate-raw"). src/deflate.rs is the packer. A built page went from 1.5 MB to 0.4 MB.

Why pack. Base64 makes bytes a third longer, and zeno.wasm is most of every page. A web server can pack a page as it sends it, but 14.7 promised one file that works from the disk and from a mail, where no server is.

Why DEFLATE. It is the one format every browser unpacks by itself, so nothing is added to the page to undo it. A better format would need its unpacker in every page, in JavaScript.

Why write it. No dependencies (5.1), and a release must not need gzip on the machine that builds a page, least of all on Windows. It is 300 lines, and it comes within two parts in a hundred of gzip -9 on zeno.wasm (292 KB against 288 KB) in a tenth of a second. The test unpacks what it packs with Python's zlib, where there is a Python.

The cost. The page needs a browser from 2023 or later. An older one is told so, in the box where errors go.

16.12 A playground, written by zeno playground

zeno playground writes playground.html: a box for a program, a box for its page, Run, a few examples, and a link that holds the program. Each run is a new frame.

Why. "What does Zeno look like?" should have an answer that is a link. Everything else needs an install first.

Why a command, and not a file in the repository. The page holds zeno.wasm, which is built, and the examples, which change. A file that is checked in is out of date the day after. The command writes it from the Zeno that is running, so it cannot disagree with it.

Why a new frame for each run. A program in a page does not end (14). Its timers and the functions it gave away are the browser's to hold, and there is no list of them to go through. Throwing the frame's page away is the one thing that is certain to stop it all, and it is what the browser does best.

Why the program is in the link, after #. Sharing then needs no server and no account, and what follows # is never sent anywhere. The price is a long link for a long program.

Left out. Colours in the program text, several files, a Stop button for a program that never returns (it needs a Worker, and a program in a Worker cannot reach the page).

16.13 Left for later

  • JSON read into values, which fetch wants (done since: chapter 18).
  • A timer that can be stopped (done since: chapter 19), and an event with the keys held down and the button of the mouse (16.6, 16.2).
  • Images and curves on a canvas.
  • The address bar: a page with several views that the back button walks through.
  • Other browsers. Only Chromium has run any of this.
  • A compiled page, small and fast (14.1). It is still the big one.

17. What 0.1 left, taken up

13.8 and the last list of version-0.1.md named things that were found and left. This chapter is the ones that have been taken up since, each with what was done and why, and one that was looked at and left as it is.

17.1 A written list says what it holds, when the place could never agree

let x: str = first([1]) used to say that items of first[str] should be list[str], at the argument. Now it says "x should be str, but this is int", at x.

The rule of 3.16b stays: a list that is written out waits for the place the result goes, so that let b: list[u8] = twice([1, 2]) is a list of bytes. What changed is that the place is listened to only if the written values could be what it asks for. A written number can be an int, a float, a sized number or a quantity. It can never be a str. Where it never can, the list is what it looks like, and the mismatch is reported where it really is: an int is being put into a str.

Why. The old message was true and in the wrong order (13.8). It named a type, first[str], that the reader never wrote, and pointed at the half of the line that was right.

17.2 Box([]) outside strict mode stays of unknown type

let b = Box([]), with class Box[T] { items: list[T] }, gives a Box whose T nothing says. In strict mode that is an error that asks for the type. Outside it, T is unknown, and no message is given. 13.8 called that "without a word" and left it open. It is now settled as it is.

Why. It is the rule of 0.2 (types are optional) and nothing else. let xs = [] is a list of unknown type outside strict mode and an error in it, and Box([]) is the same list with a class around it. A message for the one and not the other would be a rule with an exception, and a message for both would end optional types for the commonest line in a first program.

17.3 min and max take quantities in one unit. sum does not

max(3 V, 2 V) is 3 V, and min(readings) of a list[V] is in volts, in every engine. Quantities in two units are refused, with the conversion to write (x.V), and so is a quantity beside a plain number.

Why now. 13.6 refused them with a message that said to compare with <. That was honest and it was a loop to write for something every list of readings wants.

Why one unit. min gives back one of the values it was given, as it is. min(3 V, 200 mV) would be 200 mV, and min(1 V, 2000 mV) would be 1 V: the unit of the answer would depend on the numbers, and the checker has to know the unit before the program runs (1.15). With one unit going in, one unit comes out.

Why not sum. The sum of no volts is 0 V. A list that is running does not know its unit when it is empty: the unit is in the values. zeno run would print 0 where the compiled program, which knows the type, prints 0 V, and the two must print the same (5.19). So sum still refuses, and its message now gives the three lines to write: start from var total = 0 V, and add each in a loop. min and max have no such case, because the least of nothing is an error already.

17.4 Resting the mouse on a field says its type

In an editor, the x of p.x answers x: int, and a method what it takes and returns. ExprKind::Field now keeps where the name after the dot is written (at), and the checker notes each one it reads with its type.

Why only the type, and not where it was declared. A use of a variable points back to its declaration through a number the checker gives each one (9.11). A field has no such number and ast::Field has no place. Giving it one is a change to the tree that every stage reads, for a question ("where is x declared?") whose answer is nearly always "in the class two screens up", which "go to declaration" on the class already finds.

17.5 zeno test --only "text" chooses tests by their name

It runs the tests whose name has the text in it, exactly as written, and passes over the rest. The file itself runs whole first, as always.

Why text in the name, and not a pattern. A test's name is a sentence (10.16), so a word or two of it is how anyone would point at it. A pattern language is a second thing to learn for a tool that is used in a hurry.

Why the capitals count. "The same text" has one meaning. "The same, give or take capitals" has one for English and several for other languages (3.14 is the same choice for upper).

Why the program still runs whole. Tests use what the file declares and what its top level did (10.19). Running less would make a test pass alone and fail in company, or the other way.

18. JSON

A page that can ask the network (16.8) gets text back, and nearly always the text is JSON. 16.13 left reading it for later, and this is that. json.md is the walkthrough.

18.1 json.parse gives a value whose type is known when the program runs

json.parse(text) gives dicts, lists, text, numbers, bools and none, as the text has them. The checker treats the result as it treats a variable with no type written: anything may be asked of it, and a mistake is found when the line runs. Strict mode and zeno build refuse it, with the message they have for any value of unknown type.

Why. What the text holds is not known until it arrives. A language has two honest answers to that. One is a type for "some JSON" with a way to ask what each part is. The other is a value whose type is found out at run time. Zeno has the second already: it is what a type left out means (0.2), and every program a beginner writes uses it. Using it here adds nothing to learn.

The cost. JSON is out of firmware builds. A board does not parse JSON from a server, and the day one must, the answer is reading into a class (18.5), which has types.

Turned down. A Json enum with a case for each kind (Text(str), Number(float), List(...)): right for a language with only static types, and four lines of match to read one price.

18.2 json.write writes an object field by field

json.write(order) writes {"who": ..., "lines": [...]}: each field by its name, in the order the class declares them. It also takes what parse gives. A dict's keys are written as text.

Why objects. A JSON object nearly always has values of several types under its names: a name, a count, a list. A dict written in a Zeno program cannot, because its values have one type (6.31, 10.11), and that rule is right for dicts. The thing in Zeno that has named parts of different types is an object. So a program says what it sends by declaring a class, and the checker holds every value to it before anything is sent.

Why an int key becomes text. JSON's keys are text. The other choice is to refuse, and then a dict that counts by number cannot be written at all without being rebuilt by hand.

Why a quantity is refused. 3.3 V would be written 3.3, and whoever reads it has to guess the unit. Dropping a unit silently is what quantities exist to stop (1.15). The message says to write the number and put the unit in the name.

18.3 A number with nothing after the point is an int

12 and 12.0 both give an int. 12.5 gives a float.

Why. JSON has one kind of number, and what writes it often does not keep the difference: a price of two is 2 from one server and 2.0 from another, and 2 from JavaScript always. If the .0 decided the type, the same program would get an int on Monday and a float on Tuesday from the same server. Deciding by the value is at least the same every day. A program that wants a float says float(...), which takes either.

The cost. json.write(json.parse("2.0")) is 2.

18.4 A member that is null is left out of the dict

{"note": null} gives a dict with no note.

Why. A dict holds no none (10.11): get gives none for a key that is not there, and one way to say "nothing here" is the rule. JSON has two (the member is missing, or it is null), and programs that write JSON use them for the same thing. With the member left out, data.get("note") is none either way, and data["note"] is a KeyError either way.

The cost. A text that is read and written again loses its null members. In a list, where position matters, null stays as none.

18.5 Left for later

  • Reading into a class: json.read(text, Order), with each field checked as it is read. It would give JSON to strict mode and to compiled programs.
  • The place of a mistake in the text.
  • JSON laid out over several lines.

19. A timer that can be stopped

16.6 gave web.every and said why what it starts could not be stopped: stopping needs a value to stop. This chapter is that value.

19.1 web.after and web.every give a Timer, and it has stop()

let ticking = web.every(1 s, tick)
stop_button.on("click", function() { ticking.stop() })

Why. A stopwatch, a poll that ends when the answer has come, a message that should not appear because the user has moved on. With no way to stop, each is written as a var running that the function reads first, and a timer that goes on calling for as long as the page is open to be told no.

Why a value with a method, and not web.stop(number). A number is something a program can add to, print and get wrong, and web.stop(3) says nothing a reader can check. ticking.stop() reads as what it does, and the checker knows a Timer from an int. It is the shape Element has already: a value the page knows, with methods.

Why one type for both. What is stopped is a call that waits, whether it waits once or again and again. Two types would be two names for one method.

The alternatives. The function could return false to say "no more": it is short, and it makes every function given to every return something, and the stop can then only come from inside. A stop word inside the function has the same limit.

19.2 Stopping what has stopped is nothing

stop() on a timer that was stopped before, or on an after that has made its call, does nothing and is not an error.

Why. "Make sure this is not running" is what the program means, and it is true afterwards either way. An error would make every caller keep a var stopped beside the timer, which is the bookkeeping the timer was added to take away. A browser treats its own timers the same way.

19.3 A program that does not keep the timer is as it was

web.after(500 ms, tick) on a line by itself still means what it did. What it gives is dropped, as the answer of list.pop() on a line by itself is.

Why. Most timers are never stopped, and every program written for 0.2 before this has such lines.

19.4 On a desk

Under zeno run and zeno test the page calls nothing (14.6), so stop() has nothing to call off, and prints nothing: a line is printed for a change that could be seen (14.7), and this is not one. A Timer prints as Timer, and two are equal when they are one.

19.5 Left for later

  • Asking a timer whether it is still waiting.
  • Starting a stopped timer again. web.every makes a new one.

20. The playground on zeno-lang.org

The playground (16.12) goes on the website, at zeno-lang.org/playground, where anybody can open it. Two things had to change first.

20.1 In a page, one turn of a program may run for 5 seconds

A program's first run, and each call from the page (a click, a key, a timer, an answer from the network), is a turn. In a browser a turn that has gone on for 5 seconds is stopped, with an error at the line it was on: a loop, or the call it was making. On a desk nothing changes: a program runs for as long as it likes.

Why. While Zeno runs, the page does nothing else. It does not draw, scroll or let a button be pressed, and the playground runs each program in a frame that shares its thread, so a loop that never ends held the whole playground, with nothing to press to stop it. That is the first thing somebody tries in a playground. A program that stops on its own, with its line and a question, is the answer zeno run would give if it could.

Why 5 seconds. A page that does not answer for longer than that looks broken, and browsers start offering to close it at about that point. Every program in examples/ takes a small part of a second in a page, so the limit is far from anything that is meant.

Why it is not an error the program can catch. It is not about the program's data, and a try around the loop would only start the loop again. It is like going past the nesting of 200 calls (14.12).

How it costs nothing. The interpreter counts loop rounds and calls, and asks the clock once in 65536 of them, because asking it crosses into JavaScript. Counting is one addition.

Rejected. A Stop button that works at once: it needs Zeno in a Worker, where a program cannot reach the page (16.12). A count of steps instead of a clock: a step is fast on one machine and slow on another, and what matters to the person waiting is time. A frame from another origin, which some browsers run on another thread: not all do, phones least of all, and the program would no longer be able to share the Zeno the playground read in.

20.2 The playground's own page for what a program prints

A program with no page of its own (most programs) is shown in a page the playground writes: what it prints, in the playground's type, and under the last line, the error that stopped it. It follows the dark setting of the browser. zeno build --web keeps its plain page and its error at the foot (14).

Why. In the plain page the error box is pinned to the bottom of the frame, which is right for a page somebody designed, where it must not be lost among the buttons. For a program that prints, the error belongs where the next line would have been, as in a terminal; at the foot of an empty frame it reads as a thing apart.

The rest is looks. The colours of zeno-lang.org, links to Learn, the Book and the Docs, and on a phone the boxes are in 16px type, because a phone zooms into a box with smaller letters and does not zoom out again.

21. sort with a function

sort put numbers and text in order and refused everything else, and told the reader to "write the loop that puts them in yours" (6.6). That loop is a sorting algorithm, which is the thing a library is for. This chapter is sort for every list.

21.1 The function gives a key, and is not asked to compare

people.sort(function(person: Person) -> int { return person.age })

The function takes one item and returns what it is ordered by: an int, a float, a sized number or text. Smallest key first.

Why. There are two shapes in the big languages. C, JavaScript and Java ask for a function of two items that says which comes first. Python and Rust's sort_by_key ask for a key. The first can say anything, and can also be wrong in ways nobody sees: one that answers "before" both ways round, or is not the same from one call to the next, gives an order that depends on how the sorting was written, and in C it can read past the end of the list. A key cannot be wrong like that. Whatever the function returns, numbers and text have one order.

It is also what people mean. "By age" is a key. a.age < b.age is the same thought written twice, with a chance to write a both times.

The cost. An order that is not "by something" needs a key built for it, and biggest first is a minus (or two sorts for text). A function of two items is refused with the reason, since it is what someone coming from JavaScript will write first.

The alternative of taking both shapes, by how many parameters the function has, is two things under one name. It was not taken.

21.2 The same name: sort() and sort(by)

Why. It is the same act, on the same list, in place. A second name (sort_by) would be a second thing to learn for it, and sort() is then sort by the item itself. pop() and pop(at) are one name the same way.

21.3 Items with the same key stay as they were

Why. It is what a person expects of "sort the table by this column", and it is what makes two sorts add up: by name, then by age, is by age and by name within an age. Without it the order of equal items is whatever the algorithm did, which is nothing the program said.

How, with heapsort. Heapsort does not keep equal items in order. The library has it because it needs no second list and no deep stack (library.md), and a compiled program must print what zeno run prints (5.5d), so both sides have the same steps. So each item's place is kept beside its key, and two keys that are neither before the other are settled by place. No two items are equal any more, and any correct way of sorting gives one answer.

The cost. Two lists while it sorts, of keys and of places. sort() with no function still needs none.

sort() with no function is left as it is. Two equal ints cannot be told apart, so nobody can see their order. The one case that can be seen, 0.0 and -0.0, is the same in every engine and is written down (library.md).

21.4 The function is called once for each item, first

All the keys are worked out, and then the list is put in order.

Why. n calls, where asking during the sorting would be about n log n and would call the function with the list half sorted. A function that prints, or counts, does it once for each item, in the order the list was in.

If the function changes the list. It may, as one given to map may. If the list then has more or fewer items than there are keys, sort raises a ValueError and the list is as the function left it. There is no right place for an item with no key.

Found on the way. A function given to map, filter or reduce that changed the list it was walking crashed zeno run (the built programs were right). The interpreter held the list while it called the function. It lets go first now, and there is a test.

21.5 Left for later

  • A key that is a quantity: readings.sort(function(r) { return r.temperature }) where that is 20 C. sort() does not take quantities either (17.3 is about sum, and the same care is needed).
  • Two keys at once, where two sorts are written now.
  • sorted, which gives a new list and leaves the first. slice(0) and sort do it in two lines.

20.3 A program without import web gets the page that shows what it prints

In the playground, the page in page.html is used only when the program says import web. Any other program is shown in the page that shows what it prints, whatever page.html holds.

Why. A program that does not import web cannot reach a page, so the page can only get in the way: after opening the list of things to do and writing a program of one's own over it, the old page stayed, and what the new program printed went nowhere. Asking the program is the one rule that is right both ways, and there is nothing to remember: the tab is still there for a program that wants it.

20.4 The playground is an editor, and checks as you write

The playground was a box and a frame. It is now an editor with colours and line numbers, indentation that follows the braces, a problem list that is kept up to date as the program is written, Format, Share, fifteen examples in four groups, halves that can be dragged, and a phone layout with one half at a time. Partha asked for "more features" and a "modern minimal and elegant" look (2026-11-16); this is what was chosen, and why.

Checking as you write, by Zeno itself. zeno.wasm gained two calls, zeno_check and zeno_format, and the playground starts a second copy of the module to make them. Every message is the one zeno check would print, so the playground can never disagree with the compiler. A problem found while typing is worth more than one found by pressing Run, which is why the error line is marked in the gutter at once.

Colours from a list of words, not from the lexer. Colouring runs on every key and has to keep up; a short pattern in the page does, and a word coloured wrongly does no harm. The checker, which must be right, is Zeno's own.

No editor library. A text box with the coloured text drawn under it is a few hundred lines, keeps the page one file that works with nothing fetched, and leaves the browser's own typing, selection, spelling-off, Undo and phone keyboard alone. A full editor library would be several times the size of Zeno's own page for things a playground of one file does not need (completion, folding, many cursors).

Kept in the browser. What was written is kept in the browser's storage and comes back on the next visit, because losing a program to a closed tab is the worst thing a playground can do. A shared link wins over it, because somebody who opens a link wants what is in the link.

One half at a time on a phone. Two halves of a phone screen are each too small to write or read in. A switch between Code and Result, with Run going to Result, is how phone apps with two views work.

The look is the website's. The same type (Hanken Grotesk and JetBrains Mono), the same colours (Windows accent blue, #0078d4, as the one colour since 2026-11-16, red only for problems), the same logo and the same light or dark choice, which the two share. Panels with a thin rule instead of shadows, icons drawn as single lines, and the labels of Format and Share dropped to icons on a phone.

20.5 Drawing with a finger: pointer events, and a canvas counted in its own pixels

The sketch listens for "pointerdown", "pointermove" and "pointerup" instead of the mouse's events, and on a <canvas> event.x and event.y are counted inside its border, in the canvas's own pixels, however large the page shows it.

Why pointer events. A browser sends them for a mouse, a finger and a pen alike, with the same place in them, so one program draws on a desk and on a phone. Nothing in Zeno had to change for that: on already takes any event of the browser (14). The canvas says touch-action: none, so a finger draws instead of scrolling.

Why the canvas's own pixels. On a phone the canvas is shown narrower than it is (max-width: 100%), and a place counted in the page's pixels would draw a line away from the finger. draw_line takes the canvas's pixels, so the event gives the same kind of number, and a program never has to know how large the canvas is shown. Before, the place also counted the border, one pixel off. Only a canvas is counted this way: for anything else there are no pixels of its own.

22. Speed: choosing how memory is freed, --release, and a faster collector

The first benchmarks (bench/README.md, 2026-11-16) put Zeno next to C, Rust and Python on six programs. Arithmetic and arrays came out 1.2 to 1.5 times slower than C. Anything that made many blocks of memory came out 10 to 26 times slower, and the cause was the collector. A build with no collector was faster than C on the same program, but it was made for a chip and had a fixed pool of 32 KB. This chapter is what was done about it. The aim, in Partha's words, is that a Zeno program "has to run faster ... as fast as C and or Rust", while each project chooses how it frees memory.

22.1 A program chooses how memory is freed: memory owned or memory collector

package server
memory owned

A line in zeno.package says how the program frees memory when it is built. memory collector, which is what a program gets when nothing is said, is the garbage collector, and lists and objects can be shared freely. memory owned is one owner for every list and object, freed on the line where its owner ends, with no collector in the program: the rules of --no-collector (5.15), which are Rust's. zeno build --owned and zeno build --collector say the same for one build, over the file.

zeno check and zeno run of a program that says memory owned check it by the owned rules too, so that a program that runs also builds.

Why in zeno.package. How memory is freed is a property of the whole program, not of one build of it: an owned program is written differently (it says take, it does not share a list between two objects), so the choice belongs with the program, where the next person finds it. The command line can still say otherwise for one build.

Why two words and not a third for C. C's way, asking for memory and freeing it by hand, is already in the language: unsafe { } with allocate and free (5.18). It is a block, not a program, because it is the one way in which a mistake is not caught, and a mistake should be looked for in a few lines and not in the whole program.

Why not per line. A value is made on one line and used on many, and how it is freed has to go with the value, not with a line: a list made "with the collector" on line 3 and freed "by its owner" on line 9 is a list nobody can say the rules of. So the choice is made where it can be kept: for the program here, and for one value with owned (5.8), which works in a collector program too.

Alternatives. A flag only (--no-collector as it was), which every build must remember. An attribute in the source file, which a program of several files would say several times, and could say differently. (22.9 later gave a file a line of its own, for the classes it declares.)

22.2 memory owned on a computer: the pool and the text room grow

--no-collector is for a chip: one array of a fixed size for lists and objects, another for text, and the size is known when the program is built (5.9h, 5.9l). On a computer nobody knows in advance how much a program will need, so memory owned keeps the same blocks, sizes and lists of spare blocks, and when the piece of memory it is cutting from runs out it asks the system for another, twice as big as the last. The text room becomes a chain of rooms in the same way. --firmware with memory owned, and --no-collector, keep the fixed arrays.

Why doubling. A program that needs a gigabyte asks the system about twenty times, and what is left at the end of a piece is at most half of it, and usually far less.

Why a chain of rooms and not one room that moves. Text in the room is pointed at by the statement that is building it. A room that moved would leave those pointers behind. A chain never moves anything, and the compiled program does not change at all: it saves the top of the room and puts it back, and the top counts bytes across the chain.

22.3 --release: whole numbers wrap round instead of being checked

zeno build --release (and --llvm --release) compiles +, - and * of whole numbers to the processor's own instructions, which wrap round on overflow, in place of the checked ones that stop the program with an OverflowError (5.5c). Nothing else changes: // and % by zero, an index past the end, a key that is not there and u8(300) still stop the program, and zeno run checks everything as before.

Why. It is what Rust does: a debug build checks, a release build wraps. The check costs a test and a jump on every step of a tight loop, and on the benchmarks it was most of what was left between Zeno and C on integer code. A program that has been tested can drop it.

Why not drop the index checks too. An index past the end in C reads or writes memory that belongs to something else, and the program goes on, wrong, with no message. That is the mistake that safe languages exist to prevent; Rust keeps it in its release builds, and so does Zeno. A wrapped number is wrong too, but it is a number: nothing else in the program is broken by it.

The cost. A release build of a program that overflows prints something else than zeno run does. That is the one place where the engines are allowed to disagree, and it is only with the word written.

22.4 A loop walks a list in place when its body cannot change it

for item in items walks the list as it was when the loop began (5.5c), so a compiled loop has always walked a copy, made for it. The copy is made on every loop: in bench/nbody.zeno, ten million copies of a list of five planets, a quarter of the run.

Now the compilers look at the body first (ast::walks_in_place). If it never writes the name the list is reached from, calls nothing but the built-in functions that only look at their arguments, replaces no item of a list, and makes no function value, then nothing in it can change the list, the copy would be the list itself, and the loop walks the list where it is.

Why decide from the words and not from the types. It is a rule a reader can check by eye, with nothing to get wrong. A body that does more (calls a function of the program, say) still gets its copy, and behaves exactly as before; nothing is ever walked in place that could change.

22.5 The collector keeps its blocks in pages

The collector of 5.7 wrote every block in one table, sorted the table at each collection, and found the block a word points at by halving the table. On a program that keeps two million blocks and makes hundreds of millions (bench/trees.zeno), the sorting and the searching were most of the run.

Blocks now live in pages of 64 KB, each at an address that is a multiple of 64 KB, and a page holds blocks of one size (16 sizes, from 16 bytes to 2 KB; bigger blocks get pages of their own). The page keeps a bit for each block in use and a bit for each block reached. A hash table says which page covers each 64 KB of memory, so "is this word one of my blocks?" is one look and a division, and freeing a block is finding its page from its own address. A block has no header any more. This is the arrangement of most collectors that, like Zeno's, look at memory with no help from the compiler (the Boehm collector is one).

Results. bench/trees.zeno went from 57.8 to 12.0 seconds, and bench/strings.zeno from 24.5 to 3.4 (with 22.6). C takes 5.9 and 0.9.

What stayed. It is still mark and sweep, conservative, and collects when the memory in use passes twice what survived the last collection (the first limit is now 4 MB, up from 1 MB, since blocks are cheaper to keep). Every test that ran the old collector before each allocation (ZENO_COLLECT_ALWAYS) runs the new one the same way.

Left for later. A collector that looks at young blocks more often than old ones (a generational one) would make trees faster still: most of what is left is marking the same long-lived tree at every collection.

22.6 Text of one ASCII character is made once

for ch in text, split("") and the like hand out a text for each character. Each was a block of its own; now a text of one ASCII character is taken from a table of all 128 that is part of the program, and no block is made. With no collector nothing frees them, which is right, since only text in the pool is ever freed.

22.7 for ch in text walks the text and makes no list

Decision. A loop over text with one name walks the text where it is, one character at a time. The loop variable is a character of ASCII from the table of 22.6, or, for any other character, a copy of it. With a collector the copy is the collector's. With no collector the loop holds the last such copy and frees it when it makes the next one, or when the loop ends or is left; whatever keeps the character has its own copy already (a list, a variable outside the loop). With no collector the text must stay where it is while it is walked, so this is done when the loop is over a variable the body never names, or over a literal. Every other loop over text, and every loop with two names (for i, ch in text), walks a list of the characters, as before.

Why. The list was the whole cost: ten million characters made a list of ten million slots, grown by doubling, then freed. The strings benchmark went from 3.5 to 1.1 seconds (C takes 0.9). A character as a str of its own keeps the language as it was: ch is still a str, and ch == "," still means what it meant.

Rejected. A separate char type: faster still, but a second kind of text for a beginner to learn, and every ch in every program would change. Asking the body not to name the text even with a collector: there the text cannot go away while the loop holds it, so it is not needed.

22.8 A field a function never writes is read once

Decision. In a function that calls nothing but the built-ins that only look (print, len, abs, math.sqrt, ...), a number in an object that a parameter holds is read once, at the start, and kept in a local, when no statement of the function writes a field of that name on any object. The LLVM build reads it in the function's entry block. math.sqrt in the LLVM build is written in the program's own IR, as / already was (WRITTEN in src/emit_llvm.rs), so that it can be inlined.

Why. pull(b, c) in the n-body benchmark reads b.mass after it has written b.vz. C must read it again in case b and c are the same object, and the compiler joined that read with the write before it in a way the processor stalls on. Zeno knows more than C here: nothing in the function writes mass, and nothing it calls can. Read once, the benchmark went from 1.0 to 0.44 seconds, where C takes 0.43. It is what a C programmer does by hand, double m = b->mass;, done for them.

What it keeps. The meaning of the program: the field cannot change while the function runs, so the first value is the only value. A field that is written anywhere in the function, through any object, is read each time; so is every field in a function that calls one of the program's own functions or a method.

22.9 One file can say memory owned

memory owned

class Node {
    left: Node or none
    right: Node or none
}

Decision. A file may begin with the line memory owned (or memory collector), alone or beside strict, in either order. In a file that says memory owned, every class is an owned class, as if it were written owned class (5.8): each object has one owner and is freed on the line its owner ends, also in a program that has a collector. When every file of a program says memory owned and its zeno.package says nothing, the whole program is owned: it is built with no collector, as 22.1. A file that says memory collector in a program built with no collector is a mistake, and the message says so.

Why the file, and why its classes. Partha asked for the choice to be finer than the program: "very modular", "more power to the user". The file is the finest place that has a clear meaning, for the reason 22.1 gives against one line: how a value is freed has to go with the value, and a value's kind is declared in one file. A class declared in a memory owned file is owned wherever its objects go, and the rule for where the two ways meet is the one owned values already have in a collector program (5.8): a parameter that looks can be given any object, a value with one owner cannot be kept in a shared place. So nothing new has to be learned at the boundary, and nothing about the boundary is left unsaid. The trees benchmark with its file saying memory owned runs in 4.5 seconds in a collector program, against 11 with the collector and 4.1 with no collector at all.

Why not lists too. A list is made by a line, not declared, and is passed between files more than any other value; making every list of a file owned would mean that half of a program's lists follow one set of rules and half the other, with the line between them invisible. A list that should have one owner says so where it is made: owned list[int]. In a program where every file says memory owned, every list is owned anyway (22.1).

Why not per function. A function's values are made from the classes of its file, and go back to its callers; a function that freed by different rules from the file around it would be a second boundary inside one file, for a gain the file already gives. Per value (owned) and per block (unsafe { }) are the finer choices.

Alternatives. owned written in front of every class (it still can be, one at a time). A list of files in zeno.package: far from the code it is about, and wrong as soon as a file is renamed.

22.10 Two generations, watched with mprotect; and a leak found

The leak. A word that pointed just past the end of a block counted as pointing into it (22.5), so that a loop holding only the end of a list's items would keep them. But in a page of blocks laid end to end, that word is also the start of the next block, so every block reached kept the one before it alive, and that one the one before it. The trees benchmark held 2.3 GB at once where it needed 130 MB. Now the rows of items of lists have pages of their own, and only there does a word just past a block count for it.

Decision. The collector has two generations. A block that lived through a collection is old; most collections are small, look only at the young, and stop at an old block without looking inside it. Once the old have grown to twice what lived through the last full collection, the next collection is full. After each collection the full pages of blocks with pointers are made read-only with mprotect; the first write to one is caught by a signal handler, which opens the page and marks it as written. A small collection looks inside the old blocks of every page that is open. On a chip, and for now on Windows, every collection is full (22.11).

Why. The trees benchmark keeps a tree of two million nodes for the whole run, and every collection marked all of it again: 140 times. With generations it is marked a few times, and the run went from 11 to 7 seconds (C takes 6.2).

Why mprotect, not a write barrier in the compiled code. The usual way is for every store of a pointer to say so, in code the compiler adds. Here pointers are stored by compiled C, by compiled LLVM IR and by a few hundred places in the runtime; one place missed is a block freed while in use, found much later and far away. The processor's own protection of pages misses nothing, costs nothing on a page nobody writes, and needs no change to either compiler. Pages of text are never guarded, so a file read into memory by the system cannot meet a read-only page.

Rejected. Moving young blocks to another place (a copying nursery), which is faster still but needs to know exactly which words are pointers, and this collector does not (5.7). A bigger limit between collections, which trades memory for time without changing the work.

22.11 Left for later

  • Generations on Windows, with VirtualProtect and a handler of its own.
  • Generations on macOS (done after 0.3.0). They were on at first, and in the 0.3.0 release rehearsal three compiled test programs hung on a Mac with Apple silicon while Linux passed, so 0.3.0 shipped with every collection full on macOS. The cause was found after the release (22.14): a try in an LLVM build left the signal mask junk. With a matched pair the whole suite passes on a Mac with generations, and they are on there again.

22.12 Each release keeps its benchmark times

bench/record.py --version X times the benchmarks with every engine that version has and writes bench/results/versions/X.json; the release steps (docs/release.md) say to run it after a tag. 0.1.0 and 0.2.0 were recorded afterwards from their tags. Partha asked for a history (2026-11-16).

Why a script and not a step of the release workflow. GitHub's machines are shared; the same program's time moves by a quarter between runs there, which hides most changes worth seeing. One machine, run by hand, gives numbers that can be compared.

Why each time is shown against C's. C, Rust and Python run in the same record. A version's time divided by C's time on the same machine stays meaningful when the machine is a new one, so records made a year apart still line up.

Why a missing engine is left out. 0.1.0 has no --release; a build that fails because the flag does not exist is not a slow build, and a "failed" in the history would read as a bug.

22.13 The VM gets superinstructions, quick arithmetic and fields by place

Three small changes to the VM, and one shared with the interpreter. bytecode::fuse puts superinstructions over the commonest runs of instructions (local and local or constant into an operator, then a SetLocal or a JumpIfFalse; a local's field). fast_binary answers two ints or two floats in the VM itself. An object keeps its fields in a list in class order (Instance.fields: Vec<Value>), found by ClassDef::slot. A method call given every argument becomes a frame straight away. At the small sizes zeno run --vm went from 15.5 to 7.5 s on loop (Python: 11.3), 1.04 to 0.70 on fib, 18.0 to 9.3 on trees, 23.2 to 15.4 on nbody.

Why superinstructions are put over the code and not in place of it. Fusing in place would shorten the code and move every jump target; the compiler would have to work out the jumps again. Laid over the first instruction of a run, with the rest left under it, nothing moves, a jump into the middle still works, and zeno bytecode still prints what the compiler wrote, which is what docs/vm.md teaches from.

Why the quick arithmetic gives up on every error. An overflow, a division by zero or a mixed pair goes to ops::binary, which is where every message is written. One meaning stays in one place (5.10).

Rejected for 0.3. A register machine and 16-byte values, which would be the next big step; Partha chose to finish 0.3 first and decide after seeing its benchmarks (2026-11-16).

22.14 A try jumps with _setjmp and _longjmp

Outside Windows, the runtime's zeno_raise jumps with _longjmp, and the C build's try calls _setjmp (the macros zeno_setjmp and zeno_longjmp in runtime.c). Windows keeps setjmp and longjmp.

Why. The LLVM build has always called _setjmp, since IR cannot call the macro setjmp. The runtime answered with plain longjmp. On macOS longjmp also sets the signal mask from the jmp_buf, and _setjmp stores none there, so after a caught error the mask was whatever bytes were in that place. The 0.3.0 release rehearsal found it: in an LLVM build that caught an error, the write-fault signal of the collector's generations (22.10) was held back, and the next write to a guarded page stopped the program for good. glibc's _setjmp marks the mask as not saved, so Linux never showed it. The release thread traced it on a Mac (lldb on the hung program, decisions 22.11). A matched pair is correct on every system, and _setjmp saves a system call for each try on macOS. Zeno never changes the signal mask, so there is nothing for a jump to put back.

20.6 The playground shows the C a program becomes

The playground has a C tab: the program as zeno build writes it, by the same compiler, with a choice of the three builds and the runtime hidden unless asked for. Partha asked for it (2026-11-16).

Why. Zeno compiles to C, and the C is the plainest answer to "what does this line cost?". A class is a C struct, match is a chain of ifs on a tag, a firmware build passes errors as values instead of jumping. Seeing that next to the program, changing as the program changes, teaches it faster than any page of docs. The three builds are side by side because the difference between them is the point of Zeno on a chip.

Why the runtime is hidden. It is the same 2,700 or so lines for every program, and the program's own C comes after it. Shown, it pushes the part worth reading off the screen. The line numbers keep counting from the true top of the file, so they match hello.c on a desk, and Copy copies the whole file, which builds as it is.

Why by the compiler itself. zeno.wasm already holds the C emitter (main.rs is compiled for the browser too), so the C in the page is exactly the C zeno build writes, and cannot drift from it.

23. 0.4: a build as fast as C and Rust

Partha asked (2026-11-16) for Zeno to match C and Rust one to one on the benchmarks, with a new option to build that way, so that the builds there are keep every check they have.

23.1 zeno build --fast

--fast leaves out the checks a correct program never fails: a list index out of range, a float divided by zero, the square root of a number below zero, and the overflow of a whole number (as --release does; 23.6 says how the two differ). The C compiler gets -O3 -fno-math-errno, and so does clang for --llvm. The C file starts with #define ZENO_UNCHECKED 1; the runtime's checks sit behind #ifndef ZENO_UNCHECKED. In LLVM IR an item is found with a getelementptr, / of floats is fdiv and math.sqrt is llvm.sqrt.f64, with nothing in front.

Why a new option and not a faster --release. Partha asked for the existing builds to stay as they are. --release drops one check, which can only change what an overflowing program prints. --fast drops checks whose failure means reading memory that is not the list's, which is a different promise; it gets its own word, and the help says "as unforgiving as C".

Why the word fast. It says what it is for, which is how people look for it. --unchecked says what it does and was the other choice; the help text and docs/memory-modes.md say exactly which checks go, in a table, so nothing is hidden.

Why a whole number divided by zero is still caught. In C it is not a wrong answer but a signal that kills the program, or undefined behaviour an optimiser may build on. The check is one compare, usually against a constant the C compiler folds away.

Why -fno-math-errno. C's sqrt must set errno for a number below zero, so the compiler calls the library instead of using the one instruction. No Zeno program can read errno. On nbody the flag alone took a fifth off.

Rejected. -march=native: the program would not run on an older processor of the same family, and a build is often copied to another machine. restrict on parameters that are objects: two parameters may be the same object in a correct Zeno program (pull(b, b)), and restrict would make that program print a wrong answer, which is a different thing from leaving out a check that only a wrong program fails. It is also what keeps nbody behind Rust, whose borrow rules prove the two bodies apart (23.4).

23.2 Numbers become text without snprintf

str(n) and every {n} in an f-string write the digits from the right, two at a time from a table of the hundred pairs, in every build. On the strings benchmark, snprintf (which reads its format string every time) was more than a third of the run.

23.3 A big list grows with realloc

In an owned build on a computer (a pool that grows), the items of a list of 64 KB or more come from the system with calloc, not from the pool, and grow with realloc, as a C program's growing array does. realloc of a big block usually moves no bytes: the system maps the same pages at a new address. Before, each doubling took a new pool block, cleared it, copied the items over and gave the old one back. The block carries its size in front and a head whose step is ZENO_FROM_SYSTEM, so zeno_free knows where it goes back. Kept text is no longer cleared before it is copied in (zeno_alloc_raw), and join finds the end of each piece while copying it.

Why only big lists, and only with a pool that grows. A small list from the pool is cheaper than a call to the system. A build for a chip has no system to ask: its pool is all the memory there is.

23.4 Where Rust is ahead on nbody, and why nothing is done about it

This section first said that Rust wins nbody because its borrow rules prove the two bodies apart. That was a guess, and measuring it showed it wrong (2026-11-16). It is kept here, corrected, because the wrong guess would have led to the wrong work.

Counted in instructions for 200,000 steps (bench/run.py --count, 23.7): C 120.5 million, Zeno --fast 119.5 million, Rust 82.8 million.

Where Rust's lead comes from. In bench/nbody.rs the bodies are a vec! of five, written in main. LLVM sees through to it: it knows there are exactly five, opens both loops out for five, and does the arithmetic of two pairs at a time with the instructions that work on two numbers at once (sqrtpd, divpd). With the list hidden behind std::hint::black_box, so that its length is a number met while running (as the length of a Zeno list is), the same Rust runs 125.8 million instructions and takes 0.51 s: behind C (0.46 s) and behind Zeno --fast (0.38 to 0.44 s). C does not get the lead either, though its bodies are a fixed array of five: gcc and clang both leave it at about 120 million.

What was tried, and measured. restrict on both parameters of pull: 119.1 million instructions against 123.9 million, and no difference in time over eleven runs. Reading bodies[i] once, outside the inner loop: gcc already does. So telling the C compiler that the two bodies are different, which is what this section used to ask for, buys nothing here, and 23.1 was right to reject it for another reason.

Why nothing is done. To get what Rust gets, Zeno would have to know the length of a list while compiling and open the program out for that length. That is a compiler made faster for one benchmark with five bodies, not for programs. A fixed-size array of values is a feature the language may want for its own sake (a table of readings on a chip), and it is to be weighed for that, not for this.

23.5 // and % cost no more than they must

Zeno's // rounds down and % takes the sign of the right side (decision 2.1, as Python's). C's round toward zero. Two changes, in every build, that change no result:

  • By 2, 4, 8 and so on, written as a number. x // 8 is the bits of x moved three places down, keeping the sign (zeno_halved, ashr in IR), and x % 8 is its low three bits (x & 7). Both are exact for a number below zero, and here Zeno's rule is the cheap one: C needs three instructions to divide by two toward zero and Zeno needs one to divide by two downward. Neither can fail, so neither is checked. Only a number written in the expression counts (emit_c::power_of_two); a variable, or -8, goes the usual way.
  • The signs first. zeno_mod mended its result if (r != 0 && signs differ). The C compiler tested r != 0 with a jump (taken one time in seven for % 7) and, past it, worked the mended number out every time, to choose between the two afterwards. It is now if (signs differ && r != 0): for a right side above zero that is one test of the sign of r, a jump never taken while the numbers are above zero, and nothing after it. zeno_floordiv has the same order. On the loop benchmark built with --release: 391.7 million instructions before, 340.3 million after.

Rejected. Mending without a jump (r += b & mask): measured slower, more instructions on the path every number takes. Having Zeno's compiler prove that the left side is not below zero and write C's %: the C compiler already does this, once it is told the truth about overflow (23.6), and it does it for every shape of loop, where a rule here would know one or two.

23.6 In a --fast build, a whole number that overflows has no defined result

What was found. After 23.1, --fast still ran 13% more instructions than C on loop and 17% more on sort. The cause was one thing in both. --fast did its +, - and * as --release does: on unsigned numbers, which wrap round. A C compiler must then allow for the wrap. It cannot know that i, which starts at 0 and only goes up by one, is never below zero, so it kept the mending of (i * i) % 7 for a number below zero; and in the scans of quicksort it carried the index and the address side by side, one more copy on every step. A program written in C does not pay for this, because in C a signed number that overflows has no defined result, and the compiler takes it that none does.

The decision. --fast writes C's signed +, - and * as they stand ((z_i + 1)), and add nsw, sub nsw, mul nsw in LLVM IR, which is what clang writes for a C program. --release is unchanged: there a sum wraps, and the answer is defined.

What it gave (instructions, --fast --owned): loop 300.27 million against C's 300.26 million, the same instructions as C's loop; sort 78.7 million (C 73.3, Rust 78.7; it was 87.6); nbody 119.5 million (C 120.5).

Why this is right for --fast. An overflow is an error in Zeno: zeno run and every checked build stop with OverflowError. So a correct program has none, and --fast is by its own rule (23.1) the build that leaves out what only a wrong program meets. It already reads memory that is not the list's when an index is wrong; an overflow with no defined result is the same kind of promise and a smaller one. It is also the honest meaning of "as fast as C": C is that fast because it makes this assumption, and nothing else gives the C compiler the same knowledge.

What it costs. A program that overflows, built with --fast, may print a wrong number or go round a loop for ever, and not the same way on two compilers. The table in docs/memory-modes.md says so. Someone who wants speed and a defined answer builds with --release.

Rejected. Keeping the wrap and working out ranges in Zeno's own compiler: it could not show i * i to be above zero either, since a product that wraps may be below zero; the knowledge that is missing is "this does not overflow", and no analysis can supply what the build does not promise. A third option between --release and --fast: two words for speed are enough to choose from. Making % round as C's does: that changes what programs print.

23.7 A speed is counted as well as timed

On the machine the benchmarks run on, the time of one program moves by a tenth and more between runs. The loop of C and the loop of --fast after 23.6 are the same instructions, and one session timed them at 0.74 s and 0.89 s. The first table of 23.1 showed nbody a fifth behind C; counted, it was level. The guess in 23.4 was made from such a table.

So bench/run.py --count runs each program once under valgrind and writes how many instructions it ran (bench/results/now-count.json). The count is the same on every run to five figures. It is not a time: a move between registers costs nothing and a square root costs twenty times an addition. The README shows both, and a speed claim in this file is made from the two together. Timed runs now go in rounds, every engine once per round, so a slow minute of the machine falls on every engine and not on one.

23.8 Appending to a list is done where the program appends

After 23.6 the quicksort of the sort benchmark ran fewer instructions than C's (65.9 million against 66.3 million), and the program as a whole still ran 7% more. The rest was in building the list: each xs.append(seed) was a call to zeno_list_append, 24 instructions and a call, where the C program writes if (len == cap) grow; xs[len++] = seed in the loop itself.

The growing is now a function of its own, zeno_list_grow, marked noinline. What is left of zeno_list_append is a compare, a store and a count, and the C compiler puts those in the loop. In every build that goes through C. Sort with --fast --owned: 72.7 million instructions, C 73.3 million. Strings: 174.7 million from 180.4 million (Rust 173.5, C 304.2).

Why noinline on the part that grows. Left to itself the C compiler folded the growing into zeno_list_append, which made it too big to put in a loop, so every append paid for a call to reach the one in thousands that grows.

Not done. The same for a build through LLVM: its runtime is a separate file, so every runtime function is a call there. Writing append in IR, as zeno.mod is, is the way, and it waits until something measures the need.

23.9 The assumption of 23.6 stays, and what it would cost to drop it

Partha asked (2026-11-16) whether the trade of 23.6 can be overcome: C's speed, and a defined result when a whole number overflows. It cannot, and this is the measuring behind that answer. He chose to keep 23.6 as it is.

Why it cannot be had for nothing. C's loop is fast because the compiler uses a fact it cannot check: i * i does not overflow, so it is not below zero. A build that keeps a defined result when the fact is false must either test the fact or not use it. Testing costs instructions and not using it costs instructions; there is no third way to be right when it is false.

What each way costs (the loop benchmark, millions of instructions, --fast --owned with only the overflow rule changed):

What an overflow does gcc clang
C itself 300.3 220.3
assumed not to happen (23.6) 300.3 220.3
wraps round 340.3 330.3
stops the program (-fsanitize=signed-integer-overflow, trapping) 420.3 400.3
-ftrapv 740.3
OverflowError (zeno build) 460.3

Five ways of writing % 7 with no jump, so that a wrapped number needs no mending afterwards, were tried under the wrap: 400 to 440 million, all worse than the jump of 23.5.

What wrapping would cost on all six (instructions against C, gcc): fib 0.98, loop 1.13, sort 1.13, strings 0.57, trees 0.41, nbody 1.03. A model of the processor (llvm-mca) puts loop's 13% at 4% of the time on the machine the benchmarks run on and 12% on an older one. With clang, which is the C compiler on a Mac, loop is 50% more.

Found on the way. Under the wrap, sort comes back from 82.8 to 74.9 million (C 73.3) if the unchecked zeno_list_at tells the C compiler that the index is in range (__builtin_unreachable() when it is not), which --fast promises already. With 23.6 it changes nothing, so it is not in; it is the first thing to do if --fast ever wraps.

Why 23.6 stays.

  • --fast already trusts every index of every list, which is the greater promise: a wrong index reads or writes memory that is not the list's. Taking back the lesser promise would not make the build a safe one.
  • An int is 64 bits. A count that goes up a thousand million times a second overflows after 292 years. An overflow that can happen in testing is caught, with its line, by zeno run and by every build that is not --fast or --release.
  • There is a build for speed with a defined result already: --release. Zig (ReleaseFast beside ReleaseSafe) and Swift (-Ounchecked beside -O) divide their builds the same way, for the same reason.

Rejected. --fast wrapping, with a second option to assume: to match C would then take two words, and the word fast would mean "nearly". Checking only the multiplications whose result goes into a %: one rule for one benchmark.

24. 0.5: the program written in other languages

Partha asked (2026-11-16) for zeno build to write a program "in almost all mainstream languages", HTML among them, by fixed rules and with no AI when it builds. zeno build --target <language> does it; docs/targets.md is the walkthrough.

24.1 Fixed rules, the same file every time

Each target is a printer of a lowered program (24.3) plus a runtime (24.2). Nothing is guessed: the same program gives the same file, byte for byte, on every machine.

Why. Partha asked for it, and it is the only way the written program can be trusted: a rule that is right once is right every time, and tools/targets.py (24.9) can prove it for every program it runs. A translation that varies cannot be tested this way.

24.2 The rules of Zeno go with the program: a runtime per language

Each target has a runtime (src/runtime.js, src/runtime.py) that does what ops.rs and builtins.rs do, step for step and message for message, and every operator goes through it: a + b is Z.add(a, b, line). The runtime is put at the top of the written file.

Why. The promise is "it prints what zeno run prints", including where it stops. A + of the target language would wrap (C++, Go, Java, C#, Rust in release), lose precision (JavaScript) or never overflow (Python), and print floats in its own way. Zeno's + is the one that has to happen.

Why at the top of the file, not a package beside it. One file that runs by itself, with nothing to install, which is what a person who asked for "the program in Python" expects to get. The runtime is about 900 lines; it costs nothing to carry.

Rejected. Typed code (a plain + where the checker knows both sides are ints), for now: it is faster, but each shortcut is a place where the target could disagree with zeno run, and correctness comes first. It is the next step for speed, one proven case at a time.

24.3 One lowering for every target

src/lower.rs turns the checked program into a small one any language can say: unique names, each block's variables listed at its top, functions moved to the top of their block, and what Zeno writes as one expression but most languages need statements for (comprehensions, 0 <= t < 10, and/or whose right side needs statements) made into statements, in Zeno's order of evaluation. Pattern matching becomes a chain of ifs; try becomes the target's own try with a chain of tests that throws on what no except catches.

Why. Every hard question (what order things happen in, what a name refers to, which variable an inner function shares) is answered once, where it can be tested once. The printers only spell.

24.4 Names

Every variable gets a name no other variable in the program has (total, total_2). A name that is a word of any target language (one list for all of them) gets a _ after it. Names starting with z_, Z or __, which the runtime and the printers use, get v_ in front.

Why one list for all targets. A program's names are then the same in every language it is written in, which makes the files easy to compare with each other and with the Zeno.

24.5 Functions before they are written

A function declared in a block is set at the top of the block, so it can be called from a line above it, as in Zeno. A let or import it uses that is written further down is made at the top of the block too, empty until its line runs.

Why. It is Zeno's rule, and the only order in which two functions that call each other can both be set.

24.6 Ints in JavaScript

A Zeno int is a JavaScript number while it is a safe integer (below 2^53), and a BigInt beyond that, held to 64 bits by the runtime. A float is a Float object, so that 2.0 stays a float.

Why. Almost every int a program uses is small, and a number is many times faster than a BigInt. A BigInt for every int was simpler and three to ten times slower. A float has to be told from an int (2.0 prints as 2.0), and JavaScript has one kind of number.

24.7 Variables an inner function shares, in Python

A variable that an inner function uses is kept in a Cell (a new one each time its block starts, so each round of a loop has its own), and the inner function is handed its cells as keyword parameters of its own (def f(x, *, total=total)). A function made inside an expression is written as a def on the lines before the statement.

Why. Python's closures share one variable across every round of a loop, where Zeno gives each round its own; handing the cell over when the function is made gives Zeno's rule. A lambda holds one expression and a Zeno function holds statements, so it must be a def; making a function has no effect that can be seen, so it can be made a moment early.

24.8 What a target cannot do: board, web, unsafe

A program that uses import board, import web or unsafe is refused with a message naming the line.

Why. The pins of a board, the page of --web and manual memory belong to a program compiled for that place. Pretending (a pin that does nothing) would print something zeno run never does.

24.9 tools/targets.py: every target held to zeno run

The tool runs every example, every sample of the book and the corner programs in tools/targets/ with zeno run, then builds each for each target, runs it with that language's own tools and compares what it printed and whether it stopped with an error. A weekly job on GitHub runs it on the newest version of every language.

Why. "Prints what zeno run prints" is a claim about 300 programs and a dozen languages; only a machine can check it, and only on the newest versions can it catch a language changing under us.

24.10 --target html: a page, built on the JavaScript

--target html writes one .html file with the JavaScript program and its runtime in a <script>. What the program prints goes into the element zeno-output (a <pre> is added if the page has none); an error is shown under it. The program's own page (counter.html beside counter.zeno) is used if there is one, and Zeno refuses to write over it. A page cannot write files or sleep, and says so when the program tries; input asks with the browser's question box, as in --web.

Why a page and not "HTML". HTML is not a language a program can be written in: it describes a page. What a person asking for "Zeno in HTML" wants is a file that opens in a browser and runs the program, and that is a page with the program in it, in JavaScript, which is the browser's language.

Why one file. It opens from a disk, an email or any host, with nothing beside it to lose.

Why beside --web and not in place of it. --web runs Zeno itself in the page, so import web works and the program can answer buttons; it is a bigger file and the program runs at zeno run speed. --target html is the program compiled: small and fast, but without import web for now.

Why sleep is refused. A page does one thing at a time; a program that slept would freeze it. --web refuses sleep for the same reason (14.11).

24.11 The order of the targets

JavaScript first, since the page is built on it and Node runs it everywhere; then HTML; then Python, whose model (one kind of int that never overflows, dicts that keep order) is closest to JavaScript's and so tests the shared lowering against a second language early; then Go, Java, C#, C++ and Rust, the statically typed ones; then WebAssembly.

Why Python earlier than the order first suggested. It shares the most with JavaScript, so it was the cheapest way to find what in the lowering was really JavaScript's; it found nothing, which is the evidence that the lowering is not tied to one language.

24.12 Go, Java and C#: every value is the language's "anything"

In Go every Zeno value is an any, in Java and C# an Object: a whole number is a 64-bit int64/long, a float a float64/double, and a list, dict, object or function a class of the runtime. Each operator is a call of the runtime that looks at what it was given, as in JavaScript and Python.

Why not the language's own types. The checker often knows that a variable is an int, and could write int64. But a Zeno program also has values whose type is only known as the program runs (json.parse, a list of mixed things, an Optional), and both kinds have to meet in the same call. One kind of value everywhere keeps the printer small and the three runtimes the same as the other two. Typed code where the type is known is the step for speed after this one (24.2).

24.13 What each of the three languages needed

  • Go refuses a variable that is never read, so every variable is also given to _ once (_ = total). Go has no try: Zeno's try is a call of Ztry with two functions, the body and the handler, which answer with a number for what happens next (go on, break, continue or return), and the code after the call does it.
  • Java lets a lambda use only a variable that never changes, so a variable an inner function uses is kept in a Z.Cell, as in Python (24.7). Java refuses a statement it can prove is never reached, so return x is written if (true) return x;. And Java reads \u000a in a string as a new line in the source, so a character below a space is written as an octal escape.
  • C# needs neither: a lambda can change a variable outside it, and a statement never reached is only a warning, which the file turns off. It refuses a name in an inner block that one outside has, which the lowering's unique names (24.4) already rule out.

Why the lowering stays the same. All three are things a printer can do by itself. The one change the lowering needed is 24.14.

24.14 The order arguments are worked out in

Zeno works out a call's arguments from left to right. JavaScript, Python, Java and C# do too; Go and C++ do not promise it. For those two the lowering copies every argument before the last one that calls something into a variable of its own, first, in Zeno's order: so in show(count, next()) the count that is shown is the one before next() ran, as in Zeno, even if next changes it.

Why copy plain variables too. Go reads a variable whenever it likes, so a plain count beside a call is not safe either; the first version left plain variables alone, and examples/labels.zeno printed the wrong count in Go.

24.15 Nesting: 1000 calls, in every target

zeno run stops a program whose calls nest more than 1000 deep, with a message that asks if the function calls itself forever. The Go, Java and C# runtimes count calls the same way and stop with the same message, and they run the program on a thread with a big stack so that 1000 calls always fit. (JavaScript and Python count too since 24.23.)

Why. A program that recurses forever should end the same way in every language, with Zeno's message, not with a Go "goroutine stack exceeds" or a Java StackOverflowError.

24.16 The last digit of sin, exp and pow

zeno run takes math.sin, math.exp, math.pow and the other functions of floats from the C library of the computer. Python, C#, C++ and Rust use the same library, so they print the same digits. Others have their own: JavaScript's (in Node) and Java's StrictMath give a different last digit for about one input in ten, and Go's own pow for about one in three. To keep the difference as small as can be:

  • Go uses its own sqrt, exp and log (which agree with the C library on the programs here) and, for sin, cos and tan, a copy of the functions JavaScript uses, written in Go in runtime.go.
  • Java uses Math, not StrictMath: Math is allowed to use the computer's own, which agree far more often.

Where the last digit can still differ, it is listed as a known limit (in docs/targets.md): the answer is right to the 16th digit, but the 17th, which a float's printing shows, may be one off.

Why not exact everywhere now. Making every runtime give the correctly rounded answer means writing those functions in each language with extra precision, a project of its own. None of the 319 programs shows a difference; the fix waits until a program needs it.

24.17 C++: a small value, shared pointers, cells

In C++ a Zeno value is a Z::V: a tag, a union for an int, a float or a bool, and a shared_ptr for anything else (a str, a list, an object). A value is freed when the last pointer to it goes. A variable an inner function uses lives in a Z::Cell (a shared box), because a lambda written [=] copies what it uses. Arguments are put in variables first (24.14). The file builds with any C++17 compiler and needs nothing beside it.

Why shared pointers and not a collector. They free almost everything at the moment it is no longer used, with nothing to add to the file. What they miss is a cycle, such as a list that holds itself; a program that makes many of those would grow. None of the 319 does, and a collector can come later without changing the printer.

Why [=] and cells rather than [&]. A lambda that kept references to the variables around it would point at nothing once the function that made it returned, and a Zeno function can outlive the one that made it.

24.18 Rust: failures as results, try as a closure

Rust has no exceptions. Every step of runtime.rs that can fail gives a z::R, the value or the failure (an error on its way to a try, or a mistake that stops the program), and the program passes a failure on with ?. A try body is a closure called at once; the code after it looks at how it ended. A break, continue or return in the body cannot reach outside the closure, so the body ends with which one it was and the code after it does it, as in Go (24.13). A variable an inner function uses lives in a z::Cell, and each function gets its own copy of the cells it uses where it is made: the lowering lists them for each function (captures).

Why results and not panics. A panic can be caught, but it is meant for bugs: it prints to the screen unless told not to, and it is slow to catch. A result costs nothing until something fails, and ? keeps the written program as short as the others.

Why the same printing. Zeno is written in Rust, so the Rust runtime shows floats with {:?}, fixed decimals with {:.2}, and text with the same quoting, exactly as zeno run does. It is the one target that cannot differ in the last digit.

24.19 WebAssembly: through Rust, with WASI

--target wasm writes the program in Rust, has rustc build it for wasm32-wasip1, and keeps the .wasm file. WASI is the system interface that WebAssembly hosts outside a browser give a program: the keyboard, the screen, the command line and files. The file runs in wasmtime, wasmer, or Node (tools/run-wasi.js). WebAssembly has no threads, so there the program runs on its one thread, built with a 32 MB stack, which holds 1000 nested calls.

Why through Rust. Rust builds WebAssembly well and has done so for years, and the Rust target already prints what zeno run prints; a WebAssembly printer of its own would be a second compiler to keep in step. This is also the one target where zeno build runs a compiler itself, because a .wasm file is not text a person would keep: what they want is the file that runs.

Why WASI and not the browser. The page target (24.10) is already the browser's. zeno build --web puts Zeno itself in a page as WebAssembly. What was missing was a program that runs anywhere a WebAssembly host does, the same file on every computer, and that is WASI.

24.20 Text in quotes: Zeno's own rule

A text shown inside a list, a dict or an object, or named in the message of an error, is in quotes. Inside them ", \, the new line, the carriage return and the tab are written as a program writes them (\", \\, \n, \r, \t). Any other control character (below a space, and U+007F to U+009F) is written as its code, \u{1b}, because it would not show on a screen. Every other character is written as it is.
