Until now zeno run used Rust's {:?} for this, which escapes whatever Rust's tables of Unicode call "not printable" or "extending". That made ["தமிழ்"] print as ["தமிழ\u{bcd}"]: the last sign of the word is a combining one. The C that zeno build writes escaped five characters and nothing else, and the runtimes of the targets already followed the rule above. Three ways of running one program gave three answers. Now zeno run, the C runtime and every target quote a text the same way.

Why not Rust's rule everywhere. It needs Unicode's tables, which change with each version of Unicode, so the answer would depend on the version of Rust that built Zeno, and each runtime would need a copy of the tables. It also breaks words in Tamil, Hindi, Thai and every script that uses combining signs. strip and upper already keep to ASCII so that no table is needed (3.14); this is the same choice.

Why escape control characters at all. A list is shown to be read. An escape character or a zero-width control inside a text would change what the terminal does, or show nothing, and the reader could not tell "a" from "a\u{1b}".

Rejected. Escaping everything that is not ASCII: exact, but a program that prints a list of names in Tamil would print codes.

24.21 A float between two shortest texts takes the even one

A float is printed with the fewest digits that name it and no other float. Now and then two texts of that length are equally near: the float 1e15 + 0.25 lies exactly between 1000000000000000.2 and 1000000000000000.3. Zeno prints the one whose last digit is even.

Why. C, JavaScript, Python, Java and C# do, and so did the C that zeno build writes. zeno run printed the upper one, because Rust does, so zeno run and zeno build disagreed, and so did five of the targets. The even digit is also what the rest of Zeno does with a half: {x:.1f} rounds the exact value to even. It can only happen to a float that needs 16 or 17 digits. value::float_text puts Rust's answer right in that one case; the Rust target carries the same function.

Rejected. Teaching five runtimes to round up as Rust does: more code in more places, for the rarer habit.

24.22 A variable is read when its turn comes

Zeno works a line out from left to right, and reads a variable at the moment its turn comes. The lowering (24.3, 24.14) now keeps to that in four places where it did not:

  • f(b=two(), a=one()) runs two() first, as written. On JavaScript, Python, Java, C# and Rust it ran one() first, because the arguments had already been put in the order of the parameters.
  • x += f() reads x before f() runs, so a change f makes to x is lost, as in zeno run. Go and C++ read x after.
  • [x, bump()], x < bump() < y, f"{x} {bump()}", [x: bump()]: wherever a later part needs statements of its own (24.14), an earlier plain variable is copied first. Before, only earlier calls were copied, and a variable was read after the later part had changed it, on every target.
  • list[i] = value and object.field = value work out the list, the index and the value in that order.

Why. It is the rule of zeno run, and "it prints what zeno run prints" has no exceptions. None of the 319 programs changed a variable in the middle of a line that also read it, so none showed it.

Rejected. Copying every variable always: right, but each line of the written program would grow, and most lines change nothing midway. A variable is copied only when something after it on the line can run code.

24.23 Nesting is counted in JavaScript and Python too

24.15 had JavaScript and Python stop "where their own limit is". That is not the same program: zeno run stops at the 1000th nested call, with the name of the function and the line, and JavaScript ran more than 10,000 deep before it stopped with another message. Now both count as the other targets do: a call of a function of the program is Z.direct(f, line, ...), which counts it in, calls it, and counts it out again whether it returned or raised.

Why. A program that prints how deep it got, or that recurses 5000 deep on purpose, did something else in two of nine targets.

Cost. A call in Python is slower by about a third (fib: 2.3 s to 3.5 s), in JavaScript a little (0.12 s to 0.16 s). Correct first (24.2).

24.24 Corners of the library, pinned down

The review of the targets found places where zeno run did whatever the Rust it is built with does, and a target did what its own language does. Each now has a rule, which zeno run and all targets follow:

  • lines(). A line ends at \n. A \r just before that \n goes with it. A \r anywhere else, and one at the very end with no \n after it, stays in its line. It is written out in builtins.rs, not left to Rust's str::lines.
  • input(). The same for the line end. Bytes that are not UTF-8 become U+FFFD the way Rust, Python and browsers do it: one for each run of bytes that began a character and did not finish it, one for each byte that could not begin one.
  • range. A step past the last int ends the range. zeno run added the step without looking, which is a stop in a debug build and a wrong, endless list in a release build.
  • math.floor, math.ceil. Always an int: too big a float gives the biggest int, not-a-number gives 0, as math.round and zeno run do. Five targets gave the float back.
  • // and % of floats. -0.0 is kept where the arithmetic gives it (Python lost it).
  • JSON. The four hex digits of \u are digits (zeno run took \u+041); the second half of a pair is DC00 to DFFF; the blanks before a text that is not JSON, which the message leaves out, are JSON's four.
  • files. A path with nothing in it is "no file". A path through a file (notes.txt/x) "cannot be read".

Why list them. Each was found by a program written to look for it (tools/targets/edge-*.zeno), not by a user. They are small, but a rule that is whatever one compiler happens to do is not a rule (principle: unambiguous).

24.25 File names, and names in capitals

  • A program may have any file name. The Rust file names its own crate (#![crate_name]), so my prog.rs and 2048.rs build; the command that zeno build prints has -o, and quotes a name a shell would split.
  • go run refuses a file that ends in _test.go and does not see one that begins with _ or .. For those, the Go file gets program in its name: speed_test.zeno is written as speed_test_program.go.
  • Python looks for a module in the program's folder before its own library. A program called struct.py or re.py was taken for Python's module of that name. The runtime takes the folder off the list before it imports anything.
  • A name in capitals may be a macro of a C header (ERANGE, SIGINT, OVERFLOW). The C++ file says #undef for each such name of the program, after the runtime and before the program.
  • The words the list of 24.4 had missed (args, delegate, the sized ints of C++ and others) are in it.

Why #undef and not a longer list. The macros differ from system to system and there are hundreds; no list is complete. After the runtime nothing needs them.

24.26 The check holds the error to zeno run too

tools/targets.py (24.9) compared what was printed and whether the program stopped with an error. Now, when it stops, the message, the file and the line must be the same too. It also takes a corner program in several files (a folder with main.zeno), the words a program is started with (.args), and typed input that is not text (.stdin is bytes).

Why. "The same error" was promised (24.2) and not checked. The nesting difference of 24.23 would have been caught the first day.

24.27 TypeScript: the JavaScript, with the type any

zeno build --target ts writes the program as TypeScript. It is the program --target js writes, with a type on every variable, parameter and caught error, after src/runtime.ts, which is src/runtime.js with types. The type is any. The file passes tsc --strict, and Node (22.18 or newer) runs it as it is; tools/targets.py does both for every program.

Why any. What a Zeno value is, is found out while the program runs, by the runtime, in every target (24.2): that is how an int that overflows, a wrong unit or a missing field gives Zeno's message at Zeno's line. Go has any for this, Java Object, Rust an enum. TypeScript's types say what JavaScript would do with a value, and Zeno does not do what JavaScript does (1 + "a" is an error, an int is 64 bits). A type like number on a Zeno int would be a promise the program does not keep.

Why a typed copy of the runtime, and not // @ts-nocheck. A TypeScript project that takes the file should not have to switch its checker off for it. The copy costs little: a test (the_typed_runtime_is_the_runtime_with_types) takes the types out of runtime.ts and holds what is left to runtime.js, line by line, so the two cannot drift and what is tried for JavaScript holds for TypeScript.

Why the file is a module (it ends in export {};). In a TypeScript project every file that is not a module shares one set of names, so two built programs would both declare Z and neither would pass. A module's names are its own.

What changed in the JavaScript for it. The runtime took Node's file module with require("fs"), which a module does not have. It now asks Node for it by name (process.getBuiltinModule("fs")), which works in a script and in a module, so the .js file also runs where "type": "module" is set. It needs Node 22.3 or newer (20.16 in the 20 line); the oldest Node still kept up is 22.

Alternatives rejected. Real types worked out from the Zeno types (a second printer and a second runtime, and still wrong at the edges: a Zeno int is not a number). Running tsc in zeno build and keeping only the .js (then it is the js target).

24.28 PHP: its own ints and floats, lists in objects, use (&$x)

zeno build --target php writes a .php file that php runs (8.0 or newer), with src/runtime.php at its top. It is a port of the JavaScript runtime, and holds all 337 programs to zeno run.

  • An int is a PHP int, a float a PHP float. PHP keeps the two apart (3 and 3.0 are different values), and its int is 64 bits, so neither needs a box. PHP turns +, - and * that do not fit into a float instead of wrapping; the runtime sees that the answer is no longer an int and raises OverflowError.
  • A list and a dict are objects (ZList, ZDict) around a PHP array. A PHP array is copied when it is handed on; a Zeno list is shared, and xs.append(1) inside a function must be seen outside. PHP makes the key "1" the int 1; Zeno keeps them apart, so a str key is stored with an s before it.
  • An inner function names what it uses: function ($x) use (&$total). The & shares the variable. A PHP variable lasts for its whole function, so a shared variable declared in the body of a loop is unset where its block begins: the next round's is then a new variable, and the functions made in the round before keep theirs, as in Zeno.
  • No extension is needed. mbstring, ctype, bcmath and gmp are not in every PHP. Characters are counted and split with preg (always there) and by hand, and {x:.2f} works out the exact decimal text of the float with plain ints, a half going to the even digit as in zeno run.
  • A long chain is let go in a loop. PHP frees an object's fields from inside freeing the object, so a chain of 200,000 objects crashed it. ZInstance and ZCase hand their fields to a list that one loop empties, as the Rust runtime does.
  • PHP's settings are fixed at the start: no memory limit, floats written with all their digits, and a PHP warning stops the program as a fault of the runtime instead of being printed among the program's lines.

Alternatives rejected. Cells for shared variables, as the Python target has ($total->v everywhere, where PHP has a word for exactly this). mb_strlen and number_format (the first is not always installed, the second rounds a half up after rounding the float first, which is not what zeno run prints).

24.29 Zeno is built as one piece, so its speed does not move when code is added

Cargo.toml has codegen-units = 1 for a release build: the Rust compiler works on all of Zeno at once.

What happened. 0.5.0 added the targets, about 20,000 lines that nothing runs unless --target is given. No line of the VM changed, and bench/run.py --count showed it: within two in a hundred of 0.4.0's instructions, fewer on some programs and more on others. But timed, in four rounds on a quiet machine, zeno run --vm in the 0.5.0 build was slower than in 0.4.0's on every benchmark: fib 7 %, loop 5 %, sort 9 %, strings 1 %, trees 4 %, nbody 4 %. The built programs were not touched by it (their C is the same), nor was plain zeno run.

Why. By default the compiler cuts a program into sixteen pieces and works on each alone. Which functions land in one piece, and so which can be folded into which and what ends up beside what in the file, depends on everything else in the program. Adding code that never runs moved the VM's loop and its helpers about, and the processor's guesses and its cache did a little worse on the same instructions.

With one piece there is no cutting, so nothing unrelated decides it. The same source then runs fewer instructions than 0.4.0 (fib 2 % fewer, sort 1 %, strings 1 %) and, timed the same way, is within two in a hundred of 0.4.0 on five benchmarks (loop −2 %, sort −1 %, strings −2 %, trees −1 %, nbody −1 %) and 3 % above on fib, which is two hundredths of a second. Building Zeno takes 47 seconds where it took 36. The program is half a megabyte smaller.

The rule this is for. Partha, 2026-11-17: writing a program in other languages is a flag, and Zeno itself must stay as fast as it was. A count of instructions is not enough to show that; the check before a release that adds code is now both, the count and timed rounds against the release before.

Alternatives rejected. Moving the targets into a crate of their own (the VM's crate still changes with every other feature, so the same thing would come back). Whole-program lto on top of it (Zeno has no crates beside itself, so there is little for it to join; it took a minute to build and was not timed). Leaving it, as within the noise of this machine: it was the same direction on all six programs, four rounds running.

This page is docs/decisions.md in the language repository.

Every journey is halfway done. Forever.

The runner has closed 0 of 1 halves

Zeno of Elea said Achilles can never catch the tortoise: first he must cover half the distance, then half of what is left, without end. It suits a compiler that will always be almost finished. The bar at the top of each page works the same way.
